\[ \begin{bmatrix}0\\2\\0\\1\end{bmatrix} \xrightarrow{R_2} \begin{bmatrix}1\\0\\2\\0\end{bmatrix} \xrightarrow{R_3} \begin{bmatrix}0\\2\\0\\1\end{bmatrix}. \]

The station-2 write began as \(p_2y_2=[0,1,2,3]^\top\):

\[ \begin{bmatrix}0\\1\\2\\3\end{bmatrix} \xrightarrow{R_3} \begin{bmatrix}3\\2\\1\\0\end{bmatrix}. \]

The station-3 write lands where it is written. There is no later shuffle:

\[ p_3y_3 = \begin{bmatrix}\tfrac32\\0\\3\\\tfrac32\end{bmatrix}. \]

Add the five origins:

\[ \begin{aligned} x_4 &= \underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}2\\2\\2\\2\end{bmatrix}}_{x_0} + \underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}1\\2\\0\\0\end{bmatrix}}_{y_0} + \underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}0\\2\\0\\1\end{bmatrix}}_{y_1} + \underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}3\\2\\1\\0\end{bmatrix}}_{y_2} + \underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}\tfrac32\\0\\3\\\tfrac32\end{bmatrix}}_{y_3}\\ &= \begin{bmatrix}\tfrac{15}{2}\\8\\6\\\tfrac92\end{bmatrix}. \end{aligned} \]

That is also a destination-by-origin ledger:

final notebook from \(x_0\) from \(y_0\) from \(y_1\) from \(y_2\) from \(y_3\) total
1 2 1 0 3 \(3/2\) \(15/2\)
2 2 2 2 2 0 8
3 2 0 0 1 3 6
4 2 0 1 0 \(3/2\) \(9/2\)

Each row is a sum of leftovers from several depths. That is the fact that kills the sliding-window picture. Notebook 2 is not “whatever station 2 said.” It holds \(2\) from the start, \(2\) from station 0, \(2\) from station 1, and \(2\) from station 2.

The compact way to write the chase we just did is to substitute (5.1) into itself:

\[ \begin{aligned} x_1&=R_0x_0+p_0y_0,\\ x_2&=R_1R_0x_0+R_1p_0y_0+p_1y_1,\\ x_3&=R_2R_1R_0x_0+R_2R_1p_0y_0+R_2p_1y_1+p_2y_2. \end{aligned} \]

One more substitution is the four-station expansion:

\[ \boxed{ x_4= R_3R_2R_1R_0x_0 +R_3R_2R_1p_0y_0 +R_3R_2p_1y_1 +R_3p_2y_2 +p_3y_3.} \tag{5.2} \]

Every term is in \(\mathbb{R}^{4\times 1}\). A contribution written at station \(i\) is multiplied by every later leftover-copy shuffle, right to left, oldest shuffle first. The newest write \(p_3y_3\) has no later shuffle. The initial column crosses all four.

Follow one number through that product. Station 0 wrote coefficient \(1\) into notebook 1. That piece then travels

\[ 1\xrightarrow{R_1}2\xrightarrow{R_2}3\xrightarrow{R_3}2. \]

So \(y_0=2\) contributes \(2\) to final notebook 2, which is the ledger entry \(2\) in that cell. The half-strength station-0 write started in notebook 3 and ends in final notebook 1. A learned route is not the slogan “layer 0 connects to layer 4.” It names an entry strength and every intervening seat change.

That chain of seats is a path. The name is worth keeping because the write chooses where an answer first enters, and the later \(R\) matrices choose where that leftover travels. In this chapter each path is a single wire: a permutation keeps exactly one continuation alive from any starting notebook.

With denser shuffles a write can split at every later station, so “path” means a weighted family of routes rather than one wire. Let \(j_i\) be a notebook that \(p_i\) first writes into, and let \(j_{i+1},\ldots,j_3\) be later seats. One individual route into final notebook \(a\) has coefficient

\[ (R_3)_{a j_3}(R_2)_{j_3j_2}\cdots(R_{i+1})_{j_{i+1}j_i}(p_i)_{j_i}. \]

The total coefficient is the sum of this product over all intermediate seats. That sum is exactly the \(a\)-th entry of \(R_3\cdots R_{i+1}p_i\). In our permutation example each product is either zero or the original write coefficient. With dense routers several products survive and add.

Equation (5.2) groups those already-computed answers \(y_0,\ldots,y_3\). It does not claim the answers are independent. Each later peek is \(y_i=F_i(q_ix_i)\), so a later calculation can depend on earlier writes. If some \(F_i\) is nonlinear, you generally cannot expand that dependence into a linear sum of the earlier \(y\)’s. The leftover-copy ledger remains exact anyway: it says where each already-computed answer was injected and how it was shuffled.

A gradient walks the same graph backwards. These forward routes are therefore also routes by which a later loss can reach earlier notebooks and earlier answers. That is a fact about the wiring. It is not a promise that every route has a healthy size.

The later papers write this same read–compute–write recurrence and then expand it across depth [R002]. Reading the expansion is useful. Executing one is what makes the indices stop being decorative.

When the swaps become splits

Every \(R_l\) in this chapter is a permutation, so every entry is nonnegative and every row and every column adds to one. Any matrix with those two properties is doubly stochastic. The name is worth keeping because “rows and columns both add to one, nothing negative” is a constraint we will live inside for several chapters.

Hyper-Connections allow a learned leftover-copy shuffle \(R_l\) with no such promise [R001]. mHC keeps the same recurrence and asks \(R_l\) to stay inside the set of nonnegative doubly stochastic matrices — people call that set the Birkhoff polytope [R002]. go-mHC keeps the same constraint and proposes a different exact way to build those matrices [R005]. These are recent architecture proposals, not settled design law.

Our four permutations already satisfy the constraint. That was a teaching choice: permutation routes make the fate of each leftover visible. It is not a claim that trained routers usually become exact permutations, nor that every parameterization reaches every boundary point in the same way.

A soft doubly stochastic shuffle replaces each crisp arrow above with conservative splitting and recombination across notebooks. The algebraic expansion (5.2) does not change. Only the allowed coefficients do.

A trained model might use different notebooks to keep or combine leftovers from different depths. The Hyper-Connections paper reports pictures of learned connection patterns [R001]. Calling our four seats “specialized streams” is a story we told about this trace. It is not a measurement unless a particular trained model is probed.

What we would lose

If every write is fair, identical notebooks can stay identical under a symmetric shuffle. Four copies then store no more variety than one copy. Station 1’s proud peek at notebook 1 would still see the same number as the other three.

If we freeze the peek, every station receives a fixed blend. Once the notebooks diverge, the block cannot change which leftover it consumes.

If we freeze the write, every answer enters the same seats in the same amounts. The model loses control over where new work lands.

If we freeze the leftover-copy shuffle, old contents cannot change seats between stations. Writes may still differ, but the wiring of old leftovers becomes much thinner.

If we never unroll (5.1), we miss the central fact: an early write is acted on by every later shuffle. A list of four local updates does not by itself explain why \(y_0\) reaches final notebooks 1 and 2 while \(y_2\) reaches final notebooks 1, 2, and 3 in this trace.

Ways to get this wrong

Multiplying in the wrong order is the classic one. A station-0 write reaches station 4 as \(R_3R_2R_1p_0y_0\), because \(R_1\) acts first. Matrix products read right to left.

  • Calling the notebooks “the last four layers.” The ledger is four sums, not a sliding window.
  • Peeking at \(R_lx_l\). The branch input is \(q_lx_l\). The shuffle and the peek are parallel ingredients of the final addition.
  • Running \(F_l\) four times. Here \(q_lx_l\) produces one input, hence one \(y_l\). The four-vector \(p_l\) shares it.
  • Forgetting the transpose on the write. The write row is \(1\times 4\); standing it up makes a \(4\times 1\) column that can land in four notebooks.
  • Treating every peek as a probability distribution without looking. Our \(q_l\) rows happen to be nonnegative and add to one. The core equation alone does not force that.
  • Reading (5.2) as a claim that the four answers are independent. Later \(y_l\) values depend on earlier columns. The expansion groups injection origins. It does not cut the computational graph.
  • Dropping the singleton axes in your head. The toy column is shorthand for \(4\times 1\times 1\), not evidence that stream, token, and feature are interchangeable.

Try it

1. A different peek

At the entrance to station 2 the notebooks hold \([2,6,3,3]^\top\). If we peeked with \(q_2=[0,\tfrac14,\tfrac34,0]\) instead, what would \(z_2\) be? Keep \(F_2(z)=2z\) and find \(y_2\).

Answer

\(z_2=\tfrac14(6)+\tfrac34(3)=\tfrac{15}{4}\). Therefore \(y_2=2z_2=\tfrac{15}{2}\).

2. A fair write that fails

Keep station 0 the same except write equally: \(p_0=[\tfrac12,\tfrac12,\tfrac12,\tfrac12]^\top\). What is \(x_1\)? When station 1 peeks at only notebook 1 with \(q_1=[1,0,0,0]\), is it seeing something the others do not have?

Answer

Since \(y_0=2\), the write is \([1,1,1,1]^\top\). With \(R_0=I\), \(x_1=[3,3,3,3]^\top\). The peek selects notebook 1, but its content is not unique; every notebook contains \(3\).

3. Chase one 1

Suppose a hypothetical station-1 write is \(e_2=[0,1,0,0]^\top\). Using this chapter’s \(R_2\) and \(R_3\), calculate \(R_3R_2e_2\). Which final notebook receives it?

Answer

\(R_2e_2=e_3=[0,0,1,0]^\top\), then \(R_3e_3=e_2=[0,1,0,0]^\top\). It ends in final notebook 2. The intermediate and final seat numbers happen to differ and then return; calculate, do not guess from the endpoints.

4. A missing shuffle

A classmate writes

\[ x_4=R_3R_2R_1R_0x_0+R_3R_2p_0y_0+R_3R_2p_1y_1+R_3p_2y_2+p_3y_3. \]

What did they drop? Repair the term.

Answer

The station-0 write must cross \(R_1\), \(R_2\), and \(R_3\). Its term is \(R_3R_2R_1p_0y_0\). All other terms shown are correct.

5. Any number of stations

For \(L\) stations numbered \(0\) through \(L-1\), write \(x_L\) as the initial notebooks plus every write. What is the empty product when a write has no later shuffle?

Answer

With later station indices placed on the left,

\[ x_L=R_{L-1}\cdots R_0x_0+ \sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\left(R_{L-1}\cdots R_{i+1}\right)p_iy_i. \]

For \(i=L-1\), the product contains no matrices and is defined as the identity \(I_4\), leaving \(p_{L-1}y_{L-1}\).

Check yourself

Why is \(x_1\) no longer \([2,2,2,2]^\top\)? Because \(p_0\) wrote \(2\) into notebook 1 and \(1\) into notebook 3. The first unfair write is what makes a later peek able to prefer anything.

Does station 2 peek at the already-cycled notebooks? No. It peeks at \(q_2x_2\). \(R_2x_2\) is the other summand in (5.1).

How many times does each station run \(F_l\)? Once. Four write numbers share that one answer.

Why does \(y_0\) contribute \([1,2,0,0]^\top\) to \(x_4\)? Because it is written as \([2,0,1,0]^\top\), then shuffled by \(R_1\), \(R_2\), and \(R_3\). That answer names both the injection and the path.

Are the four notebooks the last four station outputs? No. The ledger is four sums. Notebook 2’s \(8\) is \(2+2+2+2+0\) from five different origins.

We picked every peek, write, and shuffle by hand. Next we stop doing that. Whatever mechanism produces \(q_l\), \(p_l\), and \(R_l\), the resulting numbers still have to survive this same walk.

Sources

  • [R001] D. Zhu et al., “Hyper-Connections,” arXiv:2409.19606, 2024; revised 2025. https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.19606
  • [R002] Z. Xie et al., “mHC: Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections,” arXiv:2512.24880, 2025; revised 2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.24880
  • [R006] K. He, X. Zhang, S. Ren, and J. Sun, “Deep Residual Learning for Image Recognition,” CVPR, 2016, pp. 770–778. https://doi.org/10.1109/CVPR.2016.90
  • [R005] T. Dandachi and S. Diggs-Galligan, “go-mHC,” arXiv:2604.02309v1, 2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02309

Chapter 6 — A small opinion about the notebooks

For four chapters we mixed the notebooks ourselves. We wrote a read row, a write column, and a leftover shuffle, then watched those numbers travel through a stack. That was the right way to learn the arithmetic. It is a terrible way to run a model.

Nobody is going to sit inside layer 47 with a pencil. The network needs a cheap opinion about the mix — an opinion that is allowed to change when the page changes.

Look at the notebooks at this position. Squeeze them into a short list. Multiply by a learned matrix. You now have a handful of scores. Those scores are not yet a legal read, a legal write, or a legal leftover mix. A second machine, usually with no extra learned weights, turns the scores into operators the rest of the layer is allowed to use.

That whole front end is the router. The name is worth keeping because it names a job, not a celebrity block. It is a tiny linear layer plus a construction that refuses illegal matrices. It is not a second attention head.

The finished pipeline is four arrows:

\[ \boxed{x_l\longrightarrow \hat x_l\longrightarrow u_l\longrightarrow H_l}. \]

Activation. Normalize and shrink. Learned projection. Then a map that is allowed to produce an operator. Keeping the last two arrows separate is the whole lesson of this chapter.

The pencil and the rulebook

Until now the routing numbers arrived by hand. A real model has to obtain them somehow.

If the numbers are just stored parameters, every sentence at that layer follows the same mix. If they depend on the current activation, the mix can change with the input. That second choice is the “dynamic” in dynamic Hyper-Connections.

A router is easy to describe as one blob. It is three choices:

  1. what summary of the page it is allowed to see;
  2. how learned numbers turn that summary into scores;
  3. how those scores become a permitted operator.

The papers in this family use a small linear layer for step 2, not an obligatory deep network [R001]; [R002]; [R003]; [R004]; [R005]. After that layer you may see tanh, sigmoid, softmax, a few Sinkhorn steps, a Kronecker product, or a Cayley construction. That later work belongs to the rulebook. It does not secretly turn the linear layer into a deep predictor.

Call the scores coordinates. The name is worth keeping because it stops you from treating the linear output as a finished matrix. Coordinates describe a construction. They are not automatically probabilities, and they are not automatically the entries of \(H\).

For a layer index \(l\), write

  • \(B\) for how many sentences ride together;
  • \(T\) for how many tokens sit in one sentence;
  • \(d\) for how many notebooks we carry;
  • \(C\) for how wide each notebook is — the feature count we used to call \(n\);
  • \(x_l\in\mathbb R^{B\times T\times d\times C}\) for the layer input;
  • \(G\) for how many separate routing decisions we will make;
  • \(r\) for how wide the squeezed summary is.

A general router first builds

\[ \hat x_l=R\!\left(N(x_l)\right)\in\mathbb R^{G\times r}. \]

\(N\) is a normalization. \(R\) is a reshape or a reduction. “Compact” means compact relative to the full state the expensive branch will see, not “a single scalar.” For one decision per token, \(G=B\times T\), and a common squeeze flattens the notebook axis with the feature axis, so \(r=dC\). For one decision per sentence, \(R\) would have to reduce the \(T\) axis, for example by pooling. For a static layer-level router there need not be an activation input at all.

Those are different architectures. A silent mean over the sentence is not clerical tidying. It changes who gets their own mix.

Squeeze the notebooks first

A layer whose features have grown large should not automatically shout louder about routing. The HC papers normalize before the dynamic linear step, and the mHC-family papers use RMSNorm on the feature list they feed the router [R001]; [R002]; [R003]; [R004]; [R005].

RMSNorm divides by a root-mean-square along a specified last axis [R013]. The name is worth keeping because the axis is the whole point. Normalize the wrong axis and you have averaged the wrong story.

For a token-local squeeze that only glues notebooks to features, one explicit shape ledger is

\[ \begin{aligned} x_l &\in \mathbb R^{B\times T\times d\times C},\\ \operatorname{flatten}_{d,C}(x_l)&\in\mathbb R^{B\times T\times dC},\\ \hat x_l=\operatorname{RMSNorm}(\operatorname{flatten}_{d,C}(x_l)) &\in\mathbb R^{B\times T\times dC}. \end{aligned} \]

This flattening does not average tokens together. It makes one feature vector from the \(d\) notebook vectors that share a batch index and a token index. Conversely,

\[ \hat x_l=\frac1T\sum_{t=1}^{T}N(x_{l,:,t,:,:}) \]

would be a sentence-level reduction. It would produce a different router. Neither choice is universally correct. Only an implementation can settle which one it uses.

A linear guess

Now the squeezed list has to become scores. For one operator type \(a\in\{\mathrm{pre},\mathrm{post},\mathrm{res}\}\), keep four objects:

  • \(W_l^a\in\mathbb R^{r\times q_a}\), a matrix of learned weights;
  • \(b_l^a\in\mathbb R^{q_a}\), a bias;
  • \(\alpha_l^a\in\mathbb R\), a learned loudness;
  • \(u_l^a\in\mathbb R^{G\times q_a}\), the coordinates.

The linear step is

\[ u_l^a=\alpha_l^a\hat x_lW_l^a+b_l^a. \]

That multiply is the projection. The name is worth keeping because it is a map from the squeezed width \(r\) onto a coordinate width \(q_a\). It is not yet a routing matrix.

Some HC writeups wrap the projected activation in tanh before the small loudness and a static term [R001]; [R002]. That changes derivatives and bounds the dynamic correction. It does not merge the pencil with the rulebook.

Read and write routers often need \(q_{\mathrm{pre}}=q_{\mathrm{post}}=d\): one number per notebook. Residual routers differ by variant:

  • HC can form unconstrained dynamic mixing coefficients directly [R001].
  • mHC produces candidate residual logits and then runs a finite Sinkhorn–Knopp cleanup [R002].
  • mHC-lite produces \(d!\) mixture logits, softmaxes them, and mixes all \(d\times d\) permutations [R003].
  • KromHC emits mixture coordinates for smaller permutation families, builds doubly stochastic factors, and takes their Kronecker product [R004].
  • go-mHC emits \(ds(ds-1)/2\) coordinates for a skew-symmetric \(ds\times ds\) matrix before its Cayley and block-norm construction [R005].

Those are architectural reports from recent papers, not a ranking. The coordinate width need not equal the number of entries in \(H_l^a\). go-mHC with \(N=ds\) emits \(q_{\mathrm{res}}=N(N-1)/2\) skew coordinates and still returns a \(d\times d\) leftover matrix [R005].

Two scores that are not yet a matrix

Take one routing group, two notebooks, and two features each: \(G=1\), \(d=2\), and \(C=2\). After normalization and flattening, suppose

\[ \hat x=[1,-1,0.5,-0.5]\in\mathbb R^{1\times4}. \]

We want two coordinates, so choose

\[ W = \begin{pmatrix} 1&0\\ 0&1\\ 1&1\\ -1&1 \end{pmatrix} \in\mathbb R^{4\times2}, \qquad \alpha=0.1, \qquad b=[0,0]\in\mathbb R^2. \]

The projection gives

\[ \hat xW=[2,-1], \qquad u=0.1[2,-1]=[0.2,-0.1]\in\mathbb R^{1\times2}. \]

These two numbers are not a \(2\times2\) leftover matrix. Use the mHC-lite-style rulebook for \(d=2\). There are two permutation matrices,

\[ P_1= \begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}, \qquad P_2= \begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}. \]

Softmax turns the coordinates into mixing weights:

\[ a=\operatorname{softmax}(u)\approx[0.5744,0.4256]. \]

The rulebook then builds

\[ H^{\mathrm{res}} =a_1P_1+a_2P_2 =\begin{pmatrix} 0.5744&0.4256\\ 0.4256&0.5744 \end{pmatrix}. \]

Every entry is nonnegative. Every row and every column sums to one. Those facts come from softmax plus the permutation mixture, not from the linear multiply.

If the two notebook vectors are \(x^{(1)}=[2,0]\) and \(x^{(2)}=[0,4]\), both in \(\mathbb R^2\), leftover routing gives

\[ H^{\mathrm{res}} \begin{bmatrix}x^{(1)}\\x^{(2)}\end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} [1.1489,1.7022]\\ [0.8511,2.2978] \end{bmatrix} \in\mathbb R^{2\times2}. \]

Change \(W\), \(b\), or \(\alpha\), and the proposal changes. Keep the same rulebook, and the row-and-column sums stay exact. The rulebook is doing useful work while receiving none of the applause.

The rulebook

The projection has spoken. Something still has to decide which operators exist.

Let \(\mathcal C_a\) be the variant-specific map. Then

\[ H_l^a=\mathcal C_a(u_l^a). \]

That map is the constraint map. The name is worth keeping because it is a separate machine from \(W\). The projection learns where to aim. The constraint map decides which targets exist.

The mHC paper maps candidate read and write scores with

\[ H_l^{\mathrm{pre}}=\sigma(\widetilde H_l^{\mathrm{pre}}), \qquad H_l^{\mathrm{post}}=2\sigma(\widetilde H_l^{\mathrm{post}}), \]

and maps the candidate leftover matrix with a finite number of Sinkhorn–Knopp iterations [R002]. Sigmoid puts each read number in \((0,1)\). Twice-sigmoid puts each write number in \((0,2)\). Those maps do not make the read or write vectors into probability distributions. Their entries need not sum to one.

The leftover operator has a different contract in each variant. Finite Sinkhorn aims at a matrix whose rows and columns sum to one, but a finite loop leaves a little numerical dirt. Permutation mixtures and KromHC are exact by construction in exact arithmetic. go-mHC maps skew coordinates to an orthogonal matrix with a Cayley transform, cuts that matrix into \(s\times s\) blocks, and sets each routing entry to a scaled squared block size; that construction proves nonnegativity and unit row and column sums [R005]. Later chapters derive each map. Here the important fact is that none of them is “the linear layer.”

Teaching the pencil

Training has to move both stages, even when only the first stage owns parameters.

Let \(\mathcal L\) be the training loss and drop the layer and operator labels for a minute. Define

\[ g_u=\frac{\partial\mathcal L}{\partial u} =J_{\mathcal C}(u)^{\top} \frac{\partial\mathcal L}{\partial H}, \]

where \(J_{\mathcal C}(u)\) is the Jacobian of the constraint map — the grid of how each output entry moves when each coordinate moves. This grid is the bridge from “which routing matrix would reduce the loss?” to “how should the coordinates move?”

For \(u=\alpha\hat xW+b\), summing over routing groups,

\[ \frac{\partial\mathcal L}{\partial W} =\alpha\hat x^{\top}g_u, \qquad \frac{\partial\mathcal L}{\partial b} =\sum_G g_u, \]

\[ \frac{\partial\mathcal L}{\partial \alpha} =\sum_G\left\langle g_u,\hat xW\right\rangle, \qquad \frac{\partial\mathcal L}{\partial\hat x} =\alpha g_uW^{\top}. \]

Backpropagation therefore trains \(W\), \(b\), and \(\alpha\), and also sends a gradient through the normalization into the current activation. The map itself may have no learned parameters and still affect every upstream gradient. Softmax, sigmoid, a fixed number of Sinkhorn steps, linear solves in a Cayley transform, and squared block sizes are differentiable on their regular domains.

Constraint maps can also muffle gradients. Saturated sigmoid or softmax coordinates have small derivatives. A finite Sinkhorn loop has a long normalization Jacobian. The Cayley route differentiates through a solve. “Exact constraint” describes the forward output. It does not automatically mean easy optimization.

Start almost idle

The dynamic correction should not vandalize the leftover highway before seeing one minibatch. The cited implementations therefore combine small loudness with biases or static terms chosen near ordinary residual behavior [R001]; [R002]; [R003]; [R004]; [R005].

HC initializes dynamic projection weights to zero and dynamic scales to \(0.01\), while keeping static routing terms [R001]. The mHC experiments report a gating-factor initialization of \(0.01\) [R002]. mHC-lite and KromHC initialize projection weights to zero, loudness to \(0.01\), and leftover biases so softmax weight piles onto identity permutations [R003]; [R004]. Finite logits still leak a little weight onto the alternatives, so “near identity” is the honest description.

That loudness number is the gain. The name is worth keeping because it is not a third copy of the bias. Bias sets the input-independent default route. Gain controls how loudly the activation-dependent correction speaks. Projection weights decide what evidence changes that route.

Why not initialize both \(W=0\) and \(\alpha=0\)? Look at the gradient above. \(\partial\mathcal L/\partial W\) is multiplied by \(\alpha\). If both start at zero, \(W\) receives no first-step gradient. Worse, \(\partial\mathcal L/\partial\alpha\) is zero while \(W=0\). A small nonzero gain lets the projection begin learning while keeping its first effect small.

What the papers actually flatten

For one routing item, the mHC paper writes the notebook state as \(x_l\in\mathbb R^{d\times C}\), then flattens it:

\[ v_l=\operatorname{vec}(x_l)\in\mathbb R^{1\times dC}, \qquad v_l'=\operatorname{RMSNorm}(v_l). \]

The normalized \(dC\)-wide vector feeds three learned projections. The pre- and post-router projections use weights in \(\mathbb R^{dC\times d}\). The leftover projection uses weights in \(\mathbb R^{dC\times d^2}\) and then reshapes its output to \(d\times d\). Sigmoid maps constrain the read and write numbers. Finite Sinkhorn constrains the leftover candidate [R002]. So the primary mHC construction does flatten the per-item notebook and feature axes. mHC-lite and KromHC keep this \(dC\)-wide routing input and change only the leftover constraint map [R003]; [R004]. Across the family, the trainable projection and the constraint map remain separate stages.

For go-mHC leftover routing, let \(s\) be the block size and \(N=ds\). The pipeline is

\[ \hat x_l \xrightarrow{\text{linear projection}} u_l\in\mathbb R^{N(N-1)/2} \xrightarrow{\text{fill skew }A_l} A_l\in\mathbb R^{N\times N} \xrightarrow{\text{Cayley}}Q_l\in O(N) \xrightarrow{\text{block norms}}H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\in\mathbb R^{d\times d}. \]

The projection learns coordinates. Skew filling, the Cayley transform, and the block norms enforce the geometry. The Cayley transform does not “learn” anything by itself.

How fine is the decision? The papers and their public code do not all say the same thing, and one of them does not quite say.

  • HC’s published PyTorch-like Algorithm 2 uses a hyper-hidden tensor of shape \(B\times L\times d\times C\). Its dynamic projections keep the \(B\) and \(L\) axes, so that language-model implementation predicts a mix per batch item and token position [R001].
  • mHC discusses memory access per token, and its pictures average token-level leftover matrices over a chosen sentence. Its displayed single-item equations use \(x_l\in\mathbb R^{d\times C}\), which matches token-level application in the reported language-model implementation [R002].
  • mHC-lite explicitly calls its measured leftover matrices “token-level” and keeps the activation-dependent mHC structure while changing the leftover constraint map [R003].
  • KromHC’s public implementation reshapes (b s) ... d to b ... s d and keeps every leading ... position through the projection. The published code is therefore per item at those leading positions, including token positions when the tensor is a sequence [R004].
  • go-mHC writes the full state as \(d\times B\times L\times D\), but its displayed leftover-router equation introduces a reduced \(\hat x_l'\) without saying which axes were reduced [R005]. The paper supports “activation-dependent and layer-specific.” The equation alone does not justify a token-wise or sentence-wise label. A concrete implementation has to state the reduction first.

A layer index on the parameters means each layer owns a router. It does not mean there is only one routing decision per layer. Likewise, “dynamic” means input-dependent. It does not automatically mean token-wise.

What we lose if we skip a piece

Skip the normalization, and router size can track activation size rather than useful direction. Deep layers may then change their mix merely because their features grew larger.

Skip the learned projection, and an activation-dependent router has no trainable way to pick coordinates. A fixed bias can still give a static mix. It cannot answer the page.

Skip the bias, and the model loses an easy input-independent default. Initialization must then force the desired baseline entirely through activation-dependent terms, which is fragile when those terms start near zero.

Skip the gain, and a randomly initialized or rapidly learned projection can shove the leftover path at full strength from the first update. A gain is not the only stabilizer, but it is a direct knob.

Skip the constraint map, and leftover coordinates become unconstrained mixing entries. Repeated unconstrained leftover matrices can grow or shrink both the forward signal and the backward gradient. Chapter 7 is that failure. Conversely, skip the projection but keep the constraint map, and the matrix can be valid yet deaf — excellent paperwork, little intelligence.

Ways to get this wrong

  • Calling coordinates probabilities. Logits become probabilities only after a map such as softmax.
  • Calling read numbers a normalized distribution. Elementwise sigmoid bounds entries. It does not make their sum one.
  • Treating \(b\) as noise. It encodes the static default route and often carries the near-identity start.
  • Setting both gain and projection to zero. That can block the projection’s first gradient.
  • Flattening every axis. Flattening \(d\) and \(C\) per token is not the same as flattening \(T,d,C\) per sentence.
  • Assuming a deep multilayer predictor. The cited router front ends are small linear projections. Later nonlinear constraints do not retroactively deepen that projection.
  • Claiming token-wise routing from the word “dynamic.” Granularity needs explicit axes or implementation evidence.
  • Confusing an exact constraint with exact identity. A matrix can satisfy the leftover contract exactly and still only sit near the identity at initialization.

Try it

1. Shape the projection

Let \(B=3\), \(T=8\), \(d=4\), and \(C=64\). A token-level router flattens only notebook and feature axes and emits leftover logits for a dense \(4\times4\) candidate. Give the shapes of \(\hat x\), \(W\), \(b\), and \(u\).

Answer

Here \(r=dC=256\) and \(q=d^2=16\). Thus \(\hat x\in\mathbb R^{3\times8\times256}\), \(W\in\mathbb R^{256\times16}\), \(b\in\mathbb R^{16}\), and \(u\in\mathbb R^{3\times8\times16}\).

2. Separate the guess from the license

Using the numerical example, replace \(u=[0.2,-0.1]\) with \(u=[5,-2]\). Does the projection output sum to one? Does the softmax output? Is the resulting permutation mixture doubly stochastic?

Answer

The coordinate sum is \(3\), so the raw projection is not normalized. Softmax produces positive weights summing to one (approximately \([0.9991,0.0009]\)). A convex combination of the two permutation matrices is doubly stochastic, independent of the raw coordinate sum.

3. Diagnose a dead start

Suppose \(u=\alpha\hat xW+b\), with \(W=0\), \(\alpha=0\), and a loss gradient \(g_u\ne0\). Compute the first-step gradients for \(W\) and \(\alpha\).

Answer

\(\partial\mathcal L/\partial W=\alpha\hat x^\top g_u=0\). Also \(\partial\mathcal L/\partial\alpha=\langle g_u,\hat xW\rangle=0\). The dynamic path cannot start moving on that step. A small nonzero gain avoids this particular dead start.

4. Audit the axes

A function accepts x with shape [B,T,d,C], computes h = rmsnorm(x.reshape(B,T,d*C)), and applies h @ W. Is routing token-level, sequence-level, or layer-level? What single change would make it sequence-level?

Answer

The \(B\) and \(T\) axes survive, so it is token-level. Reducing over \(T\), for example h = h.mean(dim=1) to shape [B,d*C], would produce one routing feature per sequence. Whether mean pooling is a good design is a separate question.

5. Count go-mHC coordinates

For \(d=4\) notebooks and block size \(s=2\), find \(N\), the number of skew coordinates, and the final leftover-matrix shape.

Answer

\(N=ds=8\). A real \(8\times8\) skew-symmetric matrix has \(8\cdot7/2=28\) independent coordinates. The constraint construction returns \(H^{\mathrm{res}}\in\mathbb R^{4\times4}\).

Check yourself

  1. A router sees a page and emits a leftover matrix. Which arrow learned the aim, and which arrow issued the license?

    The projection \(u=\alpha\hat x W+b\) learned the aim. The constraint map \(H=\mathcal C(u)\) issued the license.

  2. You hear “dynamic router.” Must every token get its own mix?

    No. Dynamic means input-dependent. Token-wise, sentence-wise, and layer-wise are extra claims. They need axes or code.

  3. Why initialize \(\alpha=0.01\) instead of \(\alpha=0\) when \(W=0\)?

    \(\partial\mathcal L/\partial W\) is multiplied by \(\alpha\), and \(\partial\mathcal L/\partial\alpha\) vanishes while \(W=0\). A small nonzero gain lets the pencil start moving.

  4. After sigmoid, a read row has entries in \((0,1)\). Is it a probability distribution?

    Not unless the entries happen to sum to one. Sigmoid bounds. It does not normalize.

  5. go-mHC emits \(N(N-1)/2\) numbers and returns a \(d\times d\) matrix. Are the coordinates flattened matrix entries?

    No. They fill a skew matrix that a later construction turns into \(H^{\mathrm{res}}\).

The compact picture is

\[ \boxed{\text{normalized activation}\rightarrow \text{linear coordinates}\rightarrow \text{valid routing operator}}. \]

Why bother with the last arrow? Leftover routing is applied again and again through depth. Even a modest growth factor compounds: a direction multiplied by \(1.1\) across 100 layers grows by \(1.1^{100}\). That is less a highway and more a launch vehicle. Chapter 7 is that calculation.

Sources

  • [R001] D. Zhu et al., “Hyper-Connections,” arXiv:2409.19606v3, 2025. https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.19606
  • [R002] Z. Xie et al., “mHC: Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections,” arXiv:2512.24880v2, 2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.24880
  • [R003] Y. Yang and J. Gao, “mHC-lite: You Don’t Need 20 Sinkhorn-Knopp Iterations,” arXiv:2601.05732, 2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.05732
  • [R004] W. Zhou et al., “KromHC: Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections with Kronecker-Product Residual Matrices,” arXiv:2601.21579v2, 2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.21579
  • [R005] T. Dandachi and S. Diggs-Galligan, “go-mHC: Direct Parameterization of Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections via Generalized Orthostochastic Matrices,” arXiv:2604.02309v1, 2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02309
  • [R013] B. Zhang and R. Sennrich, “Root Mean Square Layer Normalization,” NeurIPS 2019, arXiv:1910.07467. https://arxiv.org/abs/1910.07467

Chapter 7 — Stretching a number until it explodes

Take the number \(1.1\). Multiply it by itself. Do it again. After a hundred multiplications you have about \(13{,}780\).

A ten-percent raise looks polite at one layer. Depth is an excellent accountant and a terrible negotiator.

That is the whole chapter in one number. Later we will mix leftover copies of a sentence with a matrix, then do it again, then again. The matrix can stretch some directions and squash others. Before we let it, we need to see what repeated stretching does.

One number, many layers

Suppose each leftover copy is just a single number \(u_l\), and each layer multiplies it by the same factor \(h\):

\[ u_{l+1}=h u_l, \qquad u_L=h^L u_0. \]

Everything depends on how big \(|h|\) is.

\(h\) \(h^L\) behavior Meaning
\(1.1\) grows without bound explosion
\(0.8\) tends to \(0\) decay
\(1\) remains \(1\) persistence
\(-1\) alternates between \(1\) and \(-1\) persistent sign alternation
\(-0.8\) alternates while shrinking oscillatory decay

If the factor is allowed to change from layer to layer, write \(h_l\) for the factor at layer \(l\). Then

\[ u_L=\left(\prod_{l=0}^{L-1}h_l\right)u_0, \qquad \log\left|\frac{u_L}{u_0}\right| = \sum_{l=0}^{L-1}\log|h_l|, \]

provided no \(h_l\) is zero. No single layer is in charge. The sum of the log-gains is.

Two stretches at once

One leftover copy is too small. Take two numbers and stretch them differently:

\[ D = \begin{bmatrix} 1.2&0\\ 0&0.5 \end{bmatrix} \in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}, \qquad u_0 = \begin{bmatrix}1\\1\end{bmatrix} \in\mathbb{R}^{2}. \]

The first coordinate is multiplied by \(1.2\) every time. The second is multiplied by \(0.5\). After \(L\) layers,

\[ D^L = \begin{bmatrix} 1.2^L&0\\ 0&0.5^L \end{bmatrix}, \qquad u_L = \begin{bmatrix}1.2^L\\0.5^L\end{bmatrix}. \]

The first four steps are

\(l\) \(u_l^{(1)}\) \(u_l^{(2)}\)
0 1 1
1 1.2 0.5
2 1.44 0.25
3 1.728 0.125
4 2.0736 0.0625

One direction is already more than twice as loud. The other is almost gone. A single length for the whole pair — “the norm looks moderate” — can hide both facts.

Those two stretch factors, \(1.2\) and \(0.5\), have a name. Each is an eigenvalue of \(D\). The name is worth keeping because it still works when the special directions are not the coordinate axes: it is the factor the matrix uses when it stretches a direction without tilting it.

A direction that only stretches

A nonzero list \(q\) is special for a square matrix \(H\) when

\[ Hq=\lambda q. \]

The matrix does not scramble the orientation of \(q\). It only rescales it, maybe flips the sign, maybe twists it in the complex plane. That special list is an eigenvector. Along it, the matrix problem is the scalar problem we already solved:

\[ H^L q=\lambda^L q. \]

If a general starting list can be built from these special directions, each piece just rides its own stretch factor. That is the whole reason we bother finding them.

The biggest stretch

Look back at \(D\). One eigenvalue is \(1.2\). The other is \(0.5\). After many layers the \(1.2\) piece dominates. The eventual explosion or decay of a fixed mixer is decided by the largest of those magnitudes.

That largest magnitude is the spectral radius:

\[ \rho(H)=\max_i|\lambda_i|, \]

where \(\lambda_i\) are the eigenvalues of \(H\). The name is worth keeping because it is one number, read off the eigenvalues, that answers the first stability question for a fixed matrix: will repeated application eventually explode?

For \(D\), \(\rho(D)=1.2>1\). For a mixer whose every eigenvalue sits inside the unit circle, \(\rho<1\), and the long-run story is decay. For a fixed matrix that can be written as \(H=V\Lambda V^{-1}\), spectral radius gives the correct asymptotic exponential rate. It is an excellent first question. It is not the last one.

A turn that does not grow

Not every mixer only stretches. Some turn.

\[ R = \begin{bmatrix} 0&-1\\ 1&0 \end{bmatrix} \in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}. \]

This rotates real pairs counterclockwise by \(90^\circ\). Starting from \(u_0=(1,0)^\top\),

\[ u_1=(0,1)^\top, \qquad u_2=(-1,0)^\top, \qquad u_3=(0,-1)^\top, \qquad u_4=(1,0)^\top. \]

After four layers we are back where we started. The Euclidean length never changed:

\[ \|Ru\|_2=\|u\|_2 \]

for every real pair \(u\), because \(R^\top R=I_2\).

The eigenvalues of \(R\) are \(i\) and \(-i\). Both have magnitude one. They are complex, but the network state stays real. A real \(2\times 2\) rotation just needs a conjugate pair to record a turn. The angle is the phase. The magnitude is still the radial gain.

If we use \(1.05R\) instead, the same turn is accompanied by growth \(1.05^L\). Phase describes cycling. Magnitude decides explosion or decay.

The leftover highway

We have been stretching abstract pairs. In the network those pairs are leftover copies of a sentence table.

The running state is

\[ x_l\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m\times n}, \]

where \(d\) is how many leftover copies we carry — the persistent residual streams — \(m\) is how many tokens sit in the sentence, and \(n\) is how many features each token has. A routing matrix

\[ H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d} \]

acts only on the stream axis. The same stream-to-stream map hits every token and every feature.

Freeze one token and one feature. Their \(d\) stream values form a column

\[ u_l\in\mathbb{R}^{d}. \]

That is the pair we have been stretching. We have not pretended that a token row is itself a leftover copy.

The Hyper-Connections update is [R001]; [R002]

\[ x_{l+1} = H_l^{\mathrm{res}}x_l + (H_l^{\mathrm{post}})^\top F(H_l^{\mathrm{pre}}x_l,W_l). \]

Here \(H_l^{\mathrm{pre}}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times d}\) reads from the streams, \(F\) maps an \(m\times n\) table to another of that shape, and \(H_l^{\mathrm{post}}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times d}\) writes the result back. The first term is different: \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\) transports all leftover state to the next layer.

If we temporarily silence the computed branch, the stream column obeys

\[ u_{l+1}=H_l^{\mathrm{res}}u_l. \]

After \(L\) layers,

\[ u_L = H_{L-1}^{\mathrm{res}}H_{L-2}^{\mathrm{res}}\cdots H_0^{\mathrm{res}}u_0. \]

The rightmost matrix acts first. Reversing that order is harmless only when the matrices commute, which dynamic routers generally need not do.

This product is the architecture’s leftover-only highway. If it magnifies some stream direction by a huge factor, activations can explode before the computed branches have their say. If it crushes a direction, information carried only there becomes hard to recover. The transpose of the same product appears when we send gradients back down the highway.

The mHC paper treats the loss of the ordinary residual identity map as a scalability risk and reports that unconstrained Hyper-Connections training became unstable at larger scale [R002]. That is a recent paper’s motivation and a measured result in the regimes they tried. It is not a theorem that every unconstrained model must fail.

Every injection rides the same road

The computed branch still happens. Write its contribution as

\[ b_l = (H_l^{\mathrm{post}})^\top F(H_l^{\mathrm{pre}}x_l,W_l) \in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m\times n}. \]

Then

\[ x_{l+1}=H_l^{\mathrm{res}}x_l+b_l. \]

For indices \(b>a\), define the ordered product

\[ P_{b:a}=H_{b-1}^{\mathrm{res}}H_{b-2}^{\mathrm{res}}\cdots H_a^{\mathrm{res}} \in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}, \]

and let \(P_{a:a}=I_d\). Unrolling the recurrence gives the exact identity

\[ x_L = P_{L:0}x_0 + \sum_{i=0}^{L-1}P_{L:i+1}b_i. \]

The initial state is carried by every leftover mixer. The injection \(b_i\) is created after \(H_i^{\mathrm{res}}x_i\), so it is carried only by layers \(i+1\) through \(L-1\). That is the same product the mHC analysis highlights [R002].

A large \(\|P_{L:i+1}\|\) can therefore amplify not only the start but also a branch output inserted much later. Strong contraction can erase older injections. This statement is about the leftover transport factor. The injection \(b_i\) itself depends on \(x_i\), so the full nonlinear path still needs the branch.

Gradients walk the road backward

Let a scalar loss \(\mathcal{L}\) depend on \(x_L\). Isolate the derivative through the leftover-only path. For the frozen token-feature column,

\[ g_l=\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial u_l}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}. \]

On that path \(u_{l+1}=H_l^{\mathrm{res}}u_l\), so

\[ g_l=(H_l^{\mathrm{res}})^\top g_{l+1}, \qquad g_0=P_{L:0}^\top g_L. \]

Forward stretch and backward stretch are partners. What a matrix does to lengths, its transpose does to gradients.

Eigenvalues do not directly answer “how much can one step stretch any vector?” That one-step worst case is

\[ \sigma_{\max}(H) = \max_{v\neq 0}\frac{\|Hv\|_2}{\|v\|_2}. \]

That number is the largest singular value. Unlike an eigenvalue, it is defined for every real matrix and it talks about Euclidean length, not about a special direction. The name is worth keeping because it is the honest one-step gain, with no “if you happen to start on an eigenvector” attached.

The leftover product cannot stretch more than the product of the one-step worst cases:

\[ \|P_{L:0}\|_2 \le \prod_{l=0}^{L-1}\|H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\|_2 = \prod_{l=0}^{L-1}\sigma_{\max}(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}). \]

The bound can be loose: the direction one layer stretches most may not be the direction the next layer stretches most. It is still a valid alarm.

The full network Jacobian is not merely \(P_{L:0}\). Differentiating \(b_l\) brings in \(H_l^{\mathrm{pre}}\), \(H_l^{\mathrm{post}}\), any input dependence they have, and \(F\). The transpose product diagnoses one important highway, not every gradient.

Tame numbers, a wild start

Eigenvalues can look calm while a short product still grows.

A real matrix \(A\) is called normal when \(A^\top A=AA^\top\). Symmetric matrices and rotation matrices are normal: their special directions sit at right angles. Drop that, and the special directions can lean on each other — or fail to span the space at all.

Here is a matrix that does both jobs badly:

\[ A = \begin{bmatrix} 0.8&0.6\\ 0&0.8 \end{bmatrix} \in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}. \]

Both eigenvalues equal \(0.8\), so \(\rho(A)=0.8<1\). The long-run story is decay. But \(A\) is a repeated stretch with a sideways shove. For \(L\ge 1\),

\[ A^L = \begin{bmatrix} 0.8^L&0.6L\,0.8^{L-1}\\ 0&0.8^L \end{bmatrix}. \]

Start from \(u_0=(0,1)^\top\):

\[ u_L = \begin{bmatrix} 0.6L\,0.8^{L-1}\\ 0.8^L \end{bmatrix}. \]

At \(L=5\) this is \((1.2288,0.32768)^\top\), whose Euclidean length is about \(1.272\) — larger than the starting length \(1\). Eventually \(0.8^L\) wins and the state dies. First it grows. For deeper or more strongly sheared systems, the bump can be much larger.

The off-diagonal term is a polynomial \(L\) times a decaying exponential. Spectral radius describes the eventual rate of powers of this fixed matrix. It does not bound every finite-depth response by \(\rho(A)^L\).

A matrix that fails \(A^\top A=AA^\top\) is nonnormal. The name is worth keeping because it marks exactly this gap: calm eigenvalues, rude transients [R032]; [R033].

If a fixed matrix can be written \(A=V\Lambda V^{-1}\), then

\[ \|A^L\|_2 \le \|V\|_2\|V^{-1}\|_2\rho(A)^L. \]

The factor \(\kappa_2(V)=\|V\|_2\|V^{-1}\|_2\) measures how tilted the special directions are. Nearly parallel eigenvectors can make it huge. Eigenvalues may be calm while the eigenvectors are quietly stacking chairs against the exit.

Mixers that change their minds

Now let the matrix vary with depth. There may be no shared special directions and no identity such as

\[ \operatorname{spec}(H_{L-1}\cdots H_0) = \prod_l\operatorname{spec}(H_l). \]

That displayed relation is generally false.

A sharp counterexample uses

\[ A = \begin{bmatrix}0&2\\0&0\end{bmatrix}, \qquad B = \begin{bmatrix}0&0\\2&0\end{bmatrix}. \]

Each matrix, applied twice, gives zero. Every eigenvalue of each matrix is zero. Alternate them, with \(H_0=A\), \(H_1=B\), \(H_2=A\), and so on. Then

\[ BA = \begin{bmatrix}0&0\\0&4\end{bmatrix}. \]

For \(u_0=(0,1)^\top\),

\[ u_{2k}=(BA)^k u_0=4^k u_0. \]

Every individual spectral radius is zero. The two-layer product explodes. The matrices hand the state back and forth between directions that each one amplifies. Checking \(\rho(H_l^{\mathrm{res}})\le 1\) independently at every layer is not a complete stability certificate for dynamic routing.

For a set of possible routing matrices, the worst asymptotic growth over all products drawn from the set has a name: the joint spectral radius [R034]. The name is worth keeping because it is the right question for a family of mixers, not one mixer. It can be painful to compute. In practice, architectures often pick constraints with simple invariants and closure under multiplication, rather than solving a worst-case product problem during training.

What a useful rule would do

In the notation of the mHC paper, \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\) is the leftover-copy mixer in the Hyper-Connections update [R002]. It acts along the \(d\)-stream axis of \(x_l\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m\times n}\). Original Hyper-Connections permits learned static or input-dependent connection weights [R001]. Input dependence makes the product trajectory-dependent: different examples can induce different sequences of mixers.

The examples above are the design pressure. A useful constraint should ideally give properties that survive products — a conserved direction, a norm bound — while still letting information move among leftover copies.

The 2025–2026 mHC work reports instability for unconstrained Hyper-Connections at increased training scale and motivates restricting leftover mixers to doubly stochastic matrices [R002]. That constraint and its exact guarantees are the next chapter. The go-mHC paper keeps constrained leftover routing but replaces the constraint map with a generalized-orthostochastic construction [R005]. This chapter does not infer that construction from the stability motivation alone.

Constraining \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\) cannot by itself bound the entire nonlinear network. The read gate, the branch \(F\), the write gate, normalization, the optimizer, finite precision, and derivatives of input-dependent routing all remain. The narrower claim is valuable enough: constrain a repeatedly multiplied structural path instead of leaving it arbitrary.

Ways to get this wrong

If leftover mixers are entirely unconstrained, several failures are possible rather than inevitable:

  1. Exploding transport. A direction with repeated gain above one can dominate activations. A branch injection can also be magnified by every later mixer.
  2. Vanishing transport. Contracted directions lose old information and provide a weak leftover-only gradient path.
  3. Anisotropy. One direction can grow while another decays, as in the diagonal example. A single average statistic may miss both.
  4. Transient amplification. Nonnormal matrices can create large finite-depth responses even when their eigenvalues predict eventual decay.
  5. Sequence-dependent surprises. Dynamic matrices can align amplifying directions across layers. Safe-looking per-layer eigenvalues do not imply a safe product.
  6. Oscillation. Negative or complex eigenvalues can alternate or rotate state. Oscillation is not itself a failure — the \(90^\circ\) rotation preserved length — but uncontrolled scale attached to it can be.

Large transient gains may make optimization more sensitive to precision, initialization, and batch-specific routing. That is a plausible consequence of the product analysis, not a universal measured result for every Hyper-Connections variant.

The response is not “ban matrices.” The response is to choose a routing family whose algebraic properties match the conservation and stability properties the architecture needs.

  • “Every eigenvalue has magnitude below one, so every intermediate activation shrinks.” False for nonnormal matrices. That conclusion is asymptotic for powers of a fixed matrix, not a finite-depth norm bound.
  • Confusing spectral radius with the one-step stretch \(\|A\|_2\). In general, \(\rho(A)\le\|A\|_2\), often strictly. Singular values govern worst-case one-step Euclidean gain.
  • Multiplying per-layer eigenvalues. Eigenvalues of products do not generally equal pairwise products of eigenvalues. This shortcut needs special structure, such as the matrices sharing the same special directions.
  • Ignoring product order. \(H_1H_0\) and \(H_0H_1\) can differ.
  • Calling every negative eigenvalue unstable. The magnitude controls growth. The sign adds alternation.
  • Calling every complex eigenvalue explosive. Complex phase gives rotation; modulus gives radial gain.
  • Assuming \(\lambda=1\) preserves all state. It preserves components along its associated eigenvector. Other directions may behave differently.
  • Mixing axes. \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\) mixes leftover copies, not the \(m\) token positions or \(n\) feature coordinates in this equation.
  • Claiming a routing constraint stabilizes the whole model. It controls specified properties of one structural path; the branch and dynamic-router derivatives still matter.

Try it

1. One number, four layers

Let \(u_0=3\) and \(u_{l+1}=-0.5u_l\). What are \(u_1\), \(u_2\), and \(u_4\)? Across those four layers, how large is the leftover-only gradient multiplier?

Answer

\(u_1=-1.5\), \(u_2=0.75\), and \(u_4=(-0.5)^4(3)=0.1875\). The scalar backward multiplier has magnitude \(|-0.5|^4=0.0625\).

2. Two directions at once

For \(D=\operatorname{diag}(-1,0.9)\) and \(u_0=(2,3)^\top\), compute \(u_3\). Which coordinate persists and alternates? Which decays?

Answer

\(D^3=\operatorname{diag}(-1,0.9^3)\), so \(u_3=(-2,2.187)^\top\). The first coordinate persists in magnitude and alternates sign. The second decays without sign alternation.

3. A turn, then a growing turn

Let

\[ R=\begin{bmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{bmatrix}. \]

Show that \(R^4=I_2\). Then give the length of \((1.1R)^{10}u_0\) when \(\|u_0\|_2=2\).

Answer

Two applications give \(R^2=-I_2\), hence \(R^4=I_2\). Since \(R\) preserves Euclidean length, \((1.1R)^{10}\) scales length by \(1.1^{10}\). The answer is \(2(1.1)^{10}\approx 5.187\).

4. A bump before the decay

For

\[ A=\begin{bmatrix}a&c\\0&a\end{bmatrix}, \]

verify that

\[ A^L=\begin{bmatrix}a^L&Lc\,a^{L-1}\\0&a^L\end{bmatrix} \]

for \(L\ge 1\). Why can \(|a|<1\) still grow for a while?

Answer

Write \(A=aI+N\), where \(N=\begin{bmatrix}0&c\\0&0\end{bmatrix}\) and \(N^2=0\). The binomial expansion leaves only \((aI)^L+L(aI)^{L-1}N\), giving the formula. Although \(a^L\) eventually decays, the off-diagonal term contains \(Lca^{L-1}\), whose polynomial factor can grow for a while and can be large when \(c\) is large.

5. Dead-looking layers, live pair

Using the matrices \(A\) and \(B\) from the changing-mixer example, compute \(AB\) as well as \(BA\). Starting from \(e_1=(1,0)^\top\), which order amplifies it after two layers?

Answer

\(AB=\operatorname{diag}(4,0)\) and \(BA=\operatorname{diag}(0,4)\). With the convention that the rightmost matrix acts first, \(ABe_1=4e_1\) amplifies \(e_1\), while \(BAe_1=0\). Order changes the result even though both individual matrices have only zero eigenvalues.

Check yourself

  1. What does an eigenvalue tell you? Along its special direction, the matrix is just a number. Apply it \(L\) times and that number is raised to the \(L\).

  2. What is the spectral radius? The largest of those numbers’ magnitudes. For a fixed mixer it is the eventual explosion-or-decay rate.

  3. Why is \(\rho<1\) not a finite-depth safety proof? A nonnormal matrix can grow first and decay later. Spectral radius describes the long run of a fixed matrix, not every short product.

  4. Why is checking each layer’s eigenvalues not enough? Changing mixers can hand the state between amplifying directions. Every individual spectral radius can be zero while the product explodes.

  5. What do singular values answer that eigenvalues do not? The worst one-step stretch of Euclidean length, for every real matrix, not just along a special direction.

  6. What should a leftover-mixer constraint actually do? Give a property that survives products — a conserved direction or a norm bound — without pretending it stabilizes the whole nonlinear network.

You should now be able to read

\[ P_{L:0}=H_{L-1}^{\mathrm{res}}\cdots H_0^{\mathrm{res}} \]

as a depth-wise transport operator on the stream axis. Stability is not one binary property. Ask exactly what is conserved, what length is bounded, over which matrices, for how many layers, and whether the claim concerns the leftover-only path or the full network.

Next we let the leftover mixers move information, but we stop them from freely creating or destroying aggregate signal. The next chapter introduces nonnegative matrices whose rows and columns each sum to one.

Sources

  • [R001] D. Zhu et al., “Hyper-Connections,” arXiv:2409.19606, 2024; revised 2025. https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.19606
  • [R002] Z. Xie et al., “mHC: Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections,” arXiv:2512.24880, 2025; revised 2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.24880
  • [R032] L. N. Trefethen and M. Embree, Spectra and Pseudospectra: The Behavior of Nonnormal Matrices and Operators, Princeton University Press, 2005. https://doi.org/10.1515/9780691213101
  • [R033] R. A. Horn and C. R. Johnson, Matrix Analysis, 2nd ed., Cambridge University Press, 2012. https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9781139020411
  • [R034] G.-C. Rota and G. Strang, “A note on the joint spectral radius,” Indagationes Mathematicae, 22, 379–381, 1960. https://doi.org/10.1016/S1385-7258(60)50046-1
  • [R005] T. Dandachi and S. Diggs-Galligan, “go-mHC: Direct Parameterization of Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections via Generalized Orthostochastic Matrices,” arXiv:2604.02309v1, 2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02309

Chapter 8 — Recipes that keep the total

Three cups sit on the table. The first holds 9, the second holds 3, the third is empty. We are going to remix them. No extra liquid. Nothing down the drain.

A recipe for a new cup is a short list of fractions: how much of the first old cup, how much of the second, how much of the third. If those fractions are nonnegative and add to one, the new cup is a weighted average of the old ones. It cannot come out bigger than 9 or smaller than 0.

One honest recipe is not enough. If every new cup copies the first old cup, each recipe looks legal and we have invented two extra nines. The second honesty check is about the old cups: each one must be spent exactly once across all the new recipes.

One complete recipe per cup

Write three output recipes as the three rows of a table. Here is one we will keep using:

\[ B = \begin{pmatrix} 0.5 & 0.3 & 0.2 \\ 0.2 & 0.4 & 0.4 \\ 0.3 & 0.3 & 0.4 \end{pmatrix}. \]

Row 1 says: the first new cup is half of old cup 1, three tenths of old cup 2, two tenths of old cup 3. Check the addition:

\[ 0.5 + 0.3 + 0.2 = 1, \qquad 0.2 + 0.4 + 0.4 = 1, \qquad 0.3 + 0.3 + 0.4 = 1. \]

Every entry is at least zero. Each row is a complete recipe: nonnegative numbers that add to one.

A square table with that property is row-stochastic. The name is worth keeping because “stochastic” here just means “each row is a finished mixing recipe,” not “the matrix was drawn at random.”

If \(\mathbf{1}_3\) is a column of three ones, the three row sums are the single matrix-vector line

\[ B\mathbf{1}_3 = \mathbf{1}_3. \]

Some probability books stand their recipes on end and call the transpose “stochastic.” We will always say which way the recipes face.

Spend each cup once

Rows do not watch the budget of the old cups. This table has three perfect output recipes:

\[ R = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 1 & 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}. \]

Each new cup copies old cup 1. Pour the original \((9, 3, 0)\) through it and you get \((9, 9, 9)\). The total jumps from 12 to 27. Cup 1 was counted three times. Cups 2 and 3 were ignored.

The missing check is down the columns. Column \(j\) is the list of fractions that use old cup \(j\). If those fractions are nonnegative and add to one, that old cup is spent exactly once.

For our table \(B\),

\[ 0.5 + 0.2 + 0.3 = 1, \qquad 0.3 + 0.4 + 0.3 = 1, \qquad 0.2 + 0.4 + 0.4 = 1. \]

A square table whose columns are complete recipes is column-stochastic. The name is the row idea stood on its side: now each input has total weight one.

In matrix form that is \(B^\top\mathbf{1}_3 = \mathbf{1}_3\), or, written as a sum, \(\sum_i B_{ij} = 1\) for every column \(j\).

Honest both ways

Ask for both books at once. Every row is a complete recipe. Every column is a complete spend. The entries stay nonnegative.

A square table that passes both checks is doubly stochastic. Doubly means the two families of sums, not that anyone squared the matrix.

Our \(B\) already passed both checks, so

\[ B\mathbf{1}_3 = \mathbf{1}_3, \qquad B^\top\mathbf{1}_3 = \mathbf{1}_3, \]

and every \(B_{ij}\ge 0\). That is the whole definition.

Later we will sit this table on leftover copies of a sentence — \(d\) streams, not three kitchen cups. The same two checks still mean the same thing. The mixer may move mass among streams. It may not invent a new total, and it may not throw the total away.

Remix the three numbers

Freeze one token and one feature. The three stream values at that spot are the column

\[ v = \begin{pmatrix} 9 \\ 3 \\ 0 \end{pmatrix}. \]

Each output is one row recipe applied to \(v\):

\[ Bv = \begin{pmatrix} 0.5(9) + 0.3(3) + 0.2(0) \\ 0.2(9) + 0.4(3) + 0.4(0) \\ 0.3(9) + 0.3(3) + 0.4(0) \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 5.4 \\ 3.0 \\ 3.6 \end{pmatrix}. \]

Each new number sits between 0 and 9, because each row is a convex average. The total does not move:

\[ 9 + 3 + 0 = 12 = 5.4 + 3.0 + 3.6. \]

That is not a coincidence of these particular digits. If every column of a table sums to one, then for any column \(v\),

\[ \mathbf{1}_d^\top(Bv) = (\mathbf{1}_d^\top B)v = \mathbf{1}_d^\top v. \]

The sum across streams is conserved. So is the mean \(d^{-1}\mathbf{1}_d^\top v\).

In the network the leftover copies form a table \(x_l\) of shape \(d\times m\times n\): \(d\) streams, \(m\) token positions, \(n\) features. The same \(d\times d\) mixer is reused on every token-feature slice. Conservation holds separately at each of the \(mn\) slices. It does not say that token sums or feature sums are being mixed. Those axes are passengers.

Row honesty gives a second fact. If every stream already holds the same number, routing leaves them equal and unchanged:

\[ B\mathbf{1}_d = \mathbf{1}_d. \]

The all-ones column is a right eigenvector with eigenvalue 1. Column honesty makes \(\mathbf{1}_d^\top\) a left eigenvector with the same eigenvalue. Equal streams stay equal. The total stays the total.

Averages, not extrapolations

Take two legal mixers \(A\) and \(C\). For a number \(t\) between 0 and 1, the blend \(tA + (1-t)C\) is still nonnegative, and every row and column still sums to one. You can average more than two of them the same way.

Given points \(q_1,\ldots,q_k\), a convex combination is

\[ q = \sum_{r=1}^{k} \alpha_r q_r, \qquad \alpha_r \ge 0, \qquad \sum_{r=1}^{k} \alpha_r = 1. \]

The name is worth keeping because the coefficients are not free weights. They are a recipe. Negative coefficients would extrapolate past an endpoint. That is a different sport.

For two points, convex combinations fill the segment between them. For several points, they fill the region you can reach by weighted averaging and never by backing up past a corner.

A set is convex when the segment between any two of its points stays inside. The set of all doubly stochastic \(d\times d\) tables is convex, and each entry is trapped in \([0,1]\), so the set is bounded.

Just swap the cups

The bluntest legal mixer does not blend at all. It just reorders.

The identity leaves every cup where it is:

\[ P_{123} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}. \]

This one sends new cup 1 to old cup 1, new cup 2 to old cup 3, new cup 3 to old cup 2:

\[ P_{132} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix}. \]

A permutation matrix has exactly one \(1\) in every row and every column, and \(0\) everywhere else. Multiplying \(Pv\) only reorders the coordinates of \(v\). The name is worth keeping because the matrix is a permutation, written in the only way a computer can multiply.

Every permutation matrix is doubly stochastic: each row recipe is “take this one cup,” and each old cup is used once. There are \(d!\) of them. For three cups that is six hard rewirings. They will turn out to be the corners of the whole set.

The region those swaps span

How many free numbers are in a doubly stochastic \(d\times d\) table? There are \(d^2\) entries. Nonnegativity is inequality, not an equation. The row sums and column sums look like \(2d\) equations, but they overcount: the grand total is the sum of the row sums and also the sum of the column sums, so one equation is redundant. That leaves \(2d-1\) independent equalities, and

\[ d^2 - (2d-1) = (d-1)^2 \]

free parameters.

For \(d=2\) the set is a line segment. For \(d=3\) it is four-dimensional, even though we write nine entries. Sketching instincts should accept defeat.

The set of all such tables is the Birkhoff polytope

\[ \mathcal{B}_d = \left\{ B\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}: B_{ij}\ge 0,\ B\mathbf{1}_d=\mathbf{1}_d,\ B^\top\mathbf{1}_d=\mathbf{1}_d \right\}. \]

A polytope is a bounded convex region cut out by finitely many linear equalities and inequalities — or, equivalently, the filled region of finitely many corners. The name is worth keeping because later chapters will aim a learned mixer at this set, and we need a short handle for the target.

Birkhoff and von Neumann proved that the corners are exactly the \(d!\) permutation matrices. Equivalently, every doubly stochastic table is a convex combination of permutation matrices [R019]; [R020]. Soft routing sits between hard rewirings. The theorem says a decomposition exists. It does not say the decomposition is unique.

Our running table has one verified writing

\[ B = 0.4\,P_{123} + 0.1\,P_{132} + 0.3\,P_{231} + 0.2\,P_{312}, \]

where the subscript lists which old cup each new cup takes, and the other two corners are

\[ P_{231} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}, \qquad P_{312} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix}. \]

The four coefficients are nonnegative and add to one. Adding the tables entrywise recovers

\[ \begin{pmatrix} 0.5 & 0.3 & 0.2 \\ 0.2 & 0.4 & 0.4 \\ 0.3 & 0.3 & 0.4 \end{pmatrix}. \]

That is both an arithmetic check and a picture: \(B\) is a point inside the polytope, not a new kind of object.

Multiply two recipes

If \(A\) and \(B\) are both doubly stochastic, so is the product \(AB\).

Nonnegative times nonnegative stays nonnegative. The all-ones column is still a fixed point:

\[ (AB)\mathbf{1}_d = A(B\mathbf{1}_d) = A\mathbf{1}_d = \mathbf{1}_d, \]

\[ (AB)^\top\mathbf{1}_d = B^\top(A^\top\mathbf{1}_d) = B^\top\mathbf{1}_d = \mathbf{1}_d. \]

A stack of leftover-copy mixers is still a leftover-copy mixer. The product still conserves the stream sum and still leaves equal streams equal.

Allowed to shrink, not to grow

How much can one of these mixers stretch a vector?

The \(1\)-norm of a matrix is its largest absolute column sum. The \(\infty\)-norm is its largest absolute row sum. For any \(B\) in \(\mathcal{B}_d\) both of those are exactly 1, because every row sum and every column sum is already 1. The spectral norm then obeys

\[ \|B\|_1 = \|B\|_\infty = 1, \qquad \|B\|_2 \le \sqrt{\|B\|_1\|B\|_\infty} = 1. \]

So \(\|Bv\|_2 \le \|v\|_2\). The Birkhoff writing gives the same bound another way: a permutation matrix only reorders, so it preserves Euclidean length, and a convex average of such maps cannot have operator norm bigger than 1.

The inequality can be strict. Let \(J_d\) be the all-ones table and \(U = J_d/d\) the uniform averager. Every row and column of \(U\) is \(1/d\), so \(U\) is doubly stochastic. Any vector whose entries add to zero is sent to zero in one step. Calling this “norm preservation” would be wrong.

The honest general property is non-expansion: lengths do not grow, the mean mode is kept exactly, and disagreement among streams is allowed to shrink, even to nothing.

Unconstrained leftover-copy mixers do not promise this. Chapter 7 multiplied them across depth and watched directions explode or vanish. Double stochasticity is the bookkeeping rule that takes explosion off the table for this one product. It does not take vanishing off the table.

The leftover-copy mixer

The Hyper-Connections update is

\[ x_{l+1} = H_l^{\mathrm{res}} x_l + (H_l^{\mathrm{post}})^\top F(H_l^{\mathrm{pre}} x_l, W_l), \]

with \(x_l\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m\times n}\), a \(d\times d\) leftover-copy mixer \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\), read and write gates \(H_l^{\mathrm{pre}}, H_l^{\mathrm{post}}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times d}\), and a branch output \(F(\cdot)\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}\). The first term reroutes what is already there. The second writes something new.

mHC aims to put each leftover-copy mixer in the Birkhoff polytope,

\[ H_l^{\mathrm{res}} \in \mathcal{B}_d, \]

by a finite Sinkhorn cleanup of learned, input-dependent coefficients [R002]. Chapter 9 will look at why finite iterations and finite precision make the implemented sums approximate rather than symbolic.

On the persistence-only path, each layer then redistributes stream state by convex averaging. The product stays doubly stochastic, keeps the common-stream mode, keeps the stream mean at every token-feature coordinate, and has spectral norm at most one. The transpose used on the way back is also doubly stochastic, so it obeys the same non-expansion bound. This matrix product, by itself, cannot have gain greater than one.

Xie et al. report that, in their 27B-model experiments, mHC avoided the loss and gradient-norm instability they saw in unconstrained Hyper-Connections, and that propagation-gain deviations were much smaller [R002]. Those are paper-specific observations. They are not a theorem that every mHC-like model trains stably.

The later go-mHC construction also aims at doubly stochastic leftover-copy routing, but it gets there with an orthogonal block construction rather than finite Sinkhorn steps. The conservation and non-expansion facts in this chapter depend on the resulting matrix sitting in \(\mathcal{B}_d\), not on how the numbers were born. How much of the polytope a finite-parameter family can cover is a separate question.

Recipes that cheat

Use only row honesty, and \(R\) above is legal. For \(v = (9, 3, 0)^\top\),

\[ Rv = (9, 9, 9)^\top, \]

and the total jumps from 12 to 27. Row honesty still fixes a constant input, because \(R\mathbf{1}_3 = \mathbf{1}_3\). It does not conserve total mass.

Use only column honesty. Totals are conserved, but output rows need not be averages. The table

\[ C = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix} \]

has unit column sums, yet \(Cv = (12, 0, 0)^\top\). All the mass piles into one stream, and \(C\mathbf{1}_3 = (3, 0, 0)^\top\): equal streams are not stationary.

Drop nonnegativity and the sums can still equal one while cancellation hides large coefficients. For

\[ A_t = \begin{pmatrix} 1+t & -t \\ -t & 1+t \end{pmatrix}, \]

every row and every column sums to one, but the eigenvalue along \((1, -1)^\top\) is \(1+2t\). Take \(t > 0\) and the mixer amplifies. The inequalities in the definition are doing real work.

What we did not prove

Double stochasticity of \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\) gives nonnegative unit row and column sums, a stationary all-ones stream mode, conservation of the stream sum, closure under products, and non-expansion of the persistence-only linear map in the usual \(1\), \(2\), and \(\infty\) norms. It does not give any of the following.

  • Identity routing. A permutation can swap streams. An interior point can blend them. Only \(I_d\) leaves every stream separately unchanged.
  • Orthogonality or exact norm preservation. \(J_d/d\) is doubly stochastic and rank one. It erases all stream disagreement.
  • Invertibility or a lower singular-value bound. Gradients and signals in non-mean directions may contract to zero. The constraint blocks explosion from this mixer. It does not block every form of vanishing.
  • Unit-modulus eigenvalues. All eigenvalues satisfy \(|\lambda|\le 1\), and \(1\) is present, but other eigenvalues can sit strictly inside the unit disk or be zero.
  • Convergence to uniform mixing. A permutation can cycle forever. A changing product of mixers need not behave like repeated powers of one primitive Markov matrix.
  • Preservation of each stream, token, feature, variance, covariance, or information content. It preserves one linear quantity — the sum across streams — for each fixed token-feature coordinate. Averaging may discard recoverable distinctions.
  • Stability of the full nonlinear network or its full Jacobian. The branch \(F\), read and write gates, normalization, additive injections, parameter updates, numerical errors, and cross-layer interactions remain. Their gains are not bounded merely because \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\in\mathcal{B}_d\).
  • Exact constraint satisfaction by an approximate parameterization. Finite Sinkhorn steps and finite precision can leave row or column residuals. That is an implementation issue, not a revision of the definition.
  • Good accuracy, optimal routing, stream specialization, or successful optimization. Those are empirical outcomes, not consequences of sitting in a polytope.

“The spectral norm is at most one” is a statement about a particular linear routing term. Replacing it with “the model is stable” drops most of the model from the sentence.

Ways to get this wrong

  • Checking rows and forgetting columns. A softmax over each row gives a row-stochastic table, not generally a doubly stochastic one.
  • Checking sums and forgetting signs. Unit row and column sums without nonnegativity do not define \(\mathcal{B}_d\) and do not give convex averaging.
  • Mixing up axes. \(H^{\mathrm{res}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\) acts on streams in \(x\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m\times n}\), not on the \(m\) tokens.
  • Treating conservation as per-stream preservation. The total across streams is fixed. Individual stream values can change a lot.
  • Calling every interior mixer a probability transition without saying which way the recipes face. Row-vector and column-vector Markov conventions transpose one another.
  • Calling the whole Birkhoff polytope a smooth manifold. It is a convex polytope with faces, edges, and corners. Its relative interior is smooth inside its affine hull. Its boundary is not. “Manifold-constrained” is the architecture’s name, not permission to sand off the corners.
  • Assuming the Birkhoff decomposition is unique. The corners are fixed. Interior points may have several convex writings.

Try it

1. Check both books

Is

\[ A = \begin{pmatrix} 0.7 & 0.3 \\ 0.4 & 0.6 \end{pmatrix} \]

row-stochastic, column-stochastic, both, or neither?

Answer

All entries are nonnegative and both rows sum to \(1\), so \(A\) is row-stochastic. Its column sums are \(1.1\) and \(0.9\), so it is not column-stochastic and therefore not doubly stochastic.

2. Pour a different column

Using this chapter’s \(3\times 3\) table \(B\), route \(v = (2, -1, 5)^\top\). Check that the total is conserved and that each output sits between the smallest and largest inputs.

Answer

\[ Bv = (1.7,\ 2.0,\ 2.3)^\top. \]

Both totals equal \(6\). Each output lies in the input range \([-1, 5]\). Negative state values are allowed; nonnegativity constrains the routing weights.

3. Products stay inside

Let \(A, B \in \mathcal{B}_d\). Show that \(AB\) has nonnegative entries and unit row and column sums.

Answer

Each \((AB)_{ij} = \sum_k A_{ik} B_{kj} \ge 0\). Also \((AB)\mathbf{1} = A(B\mathbf{1}) = A\mathbf{1} = \mathbf{1}\), and \((AB)^\top\mathbf{1} = B^\top(A^\top\mathbf{1}) = \mathbf{1}\). Hence \(AB \in \mathcal{B}_d\).

4. A mixer that erases disagreement

Let \(U = J_3/3\), the \(3\times 3\) table with every entry \(1/3\). Show that \(U\) is doubly stochastic but not orthogonal. What happens to \(w = (1, -1, 0)^\top\)?

Answer

Every row and column sums to one, so \(U \in \mathcal{B}_3\). But \(Uw = 0\), even though \(w \ne 0\); therefore \(U\) is singular and cannot be orthogonal. This demonstrates that double stochasticity does not preserve all norms or prevent vanishing disagreement modes.

5. The two-cup picture

Solve the row- and column-sum equations for a general \(2\times 2\) doubly stochastic table. Name the two corners.

Answer

Every such matrix has the form

\[ B(a) = \begin{pmatrix} a & 1-a \\ 1-a & a \end{pmatrix}, \qquad 0 \le a \le 1. \]

Thus \(\mathcal{B}_2\) is a line segment. Its endpoints are the swap matrix at \(a = 0\) and the identity at \(a = 1\), exactly the two \(2\times 2\) permutation matrices.

6. The whole update, not one term

Assume \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}} \in \mathcal{B}_d\). Does it follow that \(\|x_{l+1}\|_2 \le \|x_l\|_2\) for the full Hyper-Connections update? Why or why not?

Answer

No. The bound applies to \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}} x_l\). The additive branch \((H_l^{\mathrm{post}})^\top F(H_l^{\mathrm{pre}} x_l, W_l)\) can increase the norm, and its Jacobian also contributes during backpropagation. A constraint on one term is not a bound on their sum.

Check yourself

Look at a candidate \(d\times d\) leftover-copy mixer and ask:

  • Are all entries nonnegative?
  • Do the rows sum to one? Do the columns?
  • Which axis of \(x \in \mathbb{R}^{d\times m\times n}\) does it mix?
  • Which quantity is exactly conserved?
  • Why is \(\mathbf{1}_d\) stationary?
  • Why do products stay legal and non-expansive?
  • Can the mixer still contract, erase, swap, or cycle stream directions?

Doubly stochastic means convex redistribution, stream-sum conservation, and bounded linear gain. It does not mean the whole network is stable.

A network does not emit a point of \(\mathcal{B}_d\) by accident. Raw learned numbers can be negative and can have any row or column total. Next we take those numbers and alternately fix the rows and the columns — the Sinkhorn procedure — and ask how close a finite number of passes gets us to the polytope.

Sources

  • [R002] Z. Xie et al., “mHC: Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections,” arXiv:2512.24880v2, 2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.24880
  • [R019] G. Birkhoff, “Tres observaciones sobre el álgebra lineal,” Universidad Nacional de Tucumán, Revista, Serie A 5 (1946), 147–151, MR0020547. https://cir.nii.ac.jp/crid/1570572699525842816?lang=en
  • [R020] J. von Neumann, “A Certain Zero-Sum Two-Person Game Equivalent to the Optimal Assignment Problem,” in Contributions to the Theory of Games II, Princeton University Press, 1953, pp. 5–12. https://doi.org/10.1515/9781400881970-002
  • [R021] R. Sinkhorn and P. Knopp, “Concerning Nonnegative Matrices and Doubly Stochastic Matrices,” Pacific Journal of Mathematics 21(2), 1967, 343–348. https://doi.org/10.2140/pjm.1967.21.343
  • [R023] A. W. Marshall, I. Olkin, and B. C. Arnold, Inequalities: Theory of Majorization and Its Applications, 2nd ed., Springer, 2011. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-0-387-68276-1

Chapter 9 — Rows, then columns, then again

You have a small table of mixing weights. You want every row to add to one, so each destination is a complete recipe. You also want every column to add to one, so no source is counted twice.

Those two wishes fight. Divide each row by its sum and the columns go crooked. Divide each column by its sum and the rows go crooked.

The fix is stubborn, not clever. Make the rows sum to one, then the columns, then repeat. Or start with the columns. Either way you are bargaining, not solving both sums at once.

Chapter 8 asked for both families of sums together: a doubly stochastic router. This chapter is the cheapest chase after that table, one repair at a time, and then an honest leftover: how much is still wrong when you stop.

Start with scores, not weights

The model does not hand us a polite table. It hands us unconstrained numbers, one for each source-to-destination pair. Call those raw scores logits. The name is worth keeping because it is what the network actually emits, before anyone asks about sums.

Here is a \(2\times 2\) toy:

\[ Z = \begin{pmatrix} \log 1 & \log 2\\ \log 3 & \log 4 \end{pmatrix}. \]

We need every entry positive. Then later divisions never hit zero, and every weight can be read as a share. Exponentiate each entry. Do not take a matrix exponential. A matrix exponential would mix powers of \(Z\). We only want positivity:

\[ M^{(0)}=\exp(Z)= \begin{pmatrix} 1&2\\ 3&4 \end{pmatrix}. \]

Read entry \((i,j)\) as: how strongly source stream \(j\) contributes to destination stream \(i\). A row is one destination’s recipe. A column is one source’s outgoing budget.

The mHC paper writes columns first, then rows. We will follow that order so the leftovers match their numbers. The fight is the same if you swap the order. The finite table is not.

Fix the columns, ruin the rows
