The column sums of \(M^{(0)}\) are \((4,6)\). Divide each column by its own sum:

\[ \begin{pmatrix} 1/4&2/6\\ 3/4&4/6 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 0.250000&0.333333\\ 0.750000&0.666667 \end{pmatrix}. \]

Those columns now add to one. The row sums are \(7/12\) and \(17/12\). Divide each row by its sum:

\[ M^{(1)}= \begin{pmatrix} 3/7&4/7\\ 9/17&8/17 \end{pmatrix} \approx \begin{pmatrix} 0.428571&0.571429\\ 0.529412&0.470588 \end{pmatrix}. \]

The row sums are exactly \((1,1)\). The column sums are not:

\[ (0.957983,\;1.042017). \]

The first repair did its job. The second undid part of it. That leftover is the whole point of the chapter.

Score it by looking at every row sum and every column sum, taking the distance from one, and keeping the worst. That worst leftover is the constraint error

\[ \varepsilon_\infty(D)= \max\left\{ \max_i\left|\sum_j D_{ij}-1\right|, \max_j\left|\sum_i D_{ij}-1\right| \right\}. \]

The name is just a ruler: one number that refuses to ignore whichever side you repaired last. Here

\[ \varepsilon_\infty(M^{(1)})=0.0420168. \]

If you only checked the rows you would report zero and miss the unfinished work.

Do it again

Take \(M^{(1)}\). Repair the columns, then the rows:

\[ \begin{pmatrix} 0.447368&0.548387\\ 0.552632&0.451613 \end{pmatrix}, \qquad M^{(2)}\approx \begin{pmatrix} 0.449275&0.550725\\ 0.550296&0.449704 \end{pmatrix}. \]

Rows are again \((1,1)\). Columns are now

\[ (0.99957122,\;1.00042878). \]

So

\[ \varepsilon_\infty(M^{(2)})\approx 4.2878\times 10^{-4}. \]

Two passes are much better than one for this matrix. “Much better” is still not “done.”

The name of the loop

That stubborn alternation has a name. Sinkhorn — Sinkhorn–Knopp, in the form we use — is exactly this procedure: start from a positive table and keep repairing columns, then rows, until you stop.

The name is worth keeping because the theorem people quote is about the infinite run, and a training kernel is a finite one. Same last name. Different objects.

Write the two repairs as maps. For a positive matrix \(A\),

\[ [\mathcal T_c(A)]_{ij} =\frac{A_{ij}}{\sum_{k=1}^{d}A_{kj}}, \qquad [\mathcal T_r(A)]_{ij} =\frac{A_{ij}}{\sum_{k=1}^{d}A_{ik}}. \]

Here \(i\) picks a row, \(j\) a column, \(d\) is the number of streams, and \(k\) is the index being summed away. One full iteration, in the paper’s order, is

\[ M^{(t)}=\mathcal T_r\!\left(\mathcal T_c(M^{(t-1)})\right). \]

In exact arithmetic every completed iteration ends with row sums exactly one. Its column sums need not be one. Reverse the order and you reverse which side is exact at the end of a step. You never repair both sides in the same instant.

Two people straighten the same picture from opposite sides. Under the right conditions the adjustments shrink toward agreement. Stop after either person acts and their side looks better.

One more pass, and the table it is chasing

One more pair of repairs:

\[ \begin{pmatrix} 0.449468&0.550489\\ 0.550532&0.449511 \end{pmatrix}, \qquad M^{(3)}\approx \begin{pmatrix} 0.449488&0.550512\\ 0.550508&0.449492 \end{pmatrix}. \]

Column sums about

\[ (0.99999562,\;1.00000438), \]

so

\[ \varepsilon_\infty(M^{(3)})\approx 4.3758\times 10^{-6}. \]

For this \(2\times 2\) example the table the loop is chasing is

\[ M^{(\infty)}= \begin{pmatrix} a&1-a\\ 1-a&a \end{pmatrix}, \qquad a=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{2}+\sqrt{3}}\approx 0.449490. \]

That limit is exactly doubly stochastic as a mathematical object. A stored floating-point approximation is not exact in the real-number sense, even when its displayed decimals look cooperative.

Finite is not exact

For logits \(Z\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\), the finite \(T\)-step map we actually run is

\[ S_T(Z)=M^{(T)}, \quad M^{(0)}=\exp(Z), \quad M^{(t)}=\mathcal T_r(\mathcal T_c(M^{(t-1)})). \]

Elementwise exponentiation makes every entry positive for finite real logits in exact arithmetic. It also keeps every denominator off zero.

For a strictly positive square matrix, alternating row and column scaling converges to a doubly stochastic diagonal scaling of that matrix [R021]; [R022]. More general nonnegative matrices need support conditions. Positivity ducks those edge cases. The theorem is about \(T\to\infty\), not about an arbitrary finite \(T\).

Each repair only rescales rows or columns. In matrix language, the iterates stay of the form \(D_1 M^{(0)} D_2\) with positive diagonal \(D_1\) and \(D_2\). The loop is hunting scale factors that hit the desired sums. That is also why adding one constant to every logit does not change the converged table: it multiplies \(M^{(0)}\) by one positive scalar, and the scalings swallow it.

A fixed budget chooses \(T\) before seeing the matrix. A tolerance-based loop instead stops at the first \(t\) with, for a declared tolerance \(\tau>0\),

\[ \varepsilon_\infty(M^{(t)})\le\tau. \]

Those policies answer different engineering questions:

  • fixed \(T\) gives predictable work and a static differentiation graph;
  • tolerance stopping gives an input-dependent error certificate, up to floating-point evaluation of the certificate;
  • a maximum-iteration cap is still required, because some matrices crawl.

Reserve “exact” for a construction whose algebra enforces both constraints at finite parameter values, apart from floating-point roundoff. Truncated Sinkhorn is approximate in a stronger sense: even exact real arithmetic generally leaves one family of sums off target. A tolerance such as \(10^{-5}\) certifies a bound under the chosen ruler. It does not turn approximation into equality.

A useful test reports three quantities separately:

\[ e_r=\max_i |(D\mathbf{1})_i-1|, \quad e_c=\max_j |(D^\top\mathbf{1})_j-1|, \quad e_-=\max(0,-\min_{ij}D_{ij}). \]

The first two expose order-dependent leftovers. The last catches a negative entry. For an exponential start, negatives should not arise mathematically. Overflow, invalid arithmetic, or a faulty kernel deserves a less philosophical response than “the theorem said it was fine.”

The mHC paper itself reports a small single-layer deviation and a larger deviation in composed mappings when using 20 iterations [R002]. “Converges to a doubly stochastic matrix” and “returns a doubly stochastic matrix after 20 steps” are different sentences. The first is about a limit. The second is generally false in exact arithmetic, except for special inputs, and floating point adds another error source.

Gradients through a stopped loop

For a fixed finite \(T\), \(S_T\) is a composition of smooth operations wherever every denominator is positive. Automatic differentiation can therefore push a loss gradient from \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}=S_T(Z_l)\) back to the logits \(Z_l\) through every exponentiation, sum, and division.

This is the gradient of the truncated algorithm. It is not the gradient of the exact infinite-iteration limit. The two may approach each other as the leftovers shrink. They are not the same object by definition.

Naive reverse-mode autodiff may save the intermediate matrix from all \(T\) iterations, so \(O(Td^2)\) saved values per routed item. Recomputation trades arithmetic for activation memory. The mHC paper reports a custom backward kernel that recomputes Sinkhorn intermediates on chip and walks the full iteration [R002].

Tolerance-based early stopping adds a discrete choice of how many steps to take. Each fixed-count branch is differentiable. The choice of branch is not a smooth function at the stopping boundaries. The original mHC experiments used the fixed value \(T=20\) [R002].

Numbers that explode

Exponentials overflow for large positive logits and underflow for large negative ones. Subtract a common maximum before exponentiating,

\[ M^{(0)}=\exp(Z-\max_{ij}Z_{ij}). \]

That changes only a global positive scale, so it does not change any later normalized matrix in exact arithmetic. Log-domain updates are another standard option when the numbers get ugly [R024]. Neither trick makes a finite loop exact.

Adding a small \(\epsilon\) to every denominator can prevent division by zero, but it also changes the equation: the resulting sum is \(s/(s+\epsilon)\), not one. With finite logits and a stable exponential, denominators should be positive. Production code should diagnose non-finite values rather than quietly redefine the constraint.

The mHC paper’s coefficient pipeline uses mixed precision but lists the final pre, post, and residual mappings in float32, and it implements the Sinkhorn loop in a fused kernel [R002]. That is a report about one optimized implementation, not a theorem that float32 or 20 steps is universally enough.

What it costs, and a loop you can type

Let \(b\) be batch size, \(m\) sequence length, \(d\) the number of leftover residual streams, and \(c\) the feature width. A full residual state has shape

\[ x_l\in\mathbb{R}^{b\times m\times d\times c}. \]

At each routed position the residual router is a \(d\times d\) table

\[ H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}. \]

The two \(d\)-axes are source stream and destination stream. Neither is a token axis. With dynamic routing over a batch and a sequence, the collection of tables is \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\in\mathbb{R}^{b\times m\times d\times d}\).

Suppose \(q=bm\) routed positions each own a \(d\times d\) logit matrix. One column pass and one row pass each touch \(\Theta(qd^2)\) entries. For \(T\) full iterations the normalization costs

\[ \Theta(Tq d^2) \]

arithmetic and memory traffic. It stores \(\Theta(qd^2)\) current values. Naive autodiff can additionally retain \(\Theta(Tqd^2)\) intermediates. Applying each finished router to \(d\) streams of width \(c\) costs \(\Theta(qd^2c)\), separate from the normalization itself.

For the paper’s \(d=4\) and \(T=20\), each matrix is tiny. Tiny kernels are not automatically free: reductions, launches, and repeatedly moving small tensors can dominate their modest flop count. That is why fusion and a custom backward exist. It is not a claim that big-O notation paid the electricity bill.

The following is pedagogical PyTorch, not the paper’s production kernel:

import torch


def finite_sinkhorn(logits: torch.Tensor, steps: int = 20) -> torch.Tensor:
    """Input/output: logits/router [..., d, d] -> [..., d, d]."""
    shifted = logits - logits.amax(dim=(-2, -1), keepdim=True)
    matrix = shifted.exp()
    for _ in range(steps):
        matrix = matrix / matrix.sum(dim=-2, keepdim=True)  # columns
        matrix = matrix / matrix.sum(dim=-1, keepdim=True)  # rows
    return matrix


def constraint_errors(matrix: torch.Tensor):
    """Input [..., d, d]; outputs e_row, e_col, e_negative over last axes."""
    e_row = (matrix.sum(dim=-1) - 1).abs().amax(dim=-1)
    e_col = (matrix.sum(dim=-2) - 1).abs().amax(dim=-1)
    e_negative = (-matrix.amin(dim=(-2, -1))).clamp_min(0)
    return e_row, e_col, e_negative

The leading axes may be \([b,m]\). No explicit inverse is used. A production implementation should define dtype policy, finite-value checks, backward memory strategy, and acceptance tolerances, then benchmark the fused routed layer rather than this loop in isolation.

Where the paper puts the loop

Original mHC first builds unconstrained dynamic-plus-static coordinates \(\widetilde H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\) from the normalized, flattened \(d\)-stream hidden state. It then sets [R002]

\[ H_l^{\mathrm{res}} =\operatorname{SinkhornKnopp}(\widetilde H_l^{\mathrm{res}}). \]

The paper separately maps its pre-routing coordinates through a sigmoid and its post-routing coordinates through twice a sigmoid. Sinkhorn specifically constrains the residual state-to-state router. The routed layer remains

\[ x_{l+1} =H_l^{\mathrm{res}}x_l +(H_l^{\mathrm{post}})^\top F(H_l^{\mathrm{pre}}x_l,W_l). \]

For one position, \(x_l\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times c}\), \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}x_l\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times c}\), \(H_l^{\mathrm{pre}}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times d}\), and \(H_l^{\mathrm{post}}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times d}\). The paper’s parameterization derives mappings from the flattened per-position \(dc\)-dimensional state. Its mapping visualization averages matrices over tokens in a selected sequence [R002].

Empirical result. Xie et al. used \(d=4\) for HC and mHC and fixed the Sinkhorn maximum at 20 iterations in their reported 3B, 9B, and 27B experiments [R002]. In the 27B comparison, the paper reports that unconstrained HC had a loss surge around step 12,000 correlated with gradient-norm instability, whereas mHC’s loss and gradient-norm profiles were stable relative to that HC run. It reports a final training-loss reduction of 0.021 for mHC versus its baseline.

Empirical result. On the paper’s eight reported downstream benchmarks for the 27B models, mHC exceeded the baseline on all eight and exceeded HC on seven of eight; MATH was the exception (26.0 for mHC versus 26.4 for HC). These are paper-reported results for its models, data, and evaluation settings — not evidence that mHC dominates every HC configuration.

Empirical result. The paper explicitly observes that 20 iterations only approximate double stochasticity. Its Figure 7 reports slight single-layer backward-gain deviation from one and a maximum composite gain of about 1.6, while HC’s maximum gain was nearly 3000 in the compared analysis [R002]. This supports improved propagation behavior in that experiment. It does not establish exact conservation for finite Sinkhorn or prove stability for all depths and logits.

Empirical result. With its kernel fusion, custom recomputing backward, activation recomputation, and pipeline scheduling, the paper reports 6.7% additional training time for mHC with \(d=4\) in its in-house large-scale setting [R002]. That number measures the whole optimized system in a stated regime. It should not be relabeled as the isolated cost of Sinkhorn, nor assumed for an unfused framework loop.

Interpretation. Finite Sinkhorn gave the paper a practical compromise: residual routers close enough to the Birkhoff constraints to markedly reduce measured gain drift, while retaining differentiability and bounded, predictable work. The evidence is promising recent research, not settled architectural law.

Skip a repair

If we skip positivity, negative routing weights can cancel signals, and alternating sum-normalization no longer supplies a convex-combination reading. Zero patterns can also violate the support conditions needed for matrix scaling.

If we normalize only rows, forward destination mixtures are convex, but source totals need not balance. In backpropagation through \(y=Hx\), the transpose \(H^\top\) appears. Uncontrolled column sums then matter directly to aggregate gradient gain.

If we normalize only columns, the mirror-image problem remains: outgoing source weight is balanced, but each destination’s incoming total is not fixed.

If we omit repeated alternation, the second normalization damages the first constraint and leaves a potentially large leftover. In our example, one full iteration left \(\varepsilon_\infty\approx 0.042\); three left about \(4.38\times 10^{-6}\).

If we omit error measurement, “20 iterations” becomes a ritual rather than evidence. Different logit ranges and dtypes can converge at different rates. A fixed budget is valid, but its observed errors should be characterized in the actual training regime.

Finally, omitting Sinkhorn does not mathematically guarantee failure. It returns us to unconstrained HC, whose router can learn useful mappings but lacks the double-stochastic constraint. The 27B instability is an empirical observation reported for the paper’s comparison, not a theorem that every unconstrained HC run must diverge.

Ways to get this wrong

Calling the elementwise \(\exp(Z)\) a matrix exponential. The matrix exponential mixes powers of \(Z\). Sinkhorn needs entrywise positivity.

  1. Claiming exactness after a fixed number of steps. One final row normalization makes rows exact only in ideal arithmetic. Columns remain approximate in general.
  2. Checking only the last-normalized axis. Always compute both \(e_r\) and \(e_c\).
  3. Hiding the normalization order. Column-then-row and row-then-column have different finite outputs.
  4. Treating tolerance as equality. \(\varepsilon_\infty\le 10^{-5}\) means a bounded violation in one ruler, not membership proved by exact algebra.
  5. Ignoring finite precision. Stabilize exponentiation and inspect non-finite values. An epsilon denominator changes the constraint and should be disclosed.
  6. Stopping gradients through the router. Unless you are deliberately ablating, gradients must walk the finite Sinkhorn operations so the logits can learn.
  7. Quoting 6.7% as a universal overhead. It is the paper’s optimized end-to-end measurement at \(d=4\), not a portable constant.
  8. Attributing every mHC system cost to Sinkhorn. Widened-stream I/O, routing application, saved activations, recomputation, and communication also matter.

Try it

1. One repair damages another

Apply column normalization and then row normalization to

\[ A=\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\1&1\end{pmatrix}. \]

Report the two row sums and two column sums after the full iteration.

Answer

Column normalization gives \(\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}2/3&1/2\\1/3&1/2\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\). Its row sums are \(7/6,5/6\). Row normalization gives \(\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}4/7&3/7\\2/5&3/5\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\). Row sums are \((1,1)\); column sums are \((34/35,36/35)\).

2. Measure the leftover

For

\[ D=\begin{pmatrix}0.51&0.49\\0.48&0.52\end{pmatrix}, \]

compute \(e_r\), \(e_c\), and \(\varepsilon_\infty\).

Answer

Both row sums equal one, so \(e_r=0\). Column sums are \(0.99\) and \(1.01\), so \(e_c=0.01\). Therefore \(\varepsilon_\infty=0.01\).

3. Decide whether to stop

Suppose a tolerance-based implementation uses \(\tau=10^{-4}\). Using the errors from the worked example, at which of the first three iterations may it first stop? Does that make the output exact?

Answer

The errors are approximately \(4.20\times 10^{-2}\), \(4.29\times 10^{-4}\), and \(4.38\times 10^{-6}\). The first acceptable iteration is 3. It satisfies the selected tolerance but is not exactly doubly stochastic.

4. Count the entries

Let \(b=8\), \(m=2048\), \(d=4\), and \(T=20\). What is the shape of a per-position logit tensor? How many scalar matrix entries are processed per normalization pass, and what is the asymptotic cost of all full iterations?

Answer

The logits have shape \([8,2048,4,4]\). There are \(8\cdot 2048\cdot 16=262{,}144\) entries per row or column pass. There are two passes per full iteration. The asymptotic normalization cost is \(\Theta(Tbmd^2)\); counting entry visits in this simple model gives \(2\cdot 20\cdot 262{,}144=10{,}485{,}760\), excluding reductions’ implementation details and backward work.

5. Differentiate one row

Let one row be \(u=(u_1,u_2)\) with \(u_i>0\), and let \(r_1=u_1/(u_1+u_2)\). Compute \(\partial r_1/\partial u_1\) and \(\partial r_1/\partial u_2\). What numerical danger appears when the denominator is tiny?

Answer

Writing \(s=u_1+u_2\), \(\partial r_1/\partial u_1=u_2/s^2\) and \(\partial r_1/\partial u_2=-u_1/s^2\). A tiny \(s\) can make derivatives large and divisions inaccurate. Stable exponentiation or log-domain updates address the scale problem more honestly than silently declaring the denominator to be \(s+\epsilon\).

Check yourself

  1. What is Sinkhorn, in this chapter?
    Start from a positive table and alternately repair column sums and row sums. The name comes after the procedure, not before it.

  2. After a finished column-then-row step, which sums are exact?
    The rows, in exact arithmetic. The columns generally are not.

  3. What must a leftover report include?
    Both \(e_r\) and \(e_c\), and a check that no entry went negative.

  4. Is \(T=20\) doubly stochastic?
    No. It is a finite approximation. The infinite limit is the doubly stochastic object. The mHC paper itself reports leftover gain after 20 steps.

  5. What did the 27B comparison actually show?
    In that run, unconstrained HC surged around step 12,000; mHC did not, and finished 0.021 lower in training loss. Downstream, mHC beat the baseline on eight of eight reported tasks and beat HC on seven of eight. That is paper-reported evidence, not a theorem.

  6. Does the 6.7% figure measure Sinkhorn alone?
    No. It is the paper’s optimized end-to-end extra training time at \(d=4\).

Next we ask whether we can build a router that is already fair by algebra, with no leftover error to bargain down. That route starts from permutation matrices. Numerical approximation leaves the room. Combinatorics is waiting in the corridor.

Sources

  • [R002] Z. Xie et al., “mHC: Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections,” arXiv:2512.24880v2, 2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.24880
  • [R021] R. Sinkhorn and P. Knopp, “Concerning Nonnegative Matrices and Doubly Stochastic Matrices,” Pacific Journal of Mathematics 21(2), 343–348, 1967. https://doi.org/10.2140/pjm.1967.21.343
  • [R022] P. A. Knight, “The Sinkhorn–Knopp Algorithm: Convergence and Applications,” SIAM Journal on Matrix Analysis and Applications 30(1), 261–275, 2008. https://doi.org/10.1137/060659624
  • [R024] G. Peyré and M. Cuturi, “Computational Optimal Transport,” Foundations and Trends in Machine Learning 11(5–6), 355–607, 2019; arXiv:1803.00567. https://doi.org/10.1561/2200000073
  • [R001] D. Zhu et al., “Hyper-Connections,” arXiv:2409.19606, 2024. https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.19606

Chapter 10 — Mixing a few seat shuffles

Three people walk into a room with three labeled chairs. A shuffle is a complete reassignment: Alice sits where Bob sat, Bob where Carol sat, Carol where Alice sat. Nobody gets two chairs. No chair is left empty.

That is already a wiring diagram. Each destination takes exactly one source, and every source is used once.

Now mix two shuffles. With weight \(0.7\), leave everyone put. With weight \(0.3\), swap the first two chairs. Alice’s new seat is seven-tenths her old one and three-tenths Bob’s. The bookkeeping still balances, because the same two numbers scale the whole seating chart, not one chair at a time.

Chapter 9 repaired a table by alternately fixing its rows and columns. After finitely many repairs, one side can still be wrong. This chapter writes the table so that every output is already balanced.

Two chairs, two shuffles

Two people, two chairs. There are only two seating charts: leave them put, or swap.

Write a \(1\) where an output chair reads an input chair, and a \(0\) everywhere else:

\[ P_{\mathrm{id}}= \begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}, \qquad P_{\mathrm{swap}}= \begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}. \]

Each of those matrices has one \(1\) per row and one \(1\) per column. Mix them with a weight \(a\) between \(0\) and \(1\):

\[ H=aP_{\mathrm{id}}+(1-a)P_{\mathrm{swap}} = \begin{pmatrix}a&1-a\\1-a&a\end{pmatrix}. \]

Every entry is nonnegative. Each row adds to \(1\). Each column adds to \(1\). Every nonnegative \(2\times 2\) table with those sums has exactly this form.

That matrix of zeros and a single \(1\) in each row and column is a permutation matrix. The name is worth keeping because it is a complete seating chart, not a bag of independent wires. One number will later scale the whole chart.

Three chairs by hand

Take three streams and three seating charts. The same convention: \(P_{ij}=1\) means output stream \(i\) reads input stream \(j\).

\[ P_1= \begin{pmatrix} 1&0&0\\0&1&0\\0&0&1 \end{pmatrix},\quad P_2= \begin{pmatrix} 0&1&0\\0&0&1\\1&0&0 \end{pmatrix},\quad P_3= \begin{pmatrix} 0&0&1\\1&0&0\\0&1&0 \end{pmatrix}. \]

\(P_1\) leaves everyone put. \(P_2\) is the cycle “1 reads 2, 2 reads 3, 3 reads 1.” \(P_3\) cycles the other way.

Give the three charts the weights

\[ a=(0.5,0.3,0.2)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}, \qquad a_k\ge0, \qquad \sum_{k=1}^{3}a_k=1. \]

The mix is

\[ H=0.5P_1+0.3P_2+0.2P_3 = \begin{pmatrix} 0.5&0.3&0.2\\ 0.2&0.5&0.3\\ 0.3&0.2&0.5 \end{pmatrix} \in\mathbb{R}^{3\times3}. \]

Every entry is nonnegative. The row sums and the column sums are all one:

\[ H\mathbf 1_3= \begin{pmatrix}1\\1\\1\end{pmatrix}, \qquad H^\top\mathbf 1_3= \begin{pmatrix}1\\1\\1\end{pmatrix}. \]

So \(H\) sits in \(\mathcal B_3\), the set of all \(3\times 3\) nonnegative matrices with those sums. Chapter 8 called that set the Birkhoff polytope. We will keep the name; the new fact is that we reached a point inside it by mixing seating charts, not by repairing a table.

Now hide batch, token, and feature axes and give each stream one number:

\[ x=(10,20,40)^\top\in\mathbb{R}^{3}. \]

Then

\[ Hx= \begin{pmatrix} 19\\24\\27 \end{pmatrix}. \]

The total is still \(19+24+27=70=10+20+40\). Column sums keep the total. Row sums say something else: if every stream already holds the same value, \(x=q\mathbf 1_3\), the mix leaves that constant alone.

A weighted average of seating charts with nonnegative weights that add to one is a convex mixture. The name is worth keeping because the same two rules — nonnegative, add to one — are what make the output balanced. One coefficient still belongs to a whole chart, not to a single chair.

From chairs to leftover copies

The chairs are the leftover copies of a sentence table that earlier chapters called residual streams. Let \(b\) be how many sentences sit in a batch, \(m\) the number of tokens, \(d\) the number of those leftover copies, and \(c\) the feature width. A hidden state has shape

\[ X_l\in\mathbb{R}^{b\times d\times m\times c}. \]

A residual router acts only on the stream axis. It does not shuffle the batch, the tokens, or the features. When the router can change at each token, write

\[ H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\in\mathbb{R}^{b\times m\times d\times d}. \]

For batch index \(\beta\), output stream \(i\), token \(t\), and feature \(r\), the routed state is

\[ \widetilde X_{l,\beta,i,t,r} = \sum_{j=1}^{d} H^{\mathrm{res}}_{l,\beta,t,i,j} X_{l,\beta,j,t,r}. \]

The paper’s language-model experiments look at those token-level residual matrices [R003]. The seating-chart construction itself does not care how often a model writes a new router. The same mix works for one matrix or for a stack of them.

One 1 in each row and column

A permutation of \(\{1,\ldots,d\}\) is a one-to-one reassignment of those labels: a seating chart. Call it \(\sigma\). Its matrix \(P_\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{d\times d}\) is

\[ (P_\sigma)_{ij}= \begin{cases} 1,&j=\sigma(i),\\ 0,&\text{otherwise}. \end{cases} \]

Each row contains one \(1\) because each output chooses one input. Each column contains one \(1\) because no input is chosen twice. Therefore

\[ P_\sigma\mathbf 1_d=\mathbf 1_d, \qquad P_\sigma^\top\mathbf 1_d=\mathbf 1_d, \qquad P_\sigma\ge0. \]

Every permutation matrix is already balanced. Some texts flip the convention and put a \(1\) at \(i=\sigma(j)\). Either rule is fine. Changing it halfway through a calculation is not.

Mixing always balances

Take any list of permutation matrices and any nonnegative weights that add to one. The mix

\[ B=\sum_k a_k P_k \]

cannot go negative, because neither the weights nor the charts do. Linearity does the rest:

\[ B\mathbf 1_d = \sum_k a_k P_k\mathbf 1_d = \left(\sum_k a_k\right)\mathbf 1_d = \mathbf 1_d, \]

and the same argument on the transpose gives \(B^\top\mathbf 1_d=\mathbf 1_d\).

That is the exactness argument. Every convex mixture of seating charts is doubly stochastic. The argument never asked how long the list was. One chart is already enough. A short list is enough. The full list is enough.

“Exact” describes the algebra. Floating-point additions still round. The construction does not promise a bit-for-bit row sum of 1.0 on every device.

Every balanced table is some mix

The other direction is older than any language model. Birkhoff and von Neumann proved that a matrix \(B\in\mathbb R^{d\times d}\) is doubly stochastic if and only if it is a convex mixture of \(d\times d\) permutation matrices [R019]; [R020]. If \(P_1,\ldots,P_{d!}\) list every seating chart, then

\[ B\in\mathcal B_d \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad B=\sum_{k=1}^{d!}a_kP_k \]

for some \(a\in\mathbb R^{d!}\) with

\[ a_k\ge0, \qquad \sum_{k=1}^{d!}a_k=1. \]

In geometry: the permutation matrices are the corners of the Birkhoff polytope, and the polytope is their convex hull,

\[ \mathcal B_d=\operatorname{conv}\{P_1,\ldots,P_{d!}\}. \]

The easy half is the last section. Here is the other half, as a peeling argument.

Start with \(B\in\mathcal B_d\). Draw a bipartite graph: output chairs on the left, input chairs on the right, and an edge \((i,j)\) whenever \(B_{ij}>0\). Any set \(S\) of outputs currently sends total mass \(|S|\). Each neighboring input can take total mass at most \(1\), so \(S\) must touch at least \(|S|\) inputs. Hall’s matching theorem then supplies a perfect matching sitting on the positive entries. That matching is a permutation matrix \(P\).

Let \(\varepsilon\) be the smallest \(B_{ij}\) on that matching. Then \(B-\varepsilon P\) is still nonnegative, and every row and column now sums to \(1-\varepsilon\). If \(\varepsilon<1\), renormalize the remainder:

\[ B'=\frac{B-\varepsilon P}{1-\varepsilon}\in\mathcal B_d, \qquad B=\varepsilon P+(1-\varepsilon)B'. \]

At least one positive entry becomes zero. Repeat. The process stops after finitely many steps and writes \(B\) as a convex mixture. The writing is not unique. Interior points usually have many writings.

That theorem is the Birkhoff–von Neumann theorem. The name is worth keeping because it is two claims glued together: every mix is balanced, and every balanced table is some mix. The first claim is cheap. The second claim, through this explicit list, is not.

A short mix is enough for one table

A \(d\times d\) table has \(d^2\) entries. The row-sum and column-sum rules are not independent: if you know every row sum and all but one column sum, the last column sum is forced. That is \(2d-1\) independent equations, so the balanced tables live in a flat of dimension \((d-1)^2\).

Carathéodory’s theorem then says any one point in that flat is a mix of at most

\[ (d-1)^2+1=d^2-2d+2 \]

corners [R025]. So a single target table never needs the whole list.

That existence result does not hand you one fixed short list that can represent every table. Different targets may need different corners. A router that must move from target to target either keeps a rich fixed list or solves a discrete selection problem as the target changes.

A vote among seating charts

The network still has to pick the weights. Flatten the \(d\) stream vectors of width \(c\) at one token into

\[ \widehat x_l\in\mathbb R^{dc}. \]

Following the paper’s full-list construction [R003], a learned affine map writes one score per seating chart. With normalized features \(\widehat x'_l\in\mathbb R^{dc}\), projection weights \(W_l^{\mathrm{res}}\in\mathbb R^{dc\times d!}\), bias \(b_l^{\mathrm{res}}\in\mathbb R^{d!}\), and a learned scalar gain \(\alpha_l^{\mathrm{res}}\in\mathbb R\),

\[ u_l = \alpha_l^{\mathrm{res}}\widehat x'_lW_l^{\mathrm{res}} +b_l^{\mathrm{res}} \in\mathbb R^{d!}. \]

Turn those scores into positive weights that add to one, then mix:

\[ a_l=\operatorname{softmax}(u_l)\in\mathbb R^{d!}, \qquad H_l^{\mathrm{res}} = \sum_{k=1}^{d!}a_{l,k}P_k \in\mathbb R^{d\times d}. \]

Softmax is that map from raw scores to a convex mixture. Finite scores never quite reach a corner: every chart gets a little weight. An exact permutation is only a limit. The closed-simplex theorem covers the whole polytope. Softmax covers the interior. Exact double stochasticity is not the thing that fails. The slogan “represents every matrix” is, if you say it literally.

For all \(b\times m\) routing sites the scores and weights have shape \(b\times m\times d!\), and the router has shape \(b\times m\times d\times d\). The paper implements the last sum as one matrix multiply: flatten the constant list from shape \(d!\times d\times d\) to \(d!\times d^2\), multiply, then reshape [R003].

The initialization reported in that paper piles weight on the identity chart. Finite biases still leak a little mass onto every other chart. You do not get an exact one-hot vector.

This construction — mix seating charts with a softmax, and keep the rest of the mHC layer — is mHC-lite [R003]. The name is worth keeping because it names a coordinate system, not a new kind of leftover copy. The streams were already there.

The paper is a January 2026 preprint [R003]. The algebra above is a theorem. The training comparisons later in the chapter are recent measurements, not settled consensus.

How many seating charts?

Two chairs: \(2!=2\). Three chairs: \(3!=6\). The first output has \(d\) possible sources, the next has \(d-1\), and so on:

\[ d(d-1)\cdots 2\cdot 1=d!. \]

That product is the factorial. It is just the number of seating charts. It is not a claim that every algorithm for a balanced table must list them all.

The numbers get rude quickly:

\[ 4!=24, \qquad 8!=40{,}320, \qquad 16!=20{,}922{,}789{,}888{,}000 \approx 2.09\times 10^{13}. \]

The projection alone has \((dc)\,d!+d!\) weights and biases per layer. The fixed \(0/1\) list has \(d!\,d^2\) entries. A direct mix costs on the order of \(d!\,d^2\) arithmetic operations per routing site, before anyone pays to produce the scores.

For \(d=4\), the paper’s experimental setting, \(24\) is a small constant [R003]. For \(d=8\), even with \(c=64\), the projection has shape \(512\times 40{,}320\), or \(20{,}643{,}840\) weights before its bias. At \(d=16\), merely writing down the coefficients is already hopeless. Full coverage by listing every chart is not a scalable parameterization.

Keep only a few

The same paper suggests keeping only \(K\) sampled charts when \(d\) gets larger [R003]. Fix a list

\[ \mathcal S=\{P_{s_1},\ldots,P_{s_K}\}, \qquad K\ll d!, \]

produce \(a_l\in\mathbb R^K\) by softmax, and set

\[ H_l^{\mathrm{res}} = \sum_{k=1}^{K}a_{l,k}P_{s_k}. \]

Now \(W_l^{\mathrm{res}}\in\mathbb R^{dc\times K}\), the list has shape \(K\times d\times d\), and the direct mix costs \(O(Kd^2)\) per routing site. Every output is still exactly doubly stochastic. The easy half of the theorem never needed the full list.

The reachable region is only

\[ \operatorname{conv}(\mathcal S)\subseteq\mathcal B_d, \]

and the inclusion is usually strict. \(K\) is a knob for compute, parameters, and coverage. That is the distinction that survives at large \(d\):

Construction Coefficients Doubly stochastic? Birkhoff coverage Scaling
All charts, closed simplex \(d!\) exact full factorial
All charts, finite-logit softmax \(d!\) exact relative interior; full in the closure factorial
Fixed subset \(K\) exact generally only \(\operatorname{conv}(\mathcal S)\) tunable in \(K\)

Call the stored list a permutation bank. The name is worth keeping because the bank is a tensor of whole charts, shape \(K\times d\times d\), not a bag of independent edges. Combining scores of shape \(b\times m\times K\) with that bank produces routers of shape \(b\times m\times d\times d\). No batch, token, or feature axis is being shuffled.

The rest of the layer stays

mHC-lite changes how the persistent-state router \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\) is written. It keeps the surrounding read, branch, and write structure [R003]:

\[ X_{l+1} = H_l^{\mathrm{res}}X_l + (H_l^{\mathrm{post}})^\top F(H_l^{\mathrm{pre}}X_l,W_l). \]

Here \(H_l^{\mathrm{pre}}\) reads from the \(d\) streams, \(F\) computes one branch value at each routing site, and \(H_l^{\mathrm{post}}\) writes that value back. The seating-chart mix governs only the leftover copies that persist. The theorem does not constrain the branch, and it does not invent streams that were not already there.

Yang and Gao report experiments with \(d=4\) and with 6-, 12-, and 24-layer language models on OpenWebText and FineWeb-Edu. Their final-loss table shows mHC-lite broadly comparable to mHC in that setup. Their measured gradient-norm curves fluctuate less than their mHC reimplementation [R003]. Those measurements support the proposal in the tested regimes. They do not establish universal superiority. They do not test factorial-scale stream counts.

The construction trades iterative repair for an overcomplete convex coordinate system. Later chapters will write other exact routers that refuse to list every global chart. That comparison has to be earned, not smuggled in under a large coat.

What breaks if we cheat

If the mixture weights can go negative, row and column sums can still equal one while some routing weights go below zero. The table leaves the Birkhoff polytope.

If the weights do not add to one, then

\[ H\mathbf 1_d= \left(\sum_k a_k\right)\mathbf 1_d, \]

so the all-ones mode is scaled instead of left alone.

If the basis matrices are arbitrary \(0/1\) tables rather than seating charts, a source may be copied or dropped. Column or row conservation fails.

If we drop charts from a fixed bank, exact double stochasticity does not break. Coverage can. A bank that holds only the identity and one swap cannot write a three-cycle.

If we advertise the full \(d!\) bank as scalable, memory breaks first and patience follows. The short bank repairs the scaling only by accepting a smaller polytope.

Ways to get this wrong

  • Treating each \(1\) in a permutation matrix as an independently weighted edge. One coefficient weights the whole chart.
  • Confusing exactness with coverage. A two-chart mix can be exactly balanced and still only reach a line segment inside \(\mathcal B_d\).
  • Calling the decomposition unique. Interior points usually have several writings.
  • Reversing the convention silently. Say whether rows select sources or columns select destinations.
  • Forgetting the factorial router parameters. The constant bank is not the only cost; the dynamic projection emits \(d!\) scores.
  • Claiming softmax emits a permutation exactly. Finite scores give positive weights. A one-hot vector is a limit.
  • Thinking a sparse decomposition solves dynamic scaling. A short writing for each target is not one universal short bank.
  • Equating doubly stochastic with orthogonal. A permutation is orthogonal. An average of permutations generally is not.

Try it

1. Read a seating chart

Let

\[ P=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\0&0&1\\1&0&0\end{pmatrix}, \qquad x=(4,7,9)^\top. \]

Compute \(Px\) and check both kinds of sums for \(P\).

Answer

\(Px=(7,9,4)^\top\). Every row and every column contains one \(1\), so \(P\mathbf 1_3=\mathbf 1_3\) and \(P^\top\mathbf 1_3=\mathbf 1_3\).

2. Unmix a \(2\times 2\) router

Write

\[ B=\begin{pmatrix}0.7&0.3\\0.3&0.7\end{pmatrix} \]

as a convex mixture of the two \(2\times 2\) permutation matrices.

Answer

\[ B=0.7\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix} +0.3\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}. \]

Both coefficients are nonnegative and sum to one.

3. A short list is still balanced

Let \(P_{s_1},\ldots,P_{s_K}\in\mathbb R^{d\times d}\) be any fixed permutation matrices and \(a=\operatorname{softmax}(u)\in\mathbb R^K\). Show that \(H=\sum_k a_k P_{s_k}\) is doubly stochastic. Does the argument need \(K=d!\)?

Answer

Softmax gives \(a_k>0\) and \(\sum_k a_k=1\). Hence \(H\ge 0\), \(H\mathbf 1=\sum_k a_k P_k\mathbf 1=\mathbf 1\), and similarly \(H^\top\mathbf 1=\mathbf 1\). The argument does not need every permutation; \(K=1\) already preserves the constraint.

4. Count the burden

For \(d=8\) and \(c=64\), find the shape and number of entries of \(W_l^{\mathrm{res}}\) in the full-bank construction. How many entries are in the flattened permutation bank?

Answer

Here \(dc=512\) and \(d!=40{,}320\). Thus \(W\) has shape \(512\times 40{,}320\) and \(20{,}643{,}840\) entries. The bank has \(d!\,d^2=40{,}320\times 64=2{,}580{,}480\) entries.

5. Find the missing chart

For \(d=3\), take a bank containing only the identity and the permutation that swaps streams 1 and 2. Show that no mixture in this bank equals the three-cycle \(P_2\) from the worked example.

Answer

Both bank matrices leave stream 3 fixed, so every mixture has entry \(H_{33}=1\). The three-cycle has \((P_2)_{33}=0\). It sits outside this bank’s convex hull, although every matrix inside the hull remains doubly stochastic.

6. Corners and softmax

Why can finite scores not produce the identity matrix exactly when the bank holds every permutation? Can they approach it arbitrarily closely?

Answer

Finite-logit softmax assigns every nonidentity permutation positive weight, so off-diagonal entries receive positive mass. Sending the identity score above all others without bound drives its weight to one and the rest to zero, so the identity is reached in the limit.

Check yourself

  1. What is a permutation matrix doing to the streams?
    A one-to-one rewiring. Each output reads exactly one input. Each input is used once.

  2. Why is a mix of those matrices already balanced?
    Nonnegative weights that add to one, times nonnegative charts that already have row and column sums equal to one, stay nonnegative and keep those sums.

  3. What extra claim does Birkhoff–von Neumann add?
    Every doubly stochastic matrix is some such mix, if the bank holds all \(d!\) charts and the weights may sit on the closed simplex.

  4. Why is listing every chart a bad plan at large \(d\)?
    \(4!=24\) is fine. \(8!=40{,}320\) already inflates the projection. \(16!\approx 2.09\times 10^{13}\) is not a parameterization.

  5. What do you keep, and what do you lose, if you keep only \(K\) charts?
    You keep exact double stochasticity. You generally lose the rest of the Birkhoff polytope.

  6. Given scores of shape \(b\times m\times K\) and a bank of shape \(K\times d\times d\), what comes out?
    A router of shape \(b\times m\times d\times d\). The mix is over streams. Batch, token, and feature axes ride along.

Sources

  • [R019] G. Birkhoff, “Tres observaciones sobre el algebra lineal,” Universidad Nacional de Tucumán, Revista A, vol. 5, pp. 147–151, 1946. MathSciNet MR0020547: https://mathscinet.ams.org/mathscinet-getitem?mr=20547
  • [R020] J. von Neumann, “A Certain Zero-Sum Two-Person Game Equivalent to the Optimal Assignment Problem,” in Contributions to the Theory of Games II, pp. 5–12, 1953. https://doi.org/10.1515/9781400881970-002
  • [R025] R. T. Rockafellar, Convex Analysis, Princeton University Press, 1970. ISBN 978-0-691-01586-6. https://press.princeton.edu/books/paperback/9780691015866/convex-analysis
  • [R002] Z. Xie et al., “mHC: Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections,” arXiv:2512.24880, 2025. https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.24880
  • [R021] R. Sinkhorn and P. Knopp, “Concerning Nonnegative Matrices and Doubly Stochastic Matrices,” Pacific Journal of Mathematics, vol. 21, no. 2, pp. 343–348, 1967. https://doi.org/10.2140/pjm.1967.21.343

Chapter 11 — Small mixers, tiled

You want a mixer for four leftover copies of a sentence, and you only know how to mix two. Do not invent a sixteen-entry spreadsheet and then beg it to behave. Tile the floor.

Take a small two-by-two mixer. Copy it four times. Scale each copy by one number from a second two-by-two mixer, and glue the four copies into one big square. The big square is still a mixer: every entry stays nonnegative, every row still adds to one, every column still adds to one. You never wrote sixteen independent numbers.

That is the whole trick of this chapter. It is cheap. It is exact. It also refuses some mixings a free four-by-four mixer can do, and we will see exactly which jobs it turns down.

Four copies of a tile

Call the first small mixer \(A\) and the second \(B\):

\[ A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}, \qquad B=\begin{pmatrix}e&f\\g&h\end{pmatrix}. \]

Replace every entry of \(A\) by a whole copy of \(B\), scaled by that entry:

\[ \begin{pmatrix}aB&bB\\cB&dB\end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} ae&af&be&bf\\ ag&ah&bg&bh\\ ce&cf&de&df\\ cg&ch&dg&dh \end{pmatrix}. \]

Read the first block. The number \(a\) does not sit alone; it becomes the whole tile \(aB\). The number \(b\) becomes the tile \(bB\) to its right. That is the gluing rule. The output is \(4\times4\), not \(2\times2\). This is not ordinary matrix multiplication. The side length multiplies.

That glued matrix is the Kronecker product \(A\otimes B\). The name is worth keeping because the tile rule is not “multiply \(A\) by \(B\).” It is “pave a bigger floor with scaled copies of \(B\).”

In general, if \(A\) is \(p\times p\) and \(B\) is \(q\times q\), then \(A\otimes B\) is \(pq\times pq\):

\[ A\otimes B= \begin{pmatrix} a_{11}B&\cdots&a_{1p}B\\ \vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\ a_{p1}B&\cdots&a_{pp}B \end{pmatrix}. \]

Two bits instead of one index

The four leftover copies — the four streams — can be labeled by a pair of bits instead of a single integer \(0,1,2,3\):

\[ (0,0),\ (0,1),\ (1,0),\ (1,1). \]

One two-by-two mixer stirs the first bit. The other stirs the second bit. The tiled product does both jobs at once. The rules repeat across the floor. That is why the construction is cheap. It is also why a diagonal shortcut on the floor is illegal: each factor only sees its own coordinate.

Original mHC builds a large mixer by writing a free matrix and then repairing its rows and columns with a finite Sinkhorn loop. The repair is approximate. mHC-lite instead mixes permutation matrices and lands exactly on a mixer whose rows and columns sum to one, but listing every rearrangement of \(d\) streams costs \(d!\) slots. KromHC asks for a middle path: glue small exact mixers into one large exact mixer, and do not pay for every large permutation. [R004]

Choose integers \(i_1,\ldots,i_K\ge 2\) whose product is the stream count \(d\):

\[ d=\prod_{k=1}^{K} i_k. \]

Learn one small mixer per factor,

\[ U_l^k\in\mathbb{R}^{i_k\times i_k},\qquad k=1,\ldots,K, \]

each already a mixer with nonnegative entries and unit row and column sums. The large residual mixer is the tiled product

\[ H_l^{\mathrm{res}} =U_l^K\otimes U_l^{K-1}\otimes\cdots\otimes U_l^1 \in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}. \]

That residual mixer is a KromHC router. The paper parameterizes each small factor as a convex mix of its own permutation matrices. The read and write routers stay in the ordinary mHC style. Only the residual mixer is tiled. [R004]

The paper calls the stream width \(n\). Here that letter is \(d\). Batch size is \(B\), sequence length is \(m\), feature width is \(c\), so the state is

\[ x_l\in\mathbb{R}^{B\times d\times m\times c}. \]

The residual mixer acts only on the second axis. It does not secretly reshuffle tokens.

Two honest tiles, one big square

Take two mixers that already behave:

\[ A=\begin{pmatrix}0.75&0.25\\0.25&0.75\end{pmatrix}, \qquad B=\begin{pmatrix}0.60&0.40\\0.40&0.60\end{pmatrix}. \]

Glue them:

\[ H=A\otimes B =\begin{pmatrix} 0.45&0.30&0.15&0.10\\ 0.30&0.45&0.10&0.15\\ 0.15&0.10&0.45&0.30\\ 0.10&0.15&0.30&0.45 \end{pmatrix}. \]

Check one tile by hand. The upper-left block is \(0.75\,B\):

\[ 0.75\begin{pmatrix}0.60&0.40\\0.40&0.60\end{pmatrix} =\begin{pmatrix}0.45&0.30\\0.30&0.45\end{pmatrix}. \]

Every entry is a product of nonnegative numbers, so every entry is nonnegative. The first row sums to

\[ 0.45+0.30+0.15+0.10=1, \]

and the other rows and columns do the same.

To see the mixer move mass, ignore batch, token, and feature for a moment and keep one number per stream:

\[ x=\begin{pmatrix}8\\4\\0\\0\end{pmatrix}. \]

Then

\[ Hx =\begin{pmatrix}4.8\\4.2\\1.6\\1.4\end{pmatrix}. \]

The total is still twelve: \(8+4=4.8+4.2+1.6+1.4\). Nothing was invented. Nothing was thrown away.

For any one fixed mixer \(H\), those same sixteen coefficients hit every batch item, every token, and every feature of \(x_l\in\mathbb{R}^{B\times 4\times m\times c}\). How a dynamic implementation chooses a different \(H\) at a different place is a separate question. The tiling is about streams.

Honest tiles stay honest

A square matrix \(M\) is doubly stochastic when its entries are nonnegative and every row and every column sums to one:

\[ M\ge0,\qquad M\mathbf{1}_r=\mathbf{1}_r,\qquad M^\top\mathbf{1}_r=\mathbf{1}_r. \]

Here \(\mathbf{1}_r\) is a column of \(r\) ones. You already met this bookkeeping in Chapter 8. The new fact is that tiling preserves it.

If \(A\) is \(p\times p\) and doubly stochastic, and \(B\) is \(q\times q\) and doubly stochastic, then \(A\otimes B\) is doubly stochastic. The same holds for any finite stack of tiles. [R004]; [R018]

The entries of \(A\otimes B\) look like \(a_{ij}b_{rs}\), so they stay nonnegative. The all-ones vector of length \(pq\) itself tiles:

\[ \mathbf{1}_{pq}=\mathbf{1}_p\otimes\mathbf{1}_q. \]

Kronecker products act factor by factor, \((A\otimes B)(u\otimes v)=(Au)\otimes(Bv)\), so

\[ (A\otimes B)\mathbf{1}_{pq} =(A\mathbf{1}_p)\otimes(B\mathbf{1}_q) =\mathbf{1}_p\otimes\mathbf{1}_q =\mathbf{1}_{pq}. \]

The transpose splits the same way, \((A\otimes B)^\top=A^\top\otimes B^\top\), and the column sums follow. Repeat the two-factor argument to cover more factors.

That is exactness in real arithmetic. A floating-point chip can still round. “Exact by construction” means there is no leftover Sinkhorn gap from stopping a repair loop early. It does not mean IEEE arithmetic has resigned.

KromHC makes each small tile exact as well. Let \(P_1,\ldots,P_{i_k!}\) be the permutation matrices of size \(i_k\), and set

\[ a_l^k=\operatorname{softmax}(g_l^k)\in\mathbb{R}^{i_k!}, \qquad U_l^k=\sum_{r=1}^{i_k!}a_l^k(r)P_r. \]

The weights are nonnegative and add to one. Every permutation matrix is doubly stochastic, so each \(U_l^k\) is too, and the tiled product inherits the property. [R004]

Stretch factors multiply

If \(Au=\lambda u\) and \(Bv=\mu v\), the tiled product stretches the tiled vector by the product of the two stretch factors:

\[ (A\otimes B)(u\otimes v) =(Au)\otimes(Bv) =(\lambda u)\otimes(\mu v) =\lambda\mu\,(u\otimes v). \]

So every pair of factor eigenvalues becomes one eigenvalue of the big mixer. Counting algebraic multiplicity, including for matrices that are short of a full set of eigenvectors,

\[ \operatorname{spec}(A\otimes B) =\{\lambda_i(A)\mu_j(B):1\le i\le p,\ 1\le j\le q\}. \]

That set of stretch factors is the spectrum. For more tiles, pick one eigenvalue from each factor and multiply:

\[ \operatorname{spec}\!\left(\bigotimes_{k=1}^{K}U^k\right) =\left\{\prod_{k=1}^{K}\lambda_{j_k}(U^k)\right\}_{j_1,\ldots,j_K}. \]

Every two-by-two doubly stochastic mixer has one free number:

\[ U(t)=\begin{pmatrix}t&1-t\\1-t&t\end{pmatrix}, \qquad 0\le t\le1. \]

The vector \((1,1)^\top\) stretches by \(1\). The vector \((1,-1)^\top\) stretches by \(2t-1\). For the numerical tiles above,

\[ \operatorname{spec}(A)=\{1,0.5\},\qquad \operatorname{spec}(B)=\{1,0.2\}, \]

so

\[ \operatorname{spec}(A\otimes B)=\{1,0.5,0.2,0.1\}. \]

Two uses. First, repeated mixing is predictable: each mode is scaled by a power of its eigenvalue. Second, the limitation is now visible. With only two-by-two doubly stochastic tiles, every eigenvalue is real. The big mixer cannot itself carry the complex conjugate pair that a genuine three-stream rotation needs.

Double stochasticity does bound the spectral norm by one. It does not force every non-stationary eigenvalue to be positive. A tile with \(t<1/2\) has \(2t-1<0\), so that mode flips sign each time it is applied. Stability and expressivity are different questions.

What the floor cannot cycle

At a corner of the Birkhoff polytope the mixer is a permutation: a complete wiring diagram. With two-by-two factors, each corner is either the identity \(I_2\) or the swap

\[ S=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}. \]

On bit-pair labels, a tiled product of these corners flips a fixed subset of bits. Apply it twice and every label is home. So every cycle has length one or two.

In the order \((00,01,10,11)\), the product \(S\otimes S\) sends

\[ 00\leftrightarrow11, \qquad 01\leftrightarrow10. \]

It cannot walk \(00\to01\to10\to00\). It also cannot walk one four-cycle through all four streams. Those missing permutations are perfectly doubly stochastic. They are simply not products of two two-by-two permutations.

If one factor permutation has a cycle of length \(a\) and another has a cycle of length \(b\), the pair returns after \(\operatorname{lcm}(a,b)\) steps. Larger tiles therefore allow longer cycles, and mixed sizes broaden the list. The action is still separable: simultaneous factor moves, not an arbitrary rearrangement of \(d\) streams.

The spectrum says the same thing. A three-cycle has eigenvalues

\[ 1,\quad e^{2\pi i/3},\quad e^{-2\pi i/3}, \]

which cannot appear as products of the real spectra of two-by-two doubly stochastic tiles. That does not say KromHC cannot learn useful routing. It says a cheap choice — many binary tiles — has a particular reach.

How much of the allowed set we cover

The set \(\mathcal{B}_d\) of all \(d\times d\) doubly stochastic matrices has dimension

\[ (d-1)^2. \]

A KromHC family with a fixed factorization \(d=\prod_k i_k\) has at most

\[ \sum_{k=1}^{K}(i_k-1)^2 \]

geometric degrees of freedom, because each factor \(U^k\) lives in \(\mathcal{B}_{i_k}\) and that set has dimension \((i_k-1)^2\). For \(d=4=2\cdot2\) this is \(1+1=2\). The full set \(\mathcal{B}_4\) has dimension \(9\).

The gap is not a failed training run. Softmax logits can be redundant, but they cannot invent a dimension the tiles do not have. A four-by-four Kronecker matrix also has a visible block test: all four two-by-two blocks must be scalar multiples of the same tile. Most doubly stochastic matrices fail that test.

Larger tiles trade savings for coverage. A single factor \(i_1=d\) can, in principle, recover the whole Birkhoff family, but its convex-mixture parameterization again lists \(d!\) permutations. Small tiles are both the bargain and the fence.

What the paper spends

Let the paper’s feature width be \(c\), and flatten the residual input to \(x_l'\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times dc}\). The residual projection matrices are

\[ W_l^{\mathrm{res},k}\in\mathbb{R}^{dc\times i_k!}. \]

Including the unchanged pre and post projections, biases, and scalar gains, KromHC reports the per-layer count [R004]

\[ 2d^2c+(dc+1)\sum_{k=1}^{K}i_k!+2d+3. \]

When every \(i_k=2\) and \(K=\log_2 d\), the factor-router term uses \(\sum_k i_k!=2\log_2 d\) outputs rather than \(d^2\). The common pre/post term \(2d^2c\) remains, so the paper writes the total parameter complexity as \(\mathcal{O}(d^2c)\), against its stated \(\mathcal{O}(d^3c)\) for original mHC and \(\mathcal{O}(dc\,d!)\) for mHC-lite. [R004]

There are two ways to apply the mixer. Materialize \(H\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\) and multiply, which is ordinary dense routing. Or reshape the stream axis into \(i_1\times\cdots\times i_K\) and apply each tile along its own mode. A direct count for the second route is

\[ \mathcal{O}\!\left(Bmc\,d\sum_{k=1}^{K}i_k\right), \]

or \(\mathcal{O}(Bmc\,d\log d)\) for binary tiles, without storing the full \(d\times d\) mixer. That is an algebraic opportunity, not a claim that every released kernel hits the bound. The paper emphasizes ordinary PyTorch ops and parameter count, not a specialized Sinkhorn kernel. [R004]

In the paper’s \(d=4\) language-model experiments, KromHC reported fewer extra learned parameters than mHC and mHC-lite: 240K versus 462K and 609K for six Transformer blocks, and 959K versus 1,844K and 2,433K for twelve blocks. It reported similar training loss and validation bits-per-byte, and a higher CORE score in those configurations. Validation BPB improved as \(d\) ran through \(4,8,16\), and late-training gradient norms were lower in the comparison. [R004] Those are author-reported numbers at two modest scales, not a universal ranking.

Where the tile sits

KromHC drops into the same update you already know:

\[ x_{l+1} =H_l^{\mathrm{res}}x_l +(H_l^{\mathrm{post}})^\top F(H_l^{\mathrm{pre}}x_l,W_l), \]

with shapes

\[ H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},\quad H_l^{\mathrm{pre}},H_l^{\mathrm{post}}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times d},\quad x_l,x_{l+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{B\times d\times m\times c}. \]

Only \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\) is tiled. Applied on the stream axis, it keeps the state shape \(B\times d\times m\times c\). The branch \(F\) still sees one routed tensor of shape \(B\times m\times c\). KromHC does not mean \(d\) evaluations of \(F\).

The later go-mHC paper takes KromHC’s exactness and cheapness as a plus, and uses the spectral reach as a motivating limit. It reports broader spectral coverage from a generalized orthostochastic construction, and also proposes composing that construction with Kronecker tiles. [R005] Useful comparison. Another recent proposal. Not a final ranking.

KromHC chooses a small exact subset that is easy to walk. go-mHC looks for a larger exact subset at a different price. Neither sentence says which model wins tomorrow.

What we lose if we skip a piece

Keep arbitrary learned factors and drop their doubly stochastic parameterization, and the tiled product is no longer guaranteed to be doubly stochastic. One bad tile is enough: a row sum of two in \(A\) multiplies the matching row sums in \(A\otimes B\).

Keep exact factors and drop the tiling, and you need another way to parameterize a full \(d\times d\) doubly stochastic matrix. Finite Sinkhorn returns to an approximation tolerance. Listing every large permutation returns to factorial growth. Other exact parameterizations exist. They bring their own geometry and their own bill.

Pretend the tiling restriction is not there, and architectural reasoning breaks. A target three-cycle can sit inside \(\mathcal{B}_d\) and still be unreachable with binary tiles. More training cannot buy a missing dimension.

Skipping KromHC just because it is restricted would also be a mistake. A model may not need arbitrary stream permutations. The repeated coordinate rule can be a useful bias. The paper’s experiments are evidence that useful language-model routing lives inside this smaller family. [R004]

Ways to get this wrong

  • Calling \(A\otimes B\) an ordinary matrix product. Its shape is \(pq\times pq\), not \(p\times q\), and its entries live in scaled tiles.
  • Proving only that rows sum to one. Double stochasticity also needs nonnegativity and column sums.
  • Hearing “exact” and concluding there is no floating-point error. The construction has zero Sinkhorn-iteration gap in real arithmetic. Evaluation still rounds.
  • Equating exactness with covering every doubly stochastic matrix. KromHC lands exactly inside \(\mathcal{B}_d\). It generally does not fill \(\mathcal{B}_d\).
  • Ignoring factor order. \(A\otimes B\) and \(B\otimes A\) differ by a permutation of coordinates. Under a fixed stream order they are not the same matrix.
  • Claiming KromHC can only ever make two-cycles. That claim is for permutation corners built entirely from two-by-two tiles. Larger tiles allow longer cycles, still with product structure.
  • Quoting \(\mathcal{O}(d^2c)\) as the cost of everything. In the paper that is the dominant parameter statement, including pre and post projections. Runtime depends on whether the large mixer is materialized or applied mode by mode.
  • Routing tokens with the tiles. The tiles mix the residual-stream index. Batch, sequence, and feature stay distinct.

The factors can be expressive one by one. Their tiled product is still a product. Independence makes the construction cheap. It does not invent cross-coordinate dependencies.

Try it

1. Expand and check

Let

\[ A=\begin{pmatrix}0.8&0.2\\0.2&0.8\end{pmatrix},\qquad B=\begin{pmatrix}0.3&0.7\\0.7&0.3\end{pmatrix}. \]

Glue \(A\otimes B\) by the tile rule, then add the first column.

Answer

\[ A\otimes B= \begin{pmatrix} 0.24&0.56&0.06&0.14\\ 0.56&0.24&0.14&0.06\\ 0.06&0.14&0.24&0.56\\ 0.14&0.06&0.56&0.24 \end{pmatrix}. \]

The first column sums to \(0.24+0.56+0.06+0.14=1\).

2. Find the stretch factors

For the same two tiles, what are the four eigenvalues of \(A\otimes B\)?

Answer

A matrix \(U(t)\) has eigenvalues \(1\) and \(2t-1\). Thus \(A\) has \(\{1,0.6\}\), while \(B\) has \(\{1,-0.4\}\). Pairwise products give

\[ \{1,\ 0.6,\ -0.4,\ -0.24\}. \]

The negative modes alternate sign under repeated routing.

3. Test a cycle

Can the four-cycle \(00\to01\to11\to10\to00\) equal \(P\otimes Q\) for two-by-two permutation matrices \(P\) and \(Q\)?

Answer

No. Every \(2\times2\) permutation is \(I_2\) or \(S\), so \((P\otimes Q)^2=P^2\otimes Q^2=I_4\). A four-cycle has order four, not order at most two.

4. Count the room

Compare the dimension of \(\mathcal{B}_8\) with the maximum dimension of the tiled family \(\mathcal{B}_2\otimes\mathcal{B}_2\otimes\mathcal{B}_2\).

Answer

\[ \dim\mathcal{B}_8=(8-1)^2=49. \]

Each \(\mathcal{B}_2\) factor has dimension one, so the product family has dimension at most \(1+1+1=3\). It is therefore a very small structured subset of \(\mathcal{B}_8\).

5. Pick tiles under a budget

For \(d=12\), compare \(12=2\cdot2\cdot3\) with \(12=3\cdot4\) using the residual-logit count \(\sum_k i_k!\). Which is cheaper, and what do you give up?

Answer

For \(2,2,3\), the count is \(2!+2!+3!=2+2+6=10\). For \(3,4\), it is \(3!+4!=6+24=30\). The prime-factor choice is cheaper. The caveat is expressivity: a \(4\times4\) factor can represent coordinated four-state mixing unavailable inside two independent binary factors.

Check yourself

  1. How do you build \(A\otimes B\) from two two-by-two matrices, and what is its shape?
  2. Why is the tiled product of doubly stochastic tiles still doubly stochastic?
  3. If \(A\) has eigenvalues \(1,0.5\) and \(B\) has eigenvalues \(1,0.2\), what is the spectrum of \(A\otimes B\)?
  4. Why can binary permutation tiles not make a three-cycle of streams?
  5. KromHC is exact. Does that mean it covers all of \(\mathcal{B}_d\)?
  6. The paper writes parameter complexity \(\mathcal{O}(d^2c)\). What does that count, and what does it not count?

Short answers: (1) replace each entry of \(A\) by that entry times \(B\); the result is \(4\times4\). (2) entries stay nonnegative products; all-ones tiles as \(\mathbf{1}_p\otimes\mathbf{1}_q\), and each factor already fixes that vector. (3) \(\{1,0.5,0.2,0.1\}\). (4) each binary permutation has order at most two, so the tiled permutation does too; a three-cycle does not. (5) no — exact membership is not exhaustive coverage; for \(d=4=2\cdot2\) you have two degrees of freedom against nine. (6) learned parameters, dominated by the pre/post term \(2d^2c\); runtime depends on whether you materialize \(H\) or apply tiles mode by mode.

KromHC is not “approximate mHC.” It is not free full expressivity either. It is an exact, cheap, structured mixer with an explicit bias: the floor is tiled.

Chapter 12 asks whether we can stay exact without forcing the mixer to factor as tiles.

Sources

  • [R004] W. Zhou, Y. Gu, G. Iacovides, and D. Mandic, “KromHC: Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections with Kronecker-Product Residual Matrices,” arXiv:2601.21579v2, 2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.21579
  • [R005] T. Dandachi and S. Diggs-Galligan, “go-mHC: Direct Parameterization of Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections via Generalized Orthostochastic Matrices,” arXiv:2604.02309v1, 2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02309
  • [R002] Z. Xie et al., “mHC: Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections,” arXiv:2512.24880, 2025. https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.24880
  • [R018] C. F. Van Loan, “The Ubiquitous Kronecker Product,” Journal of Computational and Applied Mathematics 123, 85–100, 2000. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0377-0427(00)00393-9
  • [R019] G. Birkhoff, “Tres observaciones sobre el álgebra lineal,” Universidad Nacional de Tucumán, Revista A 5, 147–151, 1946. https://www.ams.org/mathscinet-getitem?mr=0020547

Chapter 12 — Twenty-eight numbers and a rotation

How do you get a rotation from an unconstrained list of numbers?

A rotation is fussy. Every column has to have length one, and every pair of columns has to sit at right angles. A network layer is not fussy. It will emit any real numbers you ask for, and the optimizer is happier when those numbers have no extra rules bolted on.

Those two habits collide. This chapter starts with a free list and ends with an eight-by-eight matrix that really does rotate. We will not ask the network to satisfy the angle rules by wishing.

Columns that stay at right angles

Take a square table \(Q\) with \(N\) rows and \(N\) columns. Multiply a vector \(v\) by that table. In general the vector comes out longer, shorter, or sheared.

There is a special case. If the columns of \(Q\) are unit length and pairwise at right angles, multiplication only turns \(v\). Length is sacred:

\[ \|Qv\|_2^2 = v^\top Q^\top Q v. \]

The middle product \(Q^\top Q\) has to be the identity \(I_N\), or the lengths will not match. When

\[ Q^\top Q = I_N, \]

we call \(Q\) orthogonal. The name is worth keeping because it is exactly the right-angle condition, written once for the whole table.

An orthogonal matrix can still flip a direction. Rotations are the ones that also keep orientation; we will meet that extra filter after we have a construction.

Asking a network for all \(N^2\) entries of \(Q\), then hoping the angle equations hold, is a bad job. For \(N=8\) that is 64 numbers and a pile of quadratic constraints. We need a list the optimizer may treat as free.

Opposite pairs above the diagonal

Here is a smaller object. Write a square table whose diagonal is zero and whose lower triangle is the upper triangle with every sign flipped:

\[ A=\begin{pmatrix}0&a\\-a&0\end{pmatrix}. \]

One real number \(a\) fills the whole \(2\times 2\) table. The diagonal had no choice: a number that equals its own negative is zero. Swap the two off-diagonal seats and you swap the sign.

That pattern is skew-symmetric. The name is worth keeping because the sign flip is the entire constraint. In symbols, a real square matrix \(A\) is skew-symmetric when

\[ A^\top = -A. \]

A coordinate \(a_{ij}\) couples direction \(i\) to direction \(j\). There is no self-coupling on the diagonal. Reverse the ordered pair and the sign reverses. Skew tables describe infinitesimal twists. They are not probabilities, and they are not leftover copies of a sentence.

Once the entries strictly above the diagonal are chosen, every entry below is forced. So you need one free number per unordered pair of indices \(\{i,j\}\). There are

\[ \binom{N}{2}=\frac{N(N-1)}{2} \]

such pairs.

The working size in this chapter is \(N=8\). That eight is \(4\times 2\): four leftover copies of a sentence table, later cut into blocks of size two. For the count, eight is just the side of the square.

\[ \binom{8}{2}=\frac{8\cdot 7}{2}=28. \]

A projection that wants to emit one of these tables only needs a vector of 28 unconstrained reals,

\[ u_l=(u_1,\ldots,u_{28})\in\mathbb R^{28}, \]

one list per routed instance. The subscript \(l\) is the network layer. If the mixer is allowed to change with the batch or the token, those extra axes sit in front of the 28. We suppress them while we study one instance. Do not quietly glue the batch axis onto the leftover-copy axis.

The vector space of real \(8\times 8\) skew-symmetric matrices is later called \(\mathfrak{so}(8)\). The name does not add a twenty-ninth coordinate.

Fill the upper triangle

Pack the 28 numbers into the strict upper triangle, row by row. Row 1 gets seven, row 2 gets six, and so on:

\[ 7+6+5+4+3+2+1=28. \]

Set every lower entry to the negative of its mirror. The zero diagonal stays zero. That is the whole table:

\[ A_l= \begin{pmatrix} 0&u_1&u_2&u_3&u_4&u_5&u_6&u_7\\ -u_1&0&u_8&u_9&u_{10}&u_{11}&u_{12}&u_{13}\\ -u_2&-u_8&0&u_{14}&u_{15}&u_{16}&u_{17}&u_{18}\\ -u_3&-u_9&-u_{14}&0&u_{19}&u_{20}&u_{21}&u_{22}\\ -u_4&-u_{10}&-u_{15}&-u_{19}&0&u_{23}&u_{24}&u_{25}\\ -u_5&-u_{11}&-u_{16}&-u_{20}&-u_{23}&0&u_{26}&u_{27}\\ -u_6&-u_{12}&-u_{17}&-u_{21}&-u_{24}&-u_{26}&0&u_{28}\\ -u_7&-u_{13}&-u_{18}&-u_{22}&-u_{25}&-u_{27}&-u_{28}&0 \end{pmatrix} \in\mathbb R^{8\times 8}. \]

Transpose the table and every \(u_k\) becomes \(-u_k\), while the zeros stay put. Therefore \(A_l^\top=-A_l\).

The map \(u_l\mapsto A_l\) is linear and one-to-one for this ordering. There is no softmax. Each \(u_k\) may be any real number. A different code base may walk the triangle in a different order. That is fine, as long as packing and unpacking agree.

A two-by-two twist

Before trusting an \(8\times 8\) recipe, finish the \(2\times 2\) table by hand. One coordinate \(a\) still gives

\[ A=\begin{pmatrix}0&a\\-a&0\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb R^{2\times 2}. \]

We want a rotation out of \(A\). Form the identity \(I_2\), subtract \(A\) on the left, add \(A\) on the right, and multiply:

\[ Q=(I_2-A)(I_2+A)^{-1}. \]

The inverse is elementary:

\[ (I_2+A)^{-1} =\frac{1}{1+a^2} \begin{pmatrix}1&-a\\a&1\end{pmatrix}. \]

Multiply through and you get

\[ Q=\frac{1}{1+a^2} \begin{pmatrix} 1-a^2&-2a\\ 2a&1-a^2 \end{pmatrix}. \]

For \(a=1\),

\[ A=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad Q=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}. \]

The columns of \(Q\) have unit length and zero dot product, so \(Q^\top Q=I_2\). Under this sign convention the table rotates vectors by \(+90^\circ\). Its determinant is \(+1\). Its eigenvalues are \(+i\) and \(-i\).

This recipe is the Cayley transform. The name is worth keeping because it is a fixed, parameter-free map: the network never learns the transform, only the free list that feeds it. The go-mHC paper uses this same convention [R005].

In code, solve a linear system. Do not form \((I+A)^{-1}\) as an explicit matrix and then multiply. The inverse is how the mathematics is written, not how the kernel should run.

The rotation you can approach but never land on

Send \(|a|\) to infinity in the \(2\times 2\) formula. Then

\[ Q\longrightarrow -I_2. \]

No finite \(a\) actually produces \(-I_2\). The excluded matrix has eigenvalue \(-1\). You can walk toward that point as close as you like. You cannot sit on it.

The same hole will reappear for \(N=8\). It is not a rounding error. It is a missing chart coordinate.

Why the inverse exists

Back to eight dimensions. We want

\[ Q_l=(I_8-A_l)(I_8+A_l)^{-1}. \]

Could \(I_8+A_l\) fail to invert? Not when \(A_l\) is real and skew-symmetric.

Suppose a real vector \(v\in\mathbb R^8\) satisfies \((I_8+A_l)v=0\). Multiply on the left by \(v^\top\):

\[ 0=v^\top(I_8+A_l)v=\|v\|_2^2+v^\top A_lv. \]

The leftover scalar \(v^\top A_lv\) equals its own transpose, but

\[ (v^\top A_lv)^\top=v^\top A_l^\top v=-v^\top A_lv, \]

so it is zero. Hence \(\|v\|_2^2=0\), which forces \(v=0\). The null space is empty, so \(I_8+A_l\) is invertible. The same argument works for \(I_8-A_l\).

Over \(\mathbb C\), a real skew-symmetric matrix has purely imaginary eigenvalues \(i\theta\). Then \(-1\) cannot be an eigenvalue of \(A_l\), and \(1+i\theta\neq 0\). Same conclusion, different language.

Why the columns stay at right angles

If \(A\in\mathbb R^{N\times N}\) satisfies \(A^\top=-A\), the Cayley table

\[ Q=(I_N-A)(I_N+A)^{-1} \]

satisfies \(Q^\top Q=I_N\).

Compute the transpose, reversing the product:

\[ \begin{aligned} Q^\top &=\left[(I_N+A)^{-1}\right]^\top(I_N-A)^\top\\ &=(I_N+A^\top)^{-1}(I_N-A^\top)\\ &=(I_N-A)^{-1}(I_N+A). \end{aligned} \]

The matrices \(I_N-A\) and \(I_N+A\) commute, because both are polynomials in the same \(A\). Their inverses commute with them too. Therefore

\[ \begin{aligned} Q^\top Q &=(I_N-A)^{-1}(I_N+A)(I_N-A)(I_N+A)^{-1}\\ &=(I_N-A)^{-1}(I_N-A)(I_N+A)(I_N+A)^{-1}\\ &=I_N. \end{aligned} \]

That is exact algebra. In floating point a computed \(Q_l\) will hit the identity only to numerical precision.

Eigenvalues on the circle, except minus one

Let \(i\theta\), with \(\theta\in\mathbb R\), be an eigenvalue of \(A\). On the same complex eigenvector, \(Q\) has eigenvalue

\[ \lambda_Q=\frac{1-i\theta}{1+i\theta}. \]

Its magnitude is

\[ |\lambda_Q|^2 =\frac{1+\theta^2}{1+\theta^2}=1. \]

Cayley sends imaginary-axis eigenvalues of a skew table to unit-circle eigenvalues of an orthogonal table. Yet

\[ \frac{1-i\theta}{1+i\theta}=-1 \]

has no finite solution: it would require \(1=-1\). Only the limit \(|\theta|\to\infty\) reaches \(-1\).

The ordinary Cayley transform therefore lands on those special orthogonal matrices that do not have eigenvalue \(-1\). Conversely, if \(Q\) is a rotation matrix and \(-1\) is not an eigenvalue of \(Q\), the inverse coordinate

\[ A=(I_N-Q)(I_N+Q)^{-1} \]

is the matching skew-symmetric table.

So Cayley is a useful chart, not a single global address system for every rotation. Large coordinate magnitudes are needed to approach the omitted locus. That can hurt numerical conditioning and gradients.

“Orthogonal” does not mean “every eigenvalue is \(+1\).” Real orthogonal matrices can have eigenvalues \(+1\), \(-1\), or complex-conjugate pairs on the unit circle. Finite Cayley coordinates exclude exactly the \(-1\) case. They do not exclude ordinary complex rotations.

Two islands of eight-by-eight tables

The set of all real orthogonal \(8\times 8\) tables is

\[ O(8)=\{Q\in\mathbb R^{8\times 8}:Q^\top Q=I_8\}. \]

It has two disconnected islands. Take determinants of \(Q^\top Q=I_8\) and you get \(\det(Q)^2=1\), so \(\det(Q)=+1\) or \(-1\). The \(+1\) island is the special orthogonal group \(SO(8)\), the rotations. The \(-1\) island includes reflections. The name is worth keeping because “orthogonal” alone does not tell you which island you are on.

For a Cayley matrix,

\[ \det Q =\frac{\det(I_8-A)}{\det(I_8+A)}. \]

But

\[ \det(I_8-A)=\det((I_8-A)^\top)=\det(I_8+A), \]

so \(\det Q=+1\). Cayley never crosses into the reflection island.

That restriction is real at the level of \(Q\). It is not a restriction on the mixer we will build next, because the next map uses squared sizes of blocks and throws the signs away [R005]. Concretely, let \(R\) be the diagonal table with one \(-1\) and seven \(+1\) entries. Then \(RQ\) has the opposite determinant, while multiplying a row by \(-1\) does not change any block’s squared Frobenius size. Both islands can produce the same mixer.

Do not mash this island argument into the missing-eigenvalue caveat. They are different holes:

  • the determinant statement keeps the auxiliary \(Q\) off the entire \(\det=-1\) island, and the later sign-blind block-size map erases that distinction;
  • the \(-1\)-eigenvalue statement omits a locus even inside \(SO(8)\) from finite Cayley coordinates, though the locus sits in the closure as the coordinates run off to infinity.

This builds a router, not streams

The four leftover copies already exist. They live in

\[ x_l\in\mathbb R^{B\times 4\times L\times D}, \]

where \(B\) is how many sentences ride together, the second axis holds the four copies, \(L\) is how many tokens sit in a sentence, and \(D\) is how wide each token is. Nothing in this chapter manufactured those four copies. Nothing in this chapter turned the eight auxiliary axes of \(Q_l\) into eight copies either.

What we still need is a \(4\times 4\) table \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\) that mixes information between those copies. For one layer, each batch slice updates as

\[ x_{l+1}[b] :=H_l^{\mathrm{res}}x_l[b] +(H_l^{\mathrm{post}})^\top F(H_l^{\mathrm{pre}}x_l[b],W_l), \]

with shapes

\[ \begin{aligned} x_l&\in\mathbb R^{B\times 4\times L\times D},\\ H_l^{\mathrm{pre}}&\in\mathbb R^{1\times 4},\\ H_l^{\mathrm{post}}&\in\mathbb R^{1\times 4},\\ H_l^{\mathrm{res}}&\in\mathbb R^{4\times 4}. \end{aligned} \]

Earlier exact mixers face an awkward choice. A convex mixture over every permutation can cover the Birkhoff polytope but grows factorially with the number of copies. A Kronecker factorization is much cheaper and cannot reach every mixing pattern. The 2026 go-mHC paper proposes a third route: parameterize a larger orthogonal table continuously, then collapse its blocks to an exactly doubly stochastic mixer [R005].

Original mHC used a Sinkhorn-style map for \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\). go-mHC replaces the residual mixer with this pipeline [R005]:

\[ \text{normalized activation} \xrightarrow{\text{learned projection}} u_l\in\mathbb R^{28} \xrightarrow{\operatorname{skew}} A_l\in\mathbb R^{8\times 8} \xrightarrow{\operatorname{Cayley}} Q_l\in SO(8) \xrightarrow{\Phi_{4,2}} H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\in\mathbb R^{4\times 4}. \]

Equation (7) of that paper writes the first two arrows as a learned projection onto normalized activations, then skew. We have built and proved the middle two arrows. In the last arrow, \(Q_l\) is cut into a \(4\times 4\) grid of \(2\times 2\) blocks, and each mixer entry is a scaled squared Frobenius size. The next chapter proves that this produces nonnegative entries with unit row and column sums.

Here \(d=4\) is the number of leftover copies and \(s=2\) is that block size. Their product is the auxiliary side \(N=ds=8\). The 28 parameters belong to one routed instance of this auxiliary table. They are not 28 streams.

So: this builds a router, not streams. \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\) mixes the four copies already sitting on axis 1 of batch-first \(x_l\). The \(8\times 8\) table \(Q_l\) is scaffolding.

Skew coordinates let an ordinary optimizer emit unconstrained reals while the deterministic Cayley map enforces the right-angle invariant. Learning lives in the projection that emits \(u_l\). The Cayley transform itself has no learned parameters.

Because go-mHC appeared as arXiv:2604.02309v1 in 2026, its architectural and training claims are recent research, not settled fact. The linear-algebra statements in this chapter are standard. Claims about why this parameterization helps optimization remain paper evidence. Dandachi and Diggs-Galligan report synthetic routing experiments and a 30M-parameter GPT-style experiment [R005]. Those results do not turn every proposed optimization story into a theorem, and they do not establish behavior at arbitrary scale.

What breaks if we skip a step

Skip the antisymmetric packing, and 28 free numbers become 64 unrelated entries. The orthogonality proof dies immediately: it uses \(A^\top=-A\) twice. A generic \(A\) can also make \(I+A\) singular.

Skip Cayley and use \(A_l\) itself as the mixer, and the entries may be negative, the row and column sums are not one, and the table is \(8\times 8\) rather than the \(4\times 4\) we actually apply to the four copies. Skew-symmetry is not double stochasticity.

Use an arbitrary dense \(Q_l\) with no orthogonality, and the next chapter’s block-size row and column proof loses its identities. Normalizing afterward would be a different method, with different numerical and exactness properties.

Forget the block-size map, and you have only an orthogonal scaffold, not \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\). Call \(Q_l\) “the streams,” and the shapes stop making sense: \(Q_l\in\mathbb R^{8\times 8}\), while the state is \(x_l\in\mathbb R^{B\times 4\times L\times D}\).

Ways to get this wrong

  • Counting 64 learned coordinates. Skew-symmetry leaves \(8\cdot 7/2=28\) independent entries. The lower triangle and the diagonal are determined.
  • Building streams from \(A_l\). The streams already exist. This path builds residual routing between them.
  • Equating \(s=2\) with two extra streams. \(s\) is the auxiliary block size. Stream count stays \(d=4\).
  • Calling \(A_l\) orthogonal. \(A_l\) is skew-symmetric. \(Q_l\) is orthogonal.
  • Claiming Cayley covers all of \(O(8)\). It yields \(\det=+1\) and misses matrices with eigenvalue \(-1\) at finite coordinates.
  • Claiming determinant \(+1\) means every eigenvalue is positive. The determinant is the product of the eigenvalues. Rotations commonly have non-real unit-modulus eigenvalues.
  • Writing an explicit inverse in production code. Use a linear solve. The equations may wear an inverse; the kernel need not.
