This is not yet evidence at mHC’s largest scale Exact construction theorem; paper reports zero column-sum product error and competitive results around 60M/130M scales Exact construction theorem; paper reports stable gradients in a 30M GPT-style model and synthetic mixing results. Large-scale stability remains untested in v1

At \(d=4\), mHC-lite’s 24-way mixture is modest and its native softmax and matrix multiply stay inside ordinary framework ops. At \(d=16\), \(16!\approx2.09\times10^{13}\) coordinates end the discussion before the optimizer gets a vote. For large composite \(d\) with a hard compute budget, KromHC has the smallest polynomial cost in this comparison, but cannot express arbitrary cycles or spectra. go-mHC spends cubic work for a finite-\(s\) image that is not forced to be a Kronecker product. mHC spends iterative normalization and accepts finite tolerance. HC retains the widest unconstrained algebraic freedom and the weakest conservation guarantee.

One line that splits two locks

The exact distinction appears in one line. Given residual coordinates \(u_l\), mHC computes

\[ H_l^{\mathrm{res}}=\operatorname{SK}_S(\exp(\operatorname{mat}(u_l))) \in \mathbb R^{d\times d}, \]

whereas go-mHC sets \(N=ds\), fills \(A_l\in\mathbb R^{N\times N}\) with \(A_l^\top=-A_l\), solves for orthogonal \(Q_l\), partitions \(Q_l\) into blocks \(Q_{ij}\in\mathbb R^{s\times s}\), and defines

\[ (H_l^{\mathrm{res}})_{ij}=\frac1s\lVert Q_{ij}\rVert_F^2. \]

In exact arithmetic, go-mHC’s output is nonnegative and has every row and column sum equal to one [R005].

That statement proves membership, not full coverage, favorable conditioning of the Cayley solve, a lower validation loss, or large-scale systems efficiency. The v1 evidence for those questions is limited to the experiments it actually reports.

What falls off

Omit the constraint from HC, and products \(H_{L-1}^{\mathrm{res}}\cdots H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\) may amplify, attenuate, or sign-flip stream state without a conservation law. Omit enough Sinkhorn iterations from mHC, and the unnormalized marginal can deviate measurably from one. Omit permutations from mHC-lite, and full Birkhoff coverage no longer follows. Omit factors from KromHC, and the final matrix has the wrong dimension; enlarge factors, and factorial factor-mixture cost returns. Omit orthogonality from go-mHC, and the trace identities proving row and column sums collapse.

There is also a less dramatic failure: omit \(C,S,s\), or \(i_k\) from a complexity statement, and a correct asymptotic comparison becomes incorrect procurement advice.

Ways to get this wrong

Calling finite-step Sinkhorn “exact enough” without reporting a row/column residual is the first one. Exactness is a mathematical property; tolerance is a measured number.

  1. Counting coordinates, not learned weights. go-mHC emits \(ds(ds-1)/2\) coordinates, but its dynamic projection contains \((dC+1)ds(ds-1)/2\) parameters.
  2. Claiming finite-\(s\) go-mHC covers—or necessarily misses—\(\mathcal B_d\). At \(s=1,d\ge3\), the image is strict. For a named larger finite \(s\), neither equality nor strictness is asserted here. Images embed at integer multiples of \(s\), adjacent levels need not nest, and the union over all block sizes is dense.
  3. Claiming KromHC has arbitrary Birkhoff spectra. Kronecker eigenvalues are products of factor eigenvalues.
  4. Using \(O(d^3)\) without saying what is fixed. go-mHC is \(O(Cs^2d^3)\) in parameters and its quoted solve estimate is \(O(Cs^3d^3)\).
  5. Comparing original HC’s reduced router to mHC’s flattened router as if they were identical implementations. They are not.
  6. Equating exact double stochasticity with identity. A permutation matrix is doubly stochastic and may rearrange every stream.
  7. Treating paper FLOP estimates as wall-clock latency. Kernel launches, memory movement, fusion, precision, and backward storage matter.

Try it

1. Count the keys on one module

For \(d=4\), \(C=512\), compute one-module parameter counts for mHC, mHC-lite, binary-factor KromHC with \((i_1,i_2)=(2,2)\), and go-mHC with \(s=2\).

Answer Here \(P_{common}=2(4^2)(512)+2(4)+3=16{,}395\), \(q+1=2{,}049\), and the residual coordinate counts are 16, 24, 4, and 28. Totals are respectively \(49{,}179\), \(65{,}571\), \(24{,}591\), and \(73{,}767\).

2. Exact is not the same as covering

Classify each statement as true or false: (a) every KromHC output is doubly stochastic; (b) every doubly stochastic matrix is a binary-factor KromHC output; (c) every finite-\(s\) go-mHC output is doubly stochastic; (d) every matrix in \(\mathcal B_d\) has a finite-logit mHC-lite representation.

Answer
  1. true; (b) false in general; (c) true in exact arithmetic; (d) false as written because finite softmax logits assign positive weight to every permutation. The closure covers all of \(\mathcal B_d\), including vertices.

3. A spectrum KromHC cannot hit

Let \(d=4\) and KromHC use two \(2\times2\) doubly stochastic factors. Explain why it cannot represent a routing matrix whose spectrum contains \(i\).

Answer A \(2\times2\) doubly stochastic factor has eigenvalues \(1\) and a real number \(a\in[-1,1]\). The Kronecker spectrum is \(\{1,a,b,ab\}\), entirely real. Therefore \(i\) cannot occur.

4. A crossover you cannot invent

Using only the quoted asymptotics, can you prove that go-mHC is faster than mHC for all \(d\)?

Answer No. The estimates are \(\Theta(Cs^3d^3)\) and \(\Theta(CSd^2)\), so their ratio scales like \(sd\cdot s^2/S=s^3d/S\), up to constants. Either can be smaller in a finite regime; hardware and the omitted shared projection/application also matter. Benchmark the intended \((d,s,S,C)\) and implementation.

5. Pick a lock from a spec sheet

You require exact double stochasticity, native framework operations, \(d=32\), and the smallest parameter count among these methods. Full Birkhoff coverage is not required. Which row survives?

Answer KromHC with small factors, assuming a suitable factorization of 32. mHC is not exact at finite \(S\); mHC-lite is factorial; go-mHC is exact and native but has cubic-in-\(d\) dynamic projection cost for fixed \(s\); HC is unconstrained. The choice explicitly accepts KromHC’s restricted routing set.

Check yourself

  1. What tensor emits the routing coordinates, and what is its input width?
  2. Is membership in \(\mathcal B_d\) exact, approximate, or absent?
  3. What subset of \(\mathcal B_d\) is covered at finite hyperparameters?
  4. Which cost is being reported: parameters, arithmetic construction, route application, memory traffic, or measured time?
  5. At what model scale was stability actually tested?
Answers
  1. Name the emitting map and its width. For the later flattened family the input width is \(q=dC\); original DHC is a reduced/normalized router, not that flattened map.
  2. Exact, approximate, or absent — and say which variant you mean. Finite-step mHC is approximate; mHC-lite, KromHC, and go-mHC are exact in real arithmetic; HC is unconstrained.
  3. The produced set or its closure at the finite \(S\), \(s\), or factor list you actually use. Closure of mHC-lite is all of \(\mathcal B_d\); KromHC is a Kronecker subset; finite-\(s\) go-mHC is an image, not a mood.
  4. Say which cost. Paper construction FLOPs are not wall-clock latency, and they are not the projection count \(F_{\mathrm{proj}}\).
  5. Quote the paper’s scale: HC at 1B/7B/MoE and vision; mHC at large scale with 6.7% time overhead at \(d=4\); mHC-lite at 45M–360M; KromHC around 60M/130M; go-mHC at 30M in v1.

The compact parameter rule is \(P=(dC+1)r+2d^2C+2d+3\) for the later flattened-router family, with \(r=d^2,d!,\sum i_k!\), or \(ds(ds-1)/2\). The compact mathematical rule is simpler: exact membership and full coverage are separate axes.

The table compares mechanisms. What those mechanisms mean as learned dataflow — which streams a block reads, where it writes, and how persistent state moves through depth — still has to stay tethered to the router granularity and constraint set established here.

Sources

  • [R001] D. Zhu et al., “Hyper-Connections,” ICLR 2025, arXiv:2409.19606. https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.19606
  • [R002] Z. Xie et al., “mHC: Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections,” arXiv:2512.24880, 2025/2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.24880
  • [R003] Y. Yang and J. Gao, “mHC-lite: You Don’t Need 20 Sinkhorn-Knopp Iterations,” arXiv:2601.05732, 2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.05732
  • [R004] W. Zhou et al., “KromHC: Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections with Kronecker-Product Residual Matrices,” arXiv:2601.21579, 2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.21579
  • [R005] T. Dandachi and S. Diggs-Galligan, “go-mHC: Direct Parameterization of Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections via Generalized Orthostochastic Matrices,” arXiv:2604.02309v1, 2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02309v1
  • [R021] R. Sinkhorn and P. Knopp, “Concerning Nonnegative Matrices and Doubly Stochastic Matrices,” Pacific Journal of Mathematics 21(2), 1967, pp. 343–348. https://doi.org/10.2140/pjm.1967.21.343
  • [R019] G. Birkhoff, “Tres observaciones sobre el algebra lineal,” Universidad Nacional de Tucumán Revista, Serie A 5, 1946, pp. 147–151. https://eudml.org/doc/130662

Chapter 16 — Blend, pour, shuffle leftovers

Four labeled trays sit on a workbench. Each tray holds one leftover copy of the sentence table — a stream. A worker walks up.

The worker may scoop a blend from several trays, run one tool once, and pour the result back with different strengths. Meanwhile the leftover contents are shuffled so yesterday’s work is still there tomorrow. Then you add the pour to the leftover shuffle.

Those three moves are read, write, and persist. Together they are a dataflow: which state goes where. They do not decide which worker is standing at the bench.

We already wrote the one-line spell in Chapter 4. Today we walk it with a pencil. Then we hire three more workers who do different jobs. Then we refuse a popular mix-up. Trays are not specialists, and a pretty heat map is not a job title.

Four trays, one worker

Let \(l\) be which layer we are on. Write

  • \(d\) for how many leftover copies we carry;
  • \(B\) for how many sentences ride together;
  • \(L\) for how many tokens sit in one sentence;
  • \(D\) for how many features each token has;
  • \(\mathbf{x}_l\in\mathbb{R}^{B\times d\times L\times D}\) for the workbench;
  • \(H_l^{\mathrm{pre}}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times d}\) for the scoop;
  • \(H_l^{\mathrm{post}}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times d}\) for the pour;
  • \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\) for the leftover shuffle;
  • \(F_l:\mathbb{R}^{B\times L\times D}\to\mathbb{R}^{B\times L\times D}\) for one worker, with parameters \(W_l\).

The papers write the Hyper-Connection update in stream-first algebra [R001]; [R002]; [R005]. Under this book’s batch-first storage, apply that update independently to each \(d\times L\times D\) slice of a sentence:

\[ \mathbf{x}_{l+1}[b] := H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\mathbf{x}_l[b] + (H_l^{\mathrm{post}})^\top F_l(H_l^{\mathrm{pre}}\mathbf{x}_l[b],W_l). \tag{16.1} \]

The multiplications act only on the stream axis, which is axis 1 in stored \(\mathbf{x}_l\). The scoop therefore produces an ordinary table in \(\mathbb{R}^{B\times L\times D}\). The leftover shuffle and the pour both come back in \(\mathbb{R}^{B\times d\times L\times D}\).

Read the line from the inside out.

Innermost: \(H_l^{\mathrm{pre}}\mathbf{x}_l\) blends the trays into one table. That blend is the read. The name is worth keeping because it names which leftover copies feed the tool, not which tool is used.

Next: \(F_l\) runs once on that blend. One call. Not \(d\) calls.

Then \((H_l^{\mathrm{post}})^\top\) fans the one answer back into the trays. That fan-out is the write.

Meanwhile \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\mathbf{x}_l\) remixes the old trays without asking \(F_l\). That remix is the leftover path: persist, then add.

Those three maps together are state routing. The name is worth keeping because the thing being moved is leftover activation, not a choice of parameters. Changing \(H^{\mathrm{pre}}\), \(H^{\mathrm{post}}\), or \(H^{\mathrm{res}}\) changes connections among streams. It does not, by itself, swap the worker.

A stream is a place where a representation persists. A worker is a parameterized function. A gate can point at either one. The noun after “route” is the whole lesson.

Three numbers through the workshop

Hide batch, sequence, and feature axes by setting \(B=L=D=1\), and use \(d=3\). The workbench is then three scalars:

\[ \mathbf{x}_l= \begin{bmatrix}2\\5\\-1\end{bmatrix} \in\mathbb{R}^{3\times1\times1\times1}. \]

Choose the scoop

\[ H_l^{\mathrm{pre}}= \begin{bmatrix}0.5&0.3&0.2\end{bmatrix} \in\mathbb{R}^{1\times3}. \]

The worker sees one number,

\[ z_l=H_l^{\mathrm{pre}}\mathbf{x}_l =0.5(2)+0.3(5)+0.2(-1)=2.3. \]

Let the worker be \(F_l(z)=2z\), so \(y_l=4.6\). That evaluates \(F_l\) once, not three times.

Choose the pour

\[ H_l^{\mathrm{post}}= \begin{bmatrix}0.1&0.7&0.2\end{bmatrix} \in\mathbb{R}^{1\times3}. \]

The new answer contributes

\[ (H_l^{\mathrm{post}})^\top y_l = \begin{bmatrix}0.46\\3.22\\0.92\end{bmatrix}. \]

Finally choose a leftover shuffle

\[ H_l^{\mathrm{res}}= \begin{bmatrix} 0.8&0.2&0\\ 0.2&0.6&0.2\\ 0&0.2&0.8 \end{bmatrix} \in\mathbb{R}^{3\times3}. \]

Its rows and columns sum to one, so it is doubly stochastic. The carried state is

\[ H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\mathbf{x}_l = \begin{bmatrix}2.6\\3.2\\0.2\end{bmatrix}. \]

Add carry and write:

\[ \mathbf{x}_{l+1} = \begin{bmatrix}3.06\\6.42\\1.12\end{bmatrix}. \]

Three different questions were answered: which state was read, where new work was written, and how old state persisted. No specialist was selected. There was only one function \(F_l\).

A second kind of routing

Neural-network English has reused “routing” with the enthusiasm of a programmer naming every temporary variable data. The trays have a gate. Mixture-of-Experts also has a gate. Therefore the two must be the same, yes? No.

Suppose you have several workers \(E_1,\ldots,E_K\), each with its own parameters. A second gate produces weights \(g_1,\ldots,g_K\) and either mixes their outputs or picks a sparse subset. That is expert routing. The name is worth keeping because the thing being chosen is a parameterized computation, not a leftover tray.

The original sparsely gated Mixture-of-Experts used a trainable gate to choose a sparse combination of feed-forward subnetworks [R014]. Switch Transformer, in its simplified formulation, sends an incoming item to one expert [R015]. Exact routing granularity belongs to the particular implementation. The letters “MoE” do not specify it.

A sparse expert gate normally decides which parameter set processes an input. An HC router decides which persistent state feeds a branch, where the branch result is stored, and how state persists. HC has been evaluated with dense and sparse computational blocks [R001]. That compatibility does not identify HC routing with MoE routing.

State routing alone does not skip an attention call. Four streams do not imply four experts. Four workers do not imply sparse conditional computation.

Question State router Expert router
What is routed? activation state in persistent streams input to parameterized functions
Typical destination another stream, a branch input, or a stream write one or more experts
Does routing alone change parameter choice? no yes
Does routing alone save branch FLOPs? no only if sparse dispatch actually skips experts
Representative coefficients \(H^{\mathrm{pre}},H^{\mathrm{post}},H^{\mathrm{res}}\) \(g\) over experts
Main architectural level residual/dataflow topology conditional parameter activation

A coefficient close to zero is not automatically a computational skip. Dense multiplication by a nearly zero gate can spend the full FLOP budget with admirable precision.

Four different workers

A block has multiple branches when it contains \(K>1\) transformations \(F_1,\ldots,F_K\). The name is worth keeping because it is a graph property: more than one computation is sitting there. They may all run, only some may run, or they may run at fixed positions. Branch multiplicity is not proof of conditional computation.

We now construct a speculative extension. It is a design exercise, not an architecture claimed by the HC, mHC, or go-mHC papers.

Let \(K=4\), and give every worker the same external signature

\[ F_{l,k}:\mathbb{R}^{B\times L\times D} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^{B\times L\times D}, \qquad k\in\{1,2,3,4\}. \]

Inside that interface, set

\[ F_{l,1}=\text{attention},\quad F_{l,2}=\text{WavePDE},\quad F_{l,3}=\text{SSM},\quad F_{l,4}=\text{MLP}. \]

Attention supplies content-dependent interaction across positions [R010]. A Wave-PDE layer is a recent proposal based on differentiable second-order wave-equation simulation [R016]. A selective SSM such as Mamba makes state-space parameters input-dependent and propagates state along the sequence [R017]. An MLP transforms each position’s feature vector independently unless another operation couples positions.

These descriptions concern the operators, not the HC stream axis. Branch-specific adapters may be needed to make widths, auxiliary state, causal conventions, and normalization agree. Declaring a shared signature is an interface contract. It is not evidence that four arbitrary library modules can be plugged together without adapters. Software remains stubbornly literal.

For each worker \(k\), define a scoop \(a_{l,k}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times d}\) and a pour \(b_{l,k}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times d}\). Optionally define a worker coefficient \(g_{l,k}\in\mathbb{R}\). This scalar is layer-level in our example. It is not a claim about the granularity used in any cited implementation.

The illustrative multi-branch update is

\[ \mathbf{x}_{l+1} := H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\mathbf{x}_l + \sum_{k=1}^{4} g_{l,k} b_{l,k}^{\top} F_{l,k}(a_{l,k}\mathbf{x}_l,W_{l,k}). \tag{16.2} \]

Shape ledger for every \(k\):

object shape role
\(\mathbf{x}_l\) \(B\times d\times L\times D\) persistent state
\(a_{l,k}\) \(1\times d\) branch-specific read
\(z_{l,k}\) \(B\times L\times D\) branch input after contracting axis 1
\(y_{l,k}=F_{l,k}(z_{l,k})\) \(B\times L\times D\) branch output
\(b_{l,k}^{\top}y_{l,k}\) \(B\times d\times L\times D\) branch write
\(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\mathbf{x}_l\) \(B\times d\times L\times D\) carried state, applied batchwise

If all four \(g_{l,k}\) are nonzero and all workers are evaluated, (16.2) is a dense multi-branch block. If a router makes some \(g_{l,k}=0\) and the runtime does not dispatch those workers, it becomes conditional branch computation. If the four \(F_{l,k}\) are copies of the same architecture with separate parameters, they can be called experts. Here they are heterogeneous operators, so “branches” is the less misleading name.

A concrete routing plan

Use \(d=4\), \(B=1\), \(L=2\), and \(D=2\). Thus \(\mathbf{x}_l\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times4\times2\times2}\). Stack the scoops into \(A_l\in\mathbb{R}^{4\times4}\):

\[ A_l= \begin{bmatrix} 0.7&0.1&0.1&0.1\\ 0.1&0.7&0.1&0.1\\ 0.1&0.1&0.7&0.1\\ 0.1&0.1&0.1&0.7 \end{bmatrix}. \]

Rows correspond, in order, to attention, Wave-PDE, SSM, and MLP. Each worker receives a tensor in \(\mathbb{R}^{1\times2\times2}\), but each receives a different mixture of the four streams.

Stack the pours into \(C_l\in\mathbb{R}^{4\times4}\):

\[ C_l= \begin{bmatrix} 0.1&0.6&0.2&0.1\\ 0.2&0.1&0.6&0.1\\ 0.1&0.2&0.1&0.6\\ 0.6&0.1&0.2&0.1 \end{bmatrix}. \]

For example, the attention output is written most strongly to stream 2, while the MLP output is written most strongly to stream 1. This is not expert selection: all four workers may still run. It is also not semantic specialization. “Stream 2 receives much attention output” does not prove what stream 2 represents.

Choose the leftover map

\[ H_l^{\mathrm{res}}=0.85I_4+0.15\frac{\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}}{4} \in\mathbb{R}^{4\times4}, \]

where \(I_4\) is the \(4\times4\) identity and \(\mathbf{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}\) is all ones. This map keeps most of each stream and diffusively shares the remainder. It is doubly stochastic.

At layer \(l\), \((A_l)_{kj}\) weights an edge from stream \(j\) to worker \(k\); \((C_l)_{ki}\) weights an edge from worker \(k\) to stream \(i\); and \((H_l^{\mathrm{res}})_{ij}\) weights an edge from old stream \(j\) to new stream \(i\). Changing these coefficients across layers creates a learned, weighted dataflow graph through depth.

That picture is an interpretation, not a theorem about discrete graph discovery. The workers are nonlinear, routes can be dense, and dynamic coefficients may depend on activations. A pretty graph drawing does not make the computation discrete.

The loop that still pays for everyone

The following is pedagogical pseudocode. Production code would batch contractions, fuse cheap operations, and handle branch dispatch explicitly.

input:
    x       [B, d, L, D]
    H_res   [d, d] or [B, d, d]
    A_read  [K, d]
    C_write [K, d]
    gate    [K]
output:
    x_next  [B, d, L, D]

x_next = contract_axis1(H_res, x)         # [B, d, L, D]
for k in 0 .. K-1:
    z = contract_axis1(A_read[k], x)      # [B, L, D]
    y = branch[k](z)                      # [B, L, D]
    x_next += gate[k] * outer_stream(C_write[k], y)
return x_next

This loop evaluates every worker. Replacing gate[k] by zero after branch[k](z) saves no branch work. Sparse conditional computation requires deciding the active set first and dispatching only that set. For sequence-coupled operators such as attention, Wave-PDE, and SSMs, token-wise dispatch may also change or complicate the operator’s semantics. Routing granularity must therefore be designed, stated, and measured — not guessed from a gate tensor.

What a leftover mix is allowed to do

mHC constrains \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\) toward the Birkhoff polytope of doubly stochastic matrices using finite Sinkhorn normalization [R002]. go-mHC replaces that leftover constraint map with a generalized orthostochastic construction derived from an orthogonal block matrix [R005]. In the go-mHC primary formulation, \(H^{\mathrm{pre}}\) and \(H^{\mathrm{post}}\) remain separately generated read/write gates, while the new construction modifies \(H^{\mathrm{res}}\) [R005].

If \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\) is doubly stochastic, it is nonnegative and its rows and columns sum to one. It therefore performs convex state mixing and preserves the all-ones stream mode. This constrains leftover transport. It says nothing about whether attention or SSM is selected, and nothing about the semantic content of a stream.

[Experiment] The HC paper reports language and vision experiments, including a sparse OLMoE setting [R001]. The mHC paper reports large-scale language-model training and systems work [R002]. The go-mHC paper reports synthetic stream-mixing experiments and a 30M-parameter GPT-style validation [R005]. These are recent-paper results, not settled facts across architectures.

Training might make persistent streams carry systematically different kinds of information. That possible behavior is stream specialization. The name is worth keeping as a hypothesis, not a definition. It is an empirical claim. It requires measurements across inputs, layers, checkpoints, and seeds. It does not follow from \(d>1\), unequal gates, or a readable visualization.

Combining exact or approximately constrained leftover routing with heterogeneous workers may yield useful learned dataflow. Equation (16.2) is one possible formulation. It has not been established by the cited results as a generally superior architecture.

What disappears if we omit a verb

Omit the read, and every worker sees a fixed stream or a fixed aggregate. The architecture loses the ability to choose which leftover state supplies each computation.

Omit the write, and worker output always lands in a fixed destination. Read choice can still vary, but storage choice cannot. This is like having flexible database queries and one hard-coded table for every update.

Omit the leftover shuffle, and old streams cannot mix directly between layers. Branch writes may still couple them, but there is no independent carry path. In mHC and go-mHC, this also removes the object to which the doubly stochastic constraint is applied.

Omit branch routing from the heterogeneous extension, and all workers follow a fixed schedule. That may be perfectly reasonable, but it is not conditional computation.

Omit multiple branches, and state routing remains useful. The original equation has one \(F_l\), and the stream topology can still be dynamic.

Omit the evidence standard, and speculative stream roles quickly become “discoveries.” Architecture diagrams are unusually effective at converting hopes into past tense.

Ways to get this wrong

  1. “Each stream is an expert.” A stream stores activations; an expert owns parameters. One worker can read all streams once.
  2. “Four streams mean four calls to \(F\).” Equation (16.1) has one call. Equation (16.2) has four because we deliberately added four workers.
  3. “A soft gate gives sparse-compute savings.” Not unless dispatch avoids the gated computation.
  4. “\(H^{\mathrm{res}}\) chooses attention versus MLP.” It routes carried state. A separate branch or expert gate would choose a computation.
  5. “Doubly stochastic means one-hot routing.” It permits dense convex mixing; permutation matrices are only special cases.
  6. “Different read/write profiles prove specialization.” They show different routing coefficients, not the information content or causal function of streams.
  7. “Token-wise MoE routing transfers directly to every heterogeneous operator.” Attention, SSM, and Wave-PDE workers couple positions differently. A legal tensor shape is not yet a sound dispatch algorithm.
  8. “Latent computation graph means discrete program.” Here it is a weighted, generally dense, differentiable graph interpretation.

The leftover mix is also a dynamical system. Repeat \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\) and streams can share a stationary mode, smear into each other, or cycle. That is tomorrow’s algebra — what persist can do over depth, not which specialist gets the next token.

Try it

1. Separate the three state routes

Using the three-number workshop, recompute \(z_l\) if only the scoop changes to \(H_l^{\mathrm{pre}}=[0,1,0]\). Which other intermediate quantities change immediately?

Answer

The read becomes \(z_l=5\), so \(y_l=F_l(5)=10\) and the write becomes \([1,7,2]^\top\). The carried state \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\mathbf{x}_l=[2.6,3.2,0.2]^\top\) does not change because neither \(H^{\mathrm{res}}\) nor \(\mathbf{x}_l\) changed.

2. Classify the routers

Classify each operation as state routing, expert routing, both, or neither: (a) \(H^{\mathrm{res}}\mathbf{x}\); (b) top-1 selection among eight separately parameterized MLPs; (c) summing attention and SSM outputs with fixed weight \(1/2\); (d) reading stream 3 into one fixed MLP.

Answer
  1. state routing; (b) expert routing; (c) neither routing operation is learned — there are merely two fixed branches; (d) state routing by a fixed one-hot read, but no expert selection because only one fixed MLP exists.

3. Audit the shapes

Let \(d=4\), \(B=8\), \(L=512\), \(D=1024\), and \(K=4\). Give the shapes of \(A_l\), all branch inputs stacked over \(k\), \(C_l\), and the summed write term in (16.2).

Answer

\(A_l\in\mathbb{R}^{4\times4}\). Stacking the four branch inputs gives \(Z_l\in\mathbb{R}^{4\times8\times512\times1024}\), where the first axis indexes branches. \(C_l\in\mathbb{R}^{4\times4}\). Each written output and their sum have canonical state shape \(8\times4\times512\times1024\), matching \(B\times d\times L\times D\).

4. Find the false compute saving

A program evaluates all four workers, multiplies three outputs by zero, and retains the attention output. Has it implemented top-1 conditional computation? Repair the algorithm in words.

Answer

It has top-1 output selection but not top-1 compute: all branches ran. Compute the gate first, form the active branch set, dispatch only active examples/sequences to compatible branches, run those branches, then scatter their outputs into the write accumulation. The dispatch granularity must respect each branch’s sequence semantics.

5. Design a specialization test

Suppose stream 1 has a large attention read coefficient and stream 3 has a large SSM write coefficient. Propose evidence that would be stronger than naming stream 1 “global context” and stream 3 “memory.”

Answer

Measure stream contents and causal function across many inputs, layers, checkpoints, and random seeds. Examples include trained probes with held-out controls, interventions that ablate or swap one stream while measuring task-specific effects, routing consistency statistics, and comparisons against permuted-stream labels. A specialization claim should survive the fact that stream indices can be relabeled together with router coordinates.

Check yourself

  1. In equation (16.1), what three jobs do \(H^{\mathrm{pre}}\), \(H^{\mathrm{post}}\), and \(H^{\mathrm{res}}\) do?
  2. Why do four leftover copies not imply four experts?
  3. A program multiplies three of four branch outputs by zero after all four ran. Did it save the skipped workers’ FLOPs?
  4. If \(H^{\mathrm{res}}\) is doubly stochastic, what does that constrain, and what does it not decide?
  5. What would you need, beyond a suggestive heat map, before calling one stream “syntax” and another “memory”?
Answers
  1. Read which streams feed the worker; write the worker’s answer back into streams; persist and remix leftover state.
  2. A stream stores activations. An expert owns parameters. Equation (16.1) calls one \(F_l\) once.
  3. No. A near-zero gate still spends the full branch budget unless dispatch skips the worker.
  4. It constrains leftover transport: nonnegative convex mixing that preserves the all-ones stream mode. It does not choose a worker or assign a semantic role to a stream.
  5. Measurements of content and causal function across inputs, layers, checkpoints, and seeds. Specialization is a hypothesis, not a definition.

You should now be able to inspect a proposed routed architecture and ask four separate questions: which persistent states are read, written, and carried; which parameterized branch is selected, if any; how many branches actually execute; and what direct evidence supports any claimed semantic specialization.

For the heterogeneous example, \(A_l\) and \(C_l\) route state around attention, Wave-PDE, SSM, and MLP branches. The optional \(g_l\) routes among branches only if it affects dispatch. \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\) carries and mixes old state. None of these definitions assigns human-readable roles to streams.

  • [R005] T. Dandachi and S. Diggs-Galligan, “go-mHC: Direct Parameterization of Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections via Generalized Orthostochastic Matrices,” arXiv:2604.02309v1, 2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02309v1
  • [R014] N. Shazeer et al., “Outrageously Large Neural Networks: The Sparsely-Gated Mixture-of-Experts Layer,” arXiv:1701.06538, 2017. https://arxiv.org/abs/1701.06538
  • [R015] W. Fedus, B. Zoph, and N. Shazeer, “Switch Transformers: Scaling to Trillion Parameter Models with Simple and Efficient Sparsity,” JMLR 23(120), 2022; arXiv:2101.03961. https://arxiv.org/abs/2101.03961
  • [R010] A. Vaswani et al., “Attention Is All You Need,” arXiv:1706.03762, 2017. https://arxiv.org/abs/1706.03762

Chapter 17 — Leftover copies that refuse to settle

Three leftover copies of the same sentence sit in three cups. Pour them into one another. Sometimes the three colors become one gray. Sometimes they just swap seats. Sometimes they chase each other around the table and never sit down.

The pour is the residual router from earlier chapters: a doubly stochastic table \(H_l\). Every row is a complete recipe. Every column spends each old cup once. Those two honesty checks stop the total from exploding. They do not decide whether the cups settle.

This chapter watches one pour, then another, then a third. The names for the motions come after the pictures.

Three leftover copies, one pour

The router acts only on the leftover-copy axis. Batch, token, and feature sit still. Write \(d\) for the number of leftover copies — the streams. Freeze one coordinate \((b,t,c)\) and stack the \(d\) leftover numbers into a short column \(x_l\in\mathbb{R}^d\). Then the next layer is just

\[ x_{l+1}=H_l x_l. \]

The rest of the tensor \(X_l\in\mathbb{R}^{B\times d\times T\times C}\) has not vanished. We have merely selected one vertical slice. \(B\) is batch size, \(T\) is sequence length, and \(C\) is feature width. Every other slice uses the same table.

Take \(d=3\) and this permutation:

\[ P = \begin{pmatrix} 0&0&1\\ 1&0&0\\ 0&1&0 \end{pmatrix} \in\mathbb{R}^{3\times3}. \]

It sends \((a,b,c)^\top\) to \((c,a,b)^\top\). Start with all the ink in the first cup,

\[ x_0=e_1=(1,0,0)^\top\in\mathbb{R}^3, \]

and pour three times:

\[ \begin{aligned} x_0&=(1,0,0)^\top,\\ x_1=Px_0&=(0,1,0)^\top,\\ x_2=P^2x_0&=(0,0,1)^\top,\\ x_3=P^3x_0&=(1,0,0)^\top. \end{aligned} \]

The first cup is back. Another three pours would do the same walk. In matrix language, \(P^3=I_3\). Nothing mixed. The leftover copies just changed seats.

\(P\) is doubly stochastic. Every entry is nonnegative. Every row sums to one. Every column sums to one. Honesty did not force the colors to blend.

Stir until they match

Now use a different honest table:

\[ M = \begin{pmatrix} 1/2&1/4&1/4\\ 1/4&1/2&1/4\\ 1/4&1/4&1/2 \end{pmatrix} = \frac14 I_3+\frac14\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^\top \in\mathbb{R}^{3\times3}. \]

Each new cup keeps half of itself and takes a quarter from each neighbor. Start again with \(x_0=e_1\):

\[ \begin{aligned} x_1&=(0.5,\;0.25,\;0.25)^\top,\\ x_2&=(0.375,\;0.3125,\;0.3125)^\top,\\ x_3&=(0.34375,\;0.328125,\;0.328125)^\top,\\ x_4&=(0.3359375,\;0.33203125,\;0.33203125)^\top. \end{aligned} \]

The three numbers add to one at every step. They are walking toward

\[ u=\bigl(\tfrac13,\tfrac13,\tfrac13\bigr)^\top. \]

After four pours the first cup is already \(0.3359\ldots\), a hair above one third. Keep going and the leftover copies become interchangeable.

Both \(P\) and \(M\) are doubly stochastic. One cycles forever. One blends. “The eigenvalues are bounded” is true of both and explains neither.

Why one cycles and one fades

A special direction of a table \(H\) is a nonzero vector \(v\) that comes out pointing the same way, only scaled:

\[ Hv=\lambda v. \]

The scale factor \(\lambda\) is an eigenvalue. The name is worth keeping because it is the one number that tells you what repeated pouring does to that direction: multiply by \(\lambda\) each layer.

Every row-stochastic table has \(\lambda=1\) with \(v=\mathbf{1}\), the all-ones column. For a doubly stochastic table the uniform leftover \(u=\mathbf{1}/d\) is therefore a fixed leftover, and the stream sum \(\mathbf{1}^\top x_l\) never changes. That surviving direction is a stationary mode. The name is worth keeping because it does not move. Everything interesting happens to the leftover that disagrees with it.

For \(P\), the characteristic equation is

\[ \lambda^3-1=0. \]

Write

\[ \omega=e^{2\pi i/3}=-\frac12+i\frac{\sqrt3}{2}. \]

The three eigenvalues are the three cube roots of one:

\[ \operatorname{spec}(P)=\{1,\omega,\bar\omega\},\qquad\omega^3=1. \]

The eigenvalue \(1\) belongs to \(\mathbf{1}\). The conjugate pair is rotation by \(\pm120^\circ\) on the plane of leftover copies that already add to zero,

\[ \mathcal{D}=\{v\in\mathbb{R}^3:\mathbf{1}^\top v=0\}. \]

Call \(\mathcal{D}\) the disagreement plane. The name is worth keeping because it is exactly the leftover that is not the mean. A three-cycle does not mix that disagreement away. It walks it around the table.

A real table can have a nonreal eigenvalue. The partner is always the conjugate, and the two of them together describe a real two-dimensional wobble. Write \(\lambda=re^{i\theta}\) with magnitude \(r\ge0\) and angle \(\theta\). After \(k\) pours the scale is \(r^k\) and the phase is \(k\theta\). The leftover copies never become imaginary. The complex number is bookkeeping for a clock hand, the same way \(\cos\theta+i\sin\theta\) records a turn.

For \(M\) the eigenvalues are \(1\), \(1/4\), and \(1/4\). Split \(x_0\) into the stationary leftover and the rest:

\[ x_0-u=\bigl(\tfrac23,-\tfrac13,-\tfrac13\bigr)^\top. \]

Every vector in \(\mathcal{D}\) is multiplied by \(1/4\). So the whole walk has a closed form:

\[ x_k=u+\left(\frac14\right)^k(x_0-u). \]

That is why \(x_4\) already sits next to \(u\). Four factors of one fourth have almost erased the disagreement.

If a fixed stochastic table is irreducible and aperiodic, the leftover copies converge to a unique stationary distribution. For a doubly stochastic table that distribution is uniform. Our \(M\) is strictly positive, so it meets the conditions. \(P\) is periodic, so it does not. Both have spectral radius one. Radius one is a ceiling, not a personality.

A single table’s eigenvalues describe the powers \(H^k\) only when that table stays put, and only after you include any Jordan blocks. A deep model generally uses a product \(H_{L-1}\cdots H_1 H_0\). If the factors vary and do not commute, their individual spectra are not a complete analysis of that product.

Half stay, half rotate

A useful middle case is the lazy cycle

\[ L=\frac12(I_3+P). \]

Half the leftover stays in its cup. Half is sent around the three-cycle. The eigenvalues are

\[ 1,\qquad \frac12(1+\omega)=\frac14+i\frac{\sqrt3}{4},\qquad \frac12(1+\bar\omega)=\frac14-i\frac{\sqrt3}{4}. \]

The complex pair has magnitude \(1/2\) and angles \(\pm\pi/3\). Starting from \(e_1\),

\[ (1,0,0)^\top \to(0.5,0.5,0)^\top \to(0.25,0.5,0.25)^\top \to(0.25,0.375,0.375)^\top. \]

The disagreement rotates by \(60^\circ\) per step, in an appropriate real basis, and halves in size. That is damped circulation: the leftover chases itself around the table while walking toward gray.

When the magnitude \(r<1\), rotation is damped. When \(r=1\), it can cycle without decay. When \(r=0\), that mode is erased in one step — a decisive career change for a leftover.

The average rides through

For any leftover column \(x\in\mathbb{R}^d\), write its mean

\[ \mu=\frac1d\mathbf{1}^\top x \]

and its centered part \(z=x-\mu\mathbf{1}\). Then \(\mathbf{1}^\top z=0\). If \(H\) is doubly stochastic,

\[ H(\mu\mathbf{1})=\mu\mathbf{1}, \qquad \mathbf{1}^\top Hz=\mathbf{1}^\top z=0. \]

The stationary line \(\operatorname{span}\{\mathbf{1}\}\) and the disagreement subspace \(\mathcal{D}\) are both invariant. Spectral dynamics happen inside \(\mathcal{D}\). The mean rides through unchanged.

That gives a practical diagnostic for a fixed \(H\): ignore the guaranteed eigenvalue at one, and read the rest.

  • Values near zero: leftover distinctions vanish quickly.
  • Values near one: leftover distinctions linger.
  • Values near a root of unity: nearly periodic motion.
  • A nonreal pair: circulation in some two-dimensional leftover.

The diagnostic is incomplete for crooked tables. Eigenvectors can be badly conditioned. Jordan blocks can add polynomial factors. Layer-varying products can behave differently from any one layer. Spectra are a lens, not an MRI scanner.

They also isolate only the leftover route. In

\[ X_{l+1}=H_l X_l+(H_l^{\mathrm{post}})^\top F(H_l^{\mathrm{pre}}X_l,W_l), \]

the first term carries existing leftover copies. The second term injects a newly computed note. Eigenvalues of \(H_l\) do not, by themselves, predict the full nonlinear stack. The branch \(F\), the read and write routers, data dependence, and the product of different \(H_l\) tables remain in play.

For a pedagogical, unbatched router the leftover pour looks like this:

input:  H [d, d] or [B, d, d], X [B, d, T, C]
output: Y [B, d, T, C]
Y[b,i,t,c] = sum_j H[b or shared,i,j] * X[b,j,t,c]

This does not assert that a primary implementation shares one \(H\) across all batches or tokens. If a router is data-dependent or batched, analyze each realized \(d\times d\) table and, for depth dynamics, the realized ordered product.

Corners that never mix

A permutation cycle of length \(q\) satisfies \(P_q^q=I_q\), so every eigenvalue obeys \(\lambda^q=1\). Its spectrum is exactly the \(q\)th roots of unity:

\[ \bigl\{e^{2\pi i j/q}:j=0,1,\ldots,q-1\bigr\}. \]

A general permutation splits into disjoint cycles, and its spectrum is the union of those root sets. These tables sit at vertices of the Birkhoff polytope. Exact periodic leftover motion lives at some of the polytope’s corners. Convex mixtures of permutations often pull the nonstationary eigenvalues inward and damp those periods. That is the geometric reason \(L=\tfrac12(I_3+P)\) blends while \(P\) does not.

A map of allowed numbers

Which complex numbers can even appear? For row-stochastic tables the answer has a name.

The Karpelevič region \(\Theta_d\subset\mathbb{C}\) is the set of all numbers that occur as an eigenvalue of some \(d\times d\) row-stochastic matrix. Karpelevič characterized its boundary by algebraic arcs joining particular roots of unity of orders at most \(d\) [R035]; [R036]. It lives in the closed unit disk and is symmetric across the real axis. The name is worth keeping because it is a map of allowed leftover motions, not a portrait of one trained router.

As \(d\) grows, more roots of unity become available and the feasible region grows more boundary arcs. Read the map as follows:

  • \(1\) marks the stationary leftover;
  • points near \(0\) mark leftover that is forgotten in a hurry;
  • points near the unit circle mark leftover that persists;
  • angle marks how fast a leftover oscillates or circulates.

\(\Theta_d\) describes all row-stochastic tables. Doubly stochastic tables form a subset, so their possible eigenvalues form a subset of \(\Theta_d\). A Karpelevič plot is a valid outer landscape for mHC routing, not automatically an exact picture of the Birkhoff polytope for every \(d\).

Spectral reach is also only a surrogate for matrix reach. Distinct tables can share the same spectrum. Filling many eigenvalue bins does not prove coverage of all \((d-1)^2\) degrees of freedom in the Birkhoff polytope.

What the tiled mixer can hear

Original mHC maps residual-routing parameters toward the Birkhoff polytope using finite Sinkhorn normalization [R002]. Exact variants including KromHC and go-mHC instead construct doubly stochastic routers by design [R004]; [R005]. The stationary uniform leftover and the preservation of stream sums follow from double stochasticity, independent of how the router was written down.

KromHC builds a router from factor tables

\[ H=A_1\otimes A_2\otimes\cdots\otimes A_K, \qquad A_k\in\mathsf{B}_{i_k}, \qquad \prod_{k=1}^K i_k=d, \]

where \(\mathsf{B}_{i_k}\) is the set of \(i_k\times i_k\) doubly stochastic matrices [R004]. The Kronecker identity for spectra is

\[ \operatorname{spec}(A\otimes B) = \{\lambda_i(A)\lambda_j(B)\}_{i,j}. \]

Every \(2\times2\) doubly stochastic table has the form

\[ A(a) = \begin{pmatrix}a&1-a\\1-a&a\end{pmatrix}, \qquad 0\le a\le1, \]

with eigenvalues \(1\) and \(2a-1\), both real. Therefore a router made only from aligned \(2\times2\) factors has only real eigenvalues: products of real numbers remain stubbornly real. It cannot equal the three-cycle \(P\), whose spectrum contains \(\omega\) and \(\bar\omega\).

That proof applies to the all-\(2\times2\)-factor configuration emphasized in the go-mHC spectral comparison and commonly used to tile power-of-two stream counts. It does not prove that every KromHC configuration has only real eigenvalues. If some factor size \(i_k\ge3\), that factor can itself have complex eigenvalues and cycles up to its own coordinate structure. The broader restriction is separability: eigenvalues and eigenvectors inherit products of the chosen factor manifolds, and an arbitrary nonseparable \(d\)-stream router is generally unavailable.

The KromHC paper evaluated exact Kronecker-structured routing in language-model pretraining and emphasized parameter efficiency and exact double stochasticity [R004]. The spectral-reach comparison was reported by the later go-mHC paper: its Figure 4 optimized random spectral targets for \(d=4\) and found the tested \(2\times2\)-factor KromHC baseline confined to a real, low-dimensional slice [R005]. That is evidence about the tested parameterization and optimizer, not evidence that complex routing is necessary for every language task. KromHC’s own pretraining results show that a restricted family can still be useful.

What the rotation mixer can hear

go-mHC forms an orthogonal matrix \(Q\in\mathbb{R}^{ds\times ds}\), partitions it into \(d\times d\) blocks \(Q_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}^{s\times s}\), and defines

\[ H_{ij}=\frac1s\lVert Q_{ij}\rVert_F^2. \]

This guarantees \(H\in\mathsf{B}_d\) [R005]; [R029]. At fixed finite \(s\), however, the image \(\Phi_{d,s}(O(ds))\) is a subset of \(\mathsf{B}_d\). Exact double stochasticity is not the same claim as exact finite-\(s\) coverage.

For \(d=3\), \(s=1\), the three-cycle \(P\) is itself orthogonal, has determinant \(+1\), and has no eigenvalue \(-1\). It is therefore in the ordinary Cayley chart, and entrywise squaring leaves it unchanged. The go-mHC construction can represent this particular complex-spectrum router. That is a representability statement, not a claim that a trained network will select it.

The go-mHC paper reports toy spectral-target experiments at \(d=3\) for \(s=1,2\), and a comparison at \(d=4\). In those experiments, go-mHC reached denser portions of the observed spectral region than the tested KromHC baseline, with \(s=2\) denser than \(s=1\) [R005]. The same paper used synthetic stream-mixing tasks and a roughly 30M-parameter GPT-style model, and explicitly lists the small model scale and synthetic emphasis as limitations [R005]. It did not demonstrate that every finite-\(s\) Birkhoff matrix is reachable, that learned large-model routers realize long cycles, or that broader spectral reach universally improves downstream accuracy.

go-mHC removes the all-\(2\times2\) Kronecker product restriction and can expose complex, nonseparable leftover motion. Its reported reach is promising evidence for a parameterization. The architectural value of each spectral region remains a research question.

If we skip the pictures

If we skip the leftover motions and keep only the slogan “doubly stochastic is stable,” several different failures collapse into one sentence.

First, we confuse boundedness with mixing. The cycle \(P\) never explodes, but it never converges either. Second, we miss directed circulation: a real spectral interval cannot encode the same three-stream phase motion as \(\omega,\bar\omega\). Third, we may compare parameterizations only by parameter count while overlooking which leftover walks they exclude.

The opposite omission is equally dangerous. If we use spectra without dynamics, we may infer behavior of \(H_{L-1}\cdots H_0\) from separate layer spectra, ignore injected branch outputs, or mistake eigenvalue coverage for full matrix coverage. The correct tool is not “eigenvalues everywhere.” It is “eigenvalues where their assumptions fit.”

Ways to get this wrong

  1. “Every doubly stochastic matrix mixes to uniform.” False. A permutation cycle is doubly stochastic and periodic. Fixed-matrix convergence needs conditions such as irreducibility and aperiodicity.
  2. “Spectral radius one means all information is preserved.” False. The stationary mode has magnitude one while disagreement modes may contract to zero.
  3. “Complex eigenvalues require complex activations.” False. They encode paired real modes.
  4. “The Karpelevič region equals the spectrum of \(\mathsf{B}_d\).” Not in general; it is defined for row-stochastic matrices and provides an outer region for doubly stochastic ones.
  5. “KromHC can never produce a complex eigenvalue.” Too broad. The real-only result follows for \(2\times2\) doubly stochastic factors. Larger factors change that statement, though Kronecker separability remains.
  6. “go-mHC with \(s=2\) covers the whole Birkhoff polytope.” Unsupported. The paper reports denser sampled spectral reach at small \(d\), not finite-\(s\) surjectivity.
  7. “A spectral histogram proves better language modeling.” No. It measures a surrogate on a specified experiment; task value requires task evidence.
  8. “Average the eigenvalues across layers.” Eigenvalues do not compose that way. Ordered matrix products are less accommodating.

Try it

1. Send a leftover around the table

For the three-cycle \(P\), compute \(P^2(2,-1,4)^\top\) and \(P^3(2,-1,4)^\top\).

Answer

Since \(P(a,b,c)^\top=(c,a,b)^\top\), \(P^2(2,-1,4)^\top=(-1,4,2)^\top\), and \(P^3(2,-1,4)^\top=(2,-1,4)^\top\).

2. Predict the blend without multiplying

For the matrix \(M\) above and \(x_0=(0,1,0)^\top\), find \(x_3\) from the closed form.

Answer

Here \(u=(1/3,1/3,1/3)^\top\) and \(x_0-u=(-1/3,2/3,-1/3)^\top\). Thus

\[ x_3=u+4^{-3}(x_0-u) =(0.328125,\;0.34375,\;0.328125)^\top. \]

3. Read a lazy cycle

Let \(L_\alpha=(1-\alpha)I_3+\alpha P\), with \(0\le\alpha\le1\). Find its eigenvalues. What happens at \(\alpha=0\), \(1/2\), and \(1\)?

Answer

Because \(I_3\) and \(P\) share eigenvectors,

\[ \operatorname{spec}(L_\alpha) =\{1,\;1-\alpha+\alpha\omega,\;1-\alpha+\alpha\bar\omega\}. \]

At \(\alpha=0\), every leftover is fixed. At \(\alpha=1/2\), disagreement rotates with magnitude \(1/2\), so it decays. At \(\alpha=1\), the exact period-three cycle returns.

4. Listen to a tiled mixer

Let

\[ A=\begin{pmatrix}0.8&0.2\\0.2&0.8\end{pmatrix},\qquad B=\begin{pmatrix}0.7&0.3\\0.3&0.7\end{pmatrix}. \]

Find the eigenvalues of \(A\otimes B\). Can this router have a three-cycle eigenvalue?

Answer

\(A\) has eigenvalues \(1,0.6\), and \(B\) has \(1,0.4\). Pairwise products give

\[ \operatorname{spec}(A\otimes B)=\{1,0.6,0.4,0.24\}. \]

All are real, so neither \(e^{2\pi i/3}\) nor its conjugate is present. This particular all-\(2\times2\) Kronecker router cannot implement a three-cycle.

5. Audit a reach claim

A paper samples \(10^5\) target eigenvalues for \(d=4\). Its router reaches more histogram bins than a baseline. Which of the following follow: (a) greater sampled spectral reach; (b) full coverage of \(\mathsf{B}_4\); (c) better performance on every task; (d) evidence that the optimizer can reach more tested spectral targets?

Answer

Only (a) and (d) follow, under the reported protocol. Spectrum is not an injective description of a matrix, finite sampling cannot prove full polytope coverage, and a surrogate experiment cannot establish universal task performance.

Check yourself

  1. Both \(P\) and \(M\) are doubly stochastic. What does one do to \(e_1\), and what does the other do?
  2. After you split a leftover into its mean and its disagreement, which part can an honest mixer move?
  3. Why does a complex eigenvalue not require complex leftover copies?
  4. Why is a Karpelevič plot only an outer map for mHC routing?
  5. When is it fair to say a KromHC router has only real eigenvalues?
  6. What did the go-mHC spectral experiments show, and what did they not show?
Answers
  1. \(P^k e_1\) cycles forever through the three standard basis vectors. \(M^k e_1\to\tfrac13\mathbf{1}\).
  2. The mean rides through unchanged. Spectral dynamics live in the disagreement subspace \(\mathcal{D}\).
  3. Nonreal eigenvalues of a real table come in conjugate pairs and describe a real two-dimensional rotation-and-scale.
  4. \(\Theta_d\) is defined for all row-stochastic tables. Doubly stochastic tables are a subset, so their possible eigenvalues are a subset of \(\Theta_d\).
  5. When every factor is \(2\times2\). Larger factors can carry complex eigenvalues; Kronecker separability remains either way.
  6. Denser sampled spectral reach than the tested all-\(2\times2\) KromHC baseline at small \(d\) and \(s\). Not finite-\(s\) coverage of \(\mathsf{B}_d\), not large-model long cycles, and not a universal accuracy gain.

Look at a residual router and separate four questions: which leftover is stationary, which disagreements shrink, which leftovers alternate or rotate, and whether those conclusions concern one fixed table, an ordered depth product, or a reported experiment.

Both \(P\) and \(M\) are exactly doubly stochastic. Their different leftover walks are their different spectra.

For KromHC, say “real-only” only when the factor configuration justifies it, especially the all-\(2\times2\) case. For go-mHC, say “broader observed spectral reach at tested small \(d\) and \(s\)” rather than “full Birkhoff coverage at finite \(s\).” Precision is shorter than the correction notice.

Sources

  • [R002] Z. Xie et al., “mHC: Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections,” arXiv:2512.24880, 2025. https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.24880
  • [R004] W. Zhou, Y. Gu, G. Iacovides, and D. Mandic, “KromHC: Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections with Kronecker-Product Residual Matrices,” arXiv:2601.21579v2, 2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.21579
  • [R005] T. Dandachi and S. Diggs-Galligan, “go-mHC: Direct Parameterization of Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections via Generalized Orthostochastic Matrices,” arXiv:2604.02309v1, 2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02309
  • [R035] F. I. Karpelevich, “On the Characteristic Roots of Matrices with Nonnegative Elements,” Izvestiya Akademii Nauk SSSR, Seriya Matematicheskaya 15(4), 361–383, 1951. https://m.mathnet.ru/eng/im3317
  • [R036] C. R. Johnson and P. Paparella, “A Matricial View of the Karpelevič Theorem,” Linear Algebra and its Applications 520, 1–15, 2017, arXiv:1611.06970. https://arxiv.org/abs/1611.06970
  • [R029] I. Nechita, Z. Ouyang, and A. Szczepanek, “Generalized Unistochastic Matrices,” arXiv:2310.03436, 2023. https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.03436

Chapter 18 — A curved space of legal mixers

You have leftover copies of a sentence table — streams — and you want to remix them. The remixing table has to be honest both ways: no negative weights, each new stream a complete recipe, each old stream spent exactly once.

The legal tables do not fill a flat box of numbers. They sit on a curved surface inside that box. You can slide from one legal mixer to a nearby legal mixer. You cannot wander off the surface without breaking a recipe.

We are going to walk that surface. First with a picture. Then with names for the surface and for the allowed velocities. Then with the particular path go-mHC uses: a tiny-turn description, a rotation, a cabinet of block energies.

Mixers that refuse to be a flat sheet

Imagine walking on Earth’s surface. Latitude and longitude are two ordinary numbers, but adding ten degrees of longitude does not move you the same physical distance near the equator and near a pole. The surface is locally flat enough for a map. Globally it is not one flat sheet.

The legal mixers are like that. A \(d\times d\) table of ordinary numbers has \(d^2\) slots. Honesty both ways carves those slots down to a curved set. Around any one legal table the set still looks like a small flat neighborhood. Far away it may fold, stop at a face, or need another map.

A space that looks like ordinary Euclidean space around each point, even if the whole object curves or needs several coordinate maps, is a manifold. The name is worth keeping because the dimension that matters is the number of independent local directions, not the number of ambient coordinates used to store a point.

A car on a road can point along the road at an instant. A velocity straight through the asphalt is not allowed. That allowed instantaneous velocity is a tangent vector. All such velocities at one point form a tangent space. The name is worth keeping because the collection is linear even when the road curves.

Why here? An orthogonal matrix \(Q\in\mathbb R^{N\times N}\) must obey \(Q^\top Q=I_N\). Its \(N^2\) entries therefore cannot move independently. Tangent directions tell an optimizer how to move without immediately leaving the constraint.

A constraint can be satisfied in two rather different ways. We can optimize an arbitrary matrix and repeatedly repair it, or we can choose coordinates whose outputs obey the constraint automatically. mHC uses the first broad idea through finite Sinkhorn normalization. go-mHC uses the second: skew coordinates produce an orthogonal matrix, whose block energies produce a doubly stochastic matrix [R005]; [R029].

That switch changes more than numerical error. It changes which routers are reachable, how many parameter directions describe the same router, and how gradients are warped before they reach the router logits. “Both methods output nearly the same kind of matrix” is therefore not an optimization analysis. It is barely an introduction.

Exact constraint satisfaction asks whether every produced \(H\) lies in \(\mathcal B_d\). Coverage asks whether every member of \(\mathcal B_d\) can be produced. go-mHC proves the first for every \(s\); it does not obtain the second at any fixed finite \(s\) when \(d\ge3\).

All claims below are labeled. Results about go-mHC are especially recent and should not be treated as settled architectural law.

A tiny turn, then a finished rotation

An orthogonal matrix preserves lengths. In two dimensions, rotations and mirror reflections both do so. Rotations have determinant \(+1\); reflections have determinant \(-1\). These two types form disconnected pieces: a continuously moving rigid frame cannot turn into its mirror image without becoming singular somewhere.

A tiny rotation is easier to describe than a finished one. In two dimensions, a single scalar \(t\) produces the skew-symmetric matrix

\[ A(t)= \begin{bmatrix} 0&-t\\ t&0 \end{bmatrix} \in\mathbb R^{2\times2}. \]

Its transpose is its negative. The off-diagonal signs encode the opposition required for a turn: one coordinate leans into the other while the other leans back. Skew matrices are precisely the instantaneous directions of orthogonal motion at the identity.

The Cayley transform converts such a tiny-turn description into a finite orthogonal transformation using additions, multiplications, and a linear solve. Think of it as a rational map from a flat coordinate sheet onto most, but not all, of the rotation surface. It is a map, not a learned module; the learned router emits its input coordinates.

Two streams, one knob

Take \(d=2\) streams and \(s=1\), so \(N=ds=2\). Set \(t=1\) in the skew matrix above. Define \(I_2\in\mathbb R^{2\times2}\) as the identity and use the go-mHC Cayley convention

\[ Q=(I_2-A)(I_2+A)^{-1}. \]

A direct calculation gives

\[ Q= \begin{bmatrix} 0&1\\ -1&0 \end{bmatrix} \in\mathbb R^{2\times2}, \qquad Q^\top Q=I_2, \qquad \det Q=1. \]

For \(s=1\), each block \(Q_{ij}\in\mathbb R^{1\times1}\) is one scalar. The generalized orthostochastic map squares it:

\[ H=\Phi_{2,1}(Q)= \begin{bmatrix} Q_{11}^2&Q_{12}^2\\ Q_{21}^2&Q_{22}^2 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 0&1\\ 1&0 \end{bmatrix} \in\mathbb R^{2\times2}. \]

Thus a quarter-turn in the hidden orthogonal space becomes a stream swap. More generally,

\[ Q(t)=\frac{1}{1+t^2} \begin{bmatrix} 1-t^2&2t\\ -2t&1-t^2 \end{bmatrix}, \]

and \(H(t)\) has diagonal entry \(((1-t^2)/(1+t^2))^2\) and off-diagonal entry \((2t/(1+t^2))^2\). Each row and column sums to one.

This example also warns us about optimization. At \(t=0\), \(Q=I_2\) and \(H=I_2\). For small \(t\), the off-diagonal routing weight is approximately \(4t^2\), not \(4t\). The first derivative at zero vanishes. Exact geometry has not abolished flat spots; it has merely chosen rather elegant ones.

Neighborhoods that look like ordinary space

The Earth picture is enough to walk with. The next two sentences pin the words so later counts have something to attach to.

A smooth \(k\)-dimensional manifold is a space that can be covered by neighborhoods with smooth coordinates in \(\mathbb R^k\), with smooth changes of coordinates where neighborhoods overlap. The number \(k\) is its intrinsic dimension.

Let \(M\) be a smooth manifold and \(p\in M\). A tangent vector at \(p\) can be represented as the velocity \(\gamma'(0)\) of a smooth curve \(\gamma\) lying in \(M\) with \(\gamma(0)=p\). All such velocities form the tangent space \(T_pM\).

The Birkhoff polytope is not a smooth manifold everywhere: vertices and faces have corners. Its relative interior is a smooth open subset of its affine hull, while its boundary is better treated as a manifold with corners or a stratified set. Calling the entire polytope “the Birkhoff manifold” hides this distinction.

The allowed velocities of a rotation

The orthogonal group is

\[ O(N)=\{Q\in\mathbb R^{N\times N}:Q^\top Q=I_N\}. \]

The name “group” means that multiplying orthogonal matrices, taking an inverse, and using the identity all stay in the set. Its two components are separated by determinant. The special orthogonal group is

\[ SO(N)=\{Q\in O(N):\det Q=1\}. \]

It contains rotations and is path-connected for \(N\ge2\). Both \(O(N)\) and \(SO(N)\) have intrinsic dimension

\[ \dim O(N)=\dim SO(N)=\frac{N(N-1)}2. \]

Derivation. Let \(Q(\tau)\in O(N)\) be a smooth curve with \(Q(0)=I_N\), and write \(V=Q'(0)\in\mathbb R^{N\times N}\). Differentiating \(Q(\tau)^\top Q(\tau)=I_N\) at \(\tau=0\) gives

\[ V^\top+V=0. \]

Therefore the tangent space at the identity is the vector space of skew-symmetric matrices.

The Lie algebra of \(SO(N)\) is

\[ \mathfrak{so}(N)=\{A\in\mathbb R^{N\times N}:A^\top=-A\}=T_{I_N}SO(N). \]

A skew matrix has zero diagonal. Its strict upper triangle determines its strict lower triangle, so it has \(N(N-1)/2\) independent coordinates. At a general \(Q\in O(N)\),

\[ T_QO(N)=\{Q\Omega:\Omega\in\mathfrak{so}(N)\}. \]

This is the formal version of attaching a flat sheet of allowed velocities to every point on the curved set.

A map that misses a half-turn

For \(A\in\mathfrak{so}(N)\), define

\[ \operatorname{cay}(A)=(I_N-A)(I_N+A)^{-1}. \]

In numerical code one solves a linear system rather than forming the inverse. For real skew \(A\), \(I_N+A\) is nonsingular: skew matrices have purely imaginary eigenvalues, so \(-1\) cannot be one of them.

Theorem. \(Q=\operatorname{cay}(A)\) is in \(SO(N)\).

Derivation. Because \(A^\top=-A\), transposition swaps \(I_N-A\) and \(I_N+A\). These two polynomials in \(A\) commute, hence

\[ Q^\top Q =(I_N-A)^{-1}(I_N+A)(I_N-A)(I_N+A)^{-1} =I_N. \]

Continuity from \(A=0\), where \(Q=I_N\), places the result in the determinant-\(+1\) component.

The derivative at the origin is \(D\operatorname{cay}_0[\Delta A]=-2\Delta A\), so the map supplies valid local coordinates near \(I_N\). Globally, it reaches exactly those members of \(SO(N)\) without eigenvalue \(-1\). A rotation by \(180^\circ\) therefore sits beyond this chart, approached only as coordinates grow without bound.

“Cayley parameterizes all of \(SO(N)\)” is false. Its range is dense in \(SO(N)\), which is enough for approximation statements but not the same as exact coverage.

Drawers of squared energy

Before writing the map, picture \(Q\in\mathbb R^{N\times N}\) as a \(d\times d\) cabinet. Every drawer \(Q_{ij}\in\mathbb R^{s\times s}\) contains \(s^2\) numbers. We discard their signs and orientations and retain only each drawer’s normalized squared energy.

For \(N=ds\), partition \(Q\in O(N)\) into blocks \(Q_{ij}\in\mathbb R^{s\times s}\), where \(1\le i,j\le d\). Define

\[ \Phi_{d,s}:O(ds)\to\mathbb R^{d\times d}, \qquad \bigl[\Phi_{d,s}(Q)\bigr]_{ij} :=\frac1s\lVert Q_{ij}\rVert_F^2. \]

Let \(\mathbf1_d\in\mathbb R^d\) be the all-ones vector. The Birkhoff polytope is

\[ \mathcal B_d= \{H\in\mathbb R^{d\times d}:H_{ij}\ge0,\ H\mathbf1_d=\mathbf1_d,\ H^\top\mathbf1_d=\mathbf1_d\}. \]

Theorem. For every \(Q\in O(ds)\), \(\Phi_{d,s}(Q)\in\mathcal B_d\) [R005]; [R029].

Derivation. Squared norms are nonnegative. Orthogonality gives, for each block row \(i\),

\[ \sum_{j=1}^d Q_{ij}Q_{ij}^\top=I_s. \]

Taking traces and dividing by \(s\) makes row \(i\) sum to one. Applying the same argument to \(Q^\top Q=I_N\) makes every column sum to one.

None of these \(N\times N\) tables adds a token, a feature, or a stream. The stored stream state is still \(x_l\in\mathbb R^{B\times d\times L\times D}\), with batch size \(B\), stream axis \(d\), sequence length \(L\), and feature size \(D\). The paper’s stream-first display is an algebraic view of each batch slice. The new matrices construct only the \(d\times d\) persistent-state router.

Counting directions

A \(d\times d\) matrix has \(d^2\) entries. Its \(d\) row-sum and \(d\) column-sum equations contain one redundancy because total row mass equals total column mass. The affine hull of \(\mathcal B_d\) therefore has dimension

\[ \dim\mathcal B_d=d^2-(2d-1)=(d-1)^2. \]

At an interior point, its tangent space is

\[ \{Z\in\mathbb R^{d\times d}:Z\mathbf1_d=0,\ Z^\top\mathbf1_d=0\}. \]

Because \(\Phi_{d,s}\) is smooth, the local dimension of its image cannot exceed either source or target:

\[ \dim_{\mathrm{local}}\operatorname{im}\Phi_{d,s} \le \min\!\left\{\frac{ds(ds-1)}2,(d-1)^2\right\}. \]

For \(s=1\), this yields \(d(d-1)/2\) versus \((d-1)^2\). When \(d\ge3\), the deficit is

\[ (d-1)^2-\frac{d(d-1)}2=\frac{(d-1)(d-2)}2>0. \]

Theorem. Thus \(\Phi_{d,1}\) is not surjective onto \(\mathcal B_d\) for \(d\ge3\). The exceptional case \(d=2\) is fully covered, as the numerical example already suggested.

For \(s\ge2\), the raw source dimension is usually larger than \((d-1)^2\), so parameter counting alone cannot prove coverage. It also overcounts meaningful directions. If \(L=\operatorname{diag}(L_1,\ldots,L_d)\) and \(R=\operatorname{diag}(R_1,\ldots,R_d)\), with every \(L_i,R_j\in O(s)\), then

\[ \Phi_{d,s}(LQR)=\Phi_{d,s}(Q). \]

These within-block changes of basis are gauge symmetries: different orthogonal matrices represent the same router. They create flat fibers and null directions in the differential. Away from stabilizers, the corresponding group has dimension \(d\,s(s-1)\); this is a useful generic redundancy count, not a theorem that every fiber has exactly that dimension.

The differential makes the local geometry concrete. For a tangent perturbation \(\Delta Q=Q\Omega\in T_QO(N)\),

\[ \bigl[D\Phi_{d,s}(Q)[\Delta Q]\bigr]_{ij} =\frac{2}{s}\langle Q_{ij},\Delta Q_{ij}\rangle_F. \]

Its output automatically has zero row and column sums. Its rank can drop at singular points, so the image need not itself be one smooth manifold. It can have folds, self-overlaps, boundary strata, and changing fiber dimensions. “The \(s\)-orthostochastic manifold” is convenient shorthand, not a license to ignore those features.

What a finite block size can cover

Theorem (ordinary level). At \(s=1\) and \(d\ge3\), the orthostochastic image \(\Phi_{d,1}(O(d))\) is a proper subset of \(\mathcal B_d\) [R030].

That statement must not be silently promoted to every larger finite block size. The generalized-unistochastic source used here establishes an asymptotic density result and explicitly leaves finer fixed-level structure as a separate problem [R029]. Thus, for an arbitrary named finite \(s>1\), these notes assert exact containment \(\Phi_{d,s}(O(ds))\subseteq\mathcal B_d\), not strictness and not surjectivity. Sampling, spectral plots, and favorable optimization runs cannot decide equality of these sets.

Theorem. The closure of the union over block sizes is the full polytope:

\[ \overline{\bigcup_{s\ge1}\Phi_{d,s}(O(ds))}=\mathcal B_d. \]

This follows from the density theorem for generalized unistochastic matrices together with their real embedding at doubled block size [R029]. It says that every doubly stochastic matrix can be approximated arbitrarily closely at some sufficiently large level. It does not say that the levels are nested, that approximation error decreases monotonically for every optimizer, or that \(s=2\) is universally sufficient.

What the network actually computes

For one go-mHC routing instance, the learned projection produces \(N(N-1)/2\) coordinates, assembled into \(A_l\in\mathfrak{so}(N)\). The architecture then computes

\[ Q_l=\operatorname{cay}(A_l)\in SO(N), \qquad H_l^{\mathrm{res}}=\Phi_{d,s}(Q_l)\in\mathcal B_d, \]

before applying

\[ x_{l+1} =H_l^{\mathrm{res}}x_l +(H_l^{\mathrm{post}})^\top F(H_l^{\mathrm{pre}}x_l,W_l). \]

The multiplication \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}x_l[b]\) contracts only the stream axis of each \(d\times L\times D\) batch slice. Stored together, the result retains shape \(B\times d\times L\times D\).

Empirical result. The go-mHC paper reports faster convergence on its synthetic stream-mixing tasks and similar convergent behavior among constrained variants in a 30M-parameter TinyStories model; it explicitly limits broad performance conclusions because of experimental scale [R005]. These observations do not establish a general optimization theorem.

Derivation. At \(Q=I_N\), every off-diagonal block is zero and every diagonal block is \(I_s\). A tangent perturbation has skew diagonal blocks, orthogonal to \(I_s\) under the Frobenius inner product. Hence

\[ D\Phi_{d,s}(I_N)=0. \]

Near \(A=0\), \(Q=I_N-2A+O(\lVert A\rVert^2)\), so off-diagonal router entries grow quadratically:

\[ H_{ij}=\frac{4}{s}\lVert A_{ij}\rVert_F^2+O(\lVert A\rVert^3), \qquad i\ne j. \]

A loss depending on \(A\) only through \(H\) therefore has zero first derivative at exactly \(A=0\). Initialization and other coupled paths matter.

Interpretation. The map’s gauge symmetries create many parameter-space directions that do not change \(H\). The Cayley chart also compresses very large coordinates as it approaches rotations with eigenvalue \(-1\); in the two-dimensional example, the rotation angle is \(-2\arctan t\), whose derivative has magnitude \(2/(1+t^2)\).

Hypothesis. Compared with a softmax over permutation-mixture weights, Cayley coordinates may avoid some exponential saturation and may provide smoother paths between many routers. Conversely, their fibers, singular points, quadratic identity behavior, and chart boundary may slow particular transitions. Which effect dominates depends on initialization, target router, optimizer, \(d\), \(s\), and data. The reported synthetic advantage in [R005] is evidence for tested settings, not proof of this mechanism.

A permutation-mixture parameterization has different geometry. It maps simplex weights \(\alpha_r\ge0\), \(\sum_r\alpha_r=1\), to \(H=\sum_r\alpha_rP_r\). This is linear in \(\alpha\), globally convex in router space, and surjective when all \(d!\) permutations are available; but the representation is highly redundant and a softmax coordinate map can saturate near simplex faces. Neither coordinate system is simply “more geometric.” Both impose geometry; they impose different geometry.

What the slogans get wrong

Without the manifold picture, one may mistake \(N^2\) stored entries for \(N^2\) trainable freedoms, or mistake \(N(N-1)/2\) skew coordinates for the dimension of the final router set. Without tangent spaces, the skew condition looks like decorative antisymmetry rather than the precise infinitesimal orthogonality constraint.

Without the distinction between \(O(N)\) and \(SO(N)\), the Cayley range is overstated. Without the chart caveat, an optimizer approaching a missing \(-1\) eigenvalue can appear mysteriously ill-conditioned. Without studying \(\Phi\), exact membership in \(\mathcal B_d\) is easily confused with finite-\(s\) surjectivity.

Most importantly, omitting image and fiber geometry invites causal stories unsupported by evidence: “manifold coordinates train faster,” “more source parameters mean full coverage,” or “no softmax means no vanishing gradients.” Mathematics is useful partly because it ruins excellent slogans before they reach production.

Ways to get this wrong

  1. Calling all of \(\mathcal B_d\) a smooth manifold. Its relative interior is smooth in an affine space; its boundary has faces and corners.
  2. Equating skew matrices with orthogonal matrices. \(A\in\mathfrak{so}(N)\) is a tangent coordinate; \(Q=\operatorname{cay}(A)\in SO(N)\) is the finite transformation.
  3. Saying Cayley covers \(SO(N)\). It misses matrices with eigenvalue \(-1\), although its range is dense.
  4. Counting \(N(N-1)/2\) as output dimension. \(\Phi\) discards within-block orientation and lands in a space of dimension only \((d-1)^2\).
  5. Inferring surjectivity from enough parameters. Source dimension at least target dimension is necessary for full-dimensional image, not sufficient for surjectivity.
  6. Confusing finite-\(s\) exactness with finite-\(s\) coverage. Every output is exactly doubly stochastic; some doubly stochastic targets are absent.
  7. Claiming larger \(s\) gives nested images. The density theorem concerns the closure of a union; it does not supply simple nesting for every pair of levels.
  8. Treating optimization explanations as theorems. Convergence speed and gradient conditioning require analysis or experiments beyond set membership.

Try it

1. Allowed velocities at the identity

Let \(V=\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\c&e\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb R^{2\times2}\). Impose the first-order orthogonality condition at \(I_2\) and find all allowed \(V\).

Answer

Differentiating \(Q^\top Q=I_2\) gives \(V^\top+V=0\). Thus \(a=e=0\) and \(c=-b\), so \(V=\begin{bmatrix}0&b\\-b&0\end{bmatrix}\). There is one independent tangent direction, matching \(2(2-1)/2=1\).

2. A dimension ledger

Compute the dimensions of \(SO(ds)\) and \(\mathcal B_d\) for \((d,s)=(3,1)\) and \((3,2)\). What can dimension alone prove?

Answer

For \((3,1)\), \(N=3\), so \(\dim SO(3)=3\) while \(\dim\mathcal B_3=4\); surjectivity is impossible. For \((3,2)\), \(N=6\), so \(\dim SO(6)=15\) while the target dimension remains \(4\). The obstruction disappears, but dimension alone cannot prove surjectivity.

3. A gauge that does not change the mixer

For \(d=2,s=2\), let \(L=\operatorname{diag}(L_1,L_2)\in O(4)\), where \(L_1,L_2\in O(2)\). Show that \(\Phi_{2,2}(LQ)=\Phi_{2,2}(Q)\).

Answer

Block \((i,j)\) changes from \(Q_{ij}\in\mathbb R^{2\times2}\) to \(L_iQ_{ij}\). Orthogonal left multiplication preserves Frobenius norm: \(\lVert L_iQ_{ij}\rVert_F=\lVert Q_{ij}\rVert_F\). Every output entry is therefore unchanged.

4. A missing mixer at a finite level

Let \(d=3\), let \(P=I_3\), and let \(R\in\mathbb R^{3\times3}\) be the permutation matrix of the cycle \(1\to2\to3\to1\). At \(s=2\), can \(H=0.7P+0.3R\) belong to \(\Phi_{3,2}(O(6))\)?

Answer

No. On this segment, level-\(s\) generalized unistochastic points have mixture weight \(k/s\). At \(s=2\), the only weights are \(0,1/2,1\). Since generalized orthostochastic matrices form a subset, \(0.3\) is unreachable at this level.

5. Why the identity is flat

Use \([D\Phi(Q)[\Delta Q]]_{ij}=2\langle Q_{ij},\Delta Q_{ij}\rangle_F/s\) to prove \(D\Phi(I_{ds})=0\) for tangent \(\Delta Q\).

Answer

At identity, off-diagonal blocks \(Q_{ij}\) vanish, so their inner products vanish. Diagonal blocks equal \(I_s\). Tangency makes each diagonal block \(\Delta Q_{ii}\) skew-symmetric, hence \(\langle I_s,\Delta Q_{ii}\rangle_F=\operatorname{tr}(\Delta Q_{ii})=0\). Every differential entry is zero.

Check yourself

  1. What is \(\mathfrak{so}(N)\), and why is it not the same object as an orthogonal matrix?
  2. Does Cayley hit every rotation? What does it miss?
  3. What does \(\Phi_{d,s}\) guarantee about every output \(H\), and what does it not claim at a named finite \(s>1\)?
  4. Why is \(N(N-1)/2\) the wrong number for the dimension of the final router set?
  5. What does the union-over-\(s\) density theorem say, and what three things does it not say?
  6. The identity has a vanishing differential. Why does that matter for training, and why is it not an optimization theorem?
Answers
  1. \(\mathfrak{so}(N)\) is the tangent space of skew directions at the identity. A skew table is a coordinate; \(Q=\operatorname{cay}(A)\) is the finite rotation.
  2. No. It maps smoothly into the part of \(SO(N)\) without eigenvalue \(-1\). A \(180^\circ\) rotation sits beyond the chart.
  3. Every output lies exactly in \(\mathcal B_d\). At a named finite \(s>1\) these notes assert containment, not strictness and not surjectivity. At \(s=1\) and \(d\ge3\), the image is a proper subset.
  4. \(\Phi\) discards within-block orientation. The target has dimension \((d-1)^2\), and the source coordinates contain gauge redundancy.
  5. The closure of the union over all block sizes is the full polytope. It does not say the levels are nested, that approximation error decreases monotonically for every optimizer, or that \(s=2\) is universally sufficient.
  6. A loss that sees \(A\) only through \(H\) has zero first derivative at \(A=0\). That is geometry of this chart. Convergence speed and gradient conditioning still need analysis or experiments; the synthetic advantage in [R005] is evidence for tested settings, not a general theorem.

Sources

  • [R002] Z. Xie et al., “Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections,” arXiv:2512.24880, 2025. https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.24880
  • [R005] T. Dandachi and S. Diggs-Galligan, “go-mHC: Direct Parameterization of Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections via Generalized Orthostochastic Matrices,” arXiv:2604.02309v1, 2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02309
  • [R026] K. Helfrich, D. Willmott, and Q. Ye, “Orthogonal Recurrent Neural Networks with Scaled Cayley Transform,” arXiv:1707.09520, 2018. https://arxiv.org/abs/1707.09520
  • [R027] M. Lezcano-Casado and D. Martínez-Rubio, “Cheap Orthogonal Constraints in Neural Networks: A Simple Parametrization of the Orthogonal and Unitary Group,” Proceedings of Machine Learning Research 97, 2019. https://proceedings.mlr.press/v97/lezcano-casado19a.html
  • [R029] I. Nechita, Z. Ouyang, and A. Szczepanek, “Generalized unistochastic matrices,” arXiv:2310.03436v2, 2023. https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.03436
  • [R031] P.-A. Absil, R. Mahony, and R. Sepulchre, Optimization Algorithms on Matrix Manifolds, Princeton University Press, 2008. https://press.princeton.edu/books/hardcover/9780691132983/optimization-algorithms-on-matrix-manifolds

Chapter 19 — How many numbers, how much multiply

How many numbers, how much multiply. That is the whole question, and it is a question you can hold in one hand.

You already have the leftover-copy mixer. A short list of free numbers becomes a table that is nonnegative and sums to one both ways. The last few chapters named the path: pack the list into a skew table, turn it into a rotation, cut the rotation into blocks, and square the blocks.

That path is exact on paper. Software is not paper. Transpose the wrong two axes and the tensor still has the advertised size. Forget to square and you still get numbers. Forget to say how many times you solve the same little system, and an eight-by-eight solve that looked cheap becomes thousands of them.

Five names that are not the same

A batch of leftover copies is a four-axis box

\[ x \in \mathbb{R}^{B \times d \times L \times D}. \]

\(B\) is how many examples sit in the batch. \(d\) is how many leftover copies of the sentence table you are carrying. \(L\) is how long the sentence is. \(D\) is how wide each word’s feature list is.

The mixer we are about to build does not live on those four axes. It is cut from a larger rotation whose side is

\[ N = ds, \]

where \(s\) is the block size from the last two chapters. \(N\) is not sequence length. In the usual teaching case \(d=4\), \(s=2\), the rotation is merely \(8\times 8\). It is not a stealth attention matrix wearing a false moustache.

Keep the five names separate in every shape comment, or one of them will steal another’s seat.
