A triangle of free numbers

A skew-symmetric table \(A \in \mathbb{R}^{N \times N}\) obeys \(A^\top = -A\). The diagonal is forced to zero: a number that equals its own negative has nowhere else to go. Choose an entry above the diagonal and you have already chosen the entry below it, with the sign flipped.

So we do not store the whole square. We store a list

\[ u \in \mathbb{R}^{p}, \qquad p = \frac{N(N-1)}{2}. \]

The Cayley transform turns \(A\) into an orthogonal table \(Q \in \mathbb{R}^{N \times N}\). We then view \(Q\) as a \(d \times d\) grid of \(s \times s\) blocks. Squared Frobenius sizes throw away the signs inside each block and keep only how much orthogonal energy moved between block rows and columns.

Orthogonality gives every block row and every block column the same total squared energy. Dividing by \(s\) turns that energy into unit row and column sums. No alternating row/column repair loop is required.

“Direct” is not “free.” The dense solve is cubic in \(N = ds\). The projection that emits the \(p\) coordinates has its own feature-dependent cost. And a bug on the first arrow of

\[ \text{coordinates}\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow Q\longrightarrow H^{\mathrm{res}} \]

can still produce a tensor of the right size. A bug on the last arrow can still produce nonnegative numbers. Shape checks do not defend the contract. We will need tests that watch the invariants.

The go-mHC paper is a recent preprint. Its cost and training numbers are reported evidence, not settled architecture law [R005].

Two streams, one number

Take \(d=2\), \(s=1\), and hence \(N=2\). There is only

\[ p = \frac{2(2-1)}{2} = 1 \]

free coordinate. Choose \(u = [1/2]\) and pack it:

\[ A = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1/2 \\ -1/2 & 0 \end{pmatrix} \in \mathbb{R}^{2 \times 2}. \]

The Cayley transform is

\[ Q = (I_N - A)(I_N + A)^{-1}. \]

In software we do not form that inverse. \(I_N - A\) and \(I_N + A\) are polynomials in the same table, so they commute, and we solve

\[ (I_N + A) Q = I_N - A \]

instead. For this \(A\) the result is

\[ Q = \begin{pmatrix} 0.6 & -0.8 \\ 0.8 & 0.6 \end{pmatrix}. \]

It is orthogonal: \(Q^\top Q = I_2\). Since \(s=1\), every block is one scalar. Squaring those scalars gives the mixer

\[ H^{\mathrm{res}} = \begin{pmatrix} 0.6^2 & (-0.8)^2 \\ 0.8^2 & 0.6^2 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 0.36 & 0.64 \\ 0.64 & 0.36 \end{pmatrix}. \]

Both rows and both columns sum to one. \(Q\) still contains signed rotations. \(H^{\mathrm{res}}\) contains nonnegative leftover-copy weights. They are not interchangeable.

Why the mixer sums to one

If \(A^\top = -A\), then in exact arithmetic the Cayley result \(Q\) is orthogonal, and

\[ H_{ij}^{\mathrm{res}} = \frac{1}{s} \lVert Q_{ij} \rVert_F^2 \]

is doubly stochastic.

Skew symmetry gives \((I+A)^\top = I-A\). The Cayley factors commute, and multiplying through yields \(Q^\top Q = I_N\). Cut \(Q\) into blocks \(Q_{ij} \in \mathbb{R}^{s \times s}\). From \(QQ^\top = I_N\), block row \(i\) obeys

\[ \sum_{j=1}^{d} Q_{ij} Q_{ij}^\top = I_s. \]

Take traces:

\[ \sum_{j=1}^{d} \lVert Q_{ij} \rVert_F^2 = s, \qquad \sum_{j=1}^{d} H_{ij}^{\mathrm{res}} = 1. \]

The same argument on \(Q^\top Q = I_N\) gives

\[ \sum_{i=1}^{d} H_{ij}^{\mathrm{res}} = 1. \]

Every entry is nonnegative because it is a sum of squares. “Exact” describes the mathematical image. Floating-point tests still use tolerances. Silicon has declined the invitation to implement real arithmetic.

The recipe, with every shape named

A teaching summary first reduces the leftover copies

\[ x \in \mathbb{R}^{B \times d \times L \times D} \]

to a per-example list

\[ h \in \mathbb{R}^{B \times C_{\mathrm{in}}}. \]

Averaging over the sentence axis and flattening leftover-copy and feature axes gives \(C_{\mathrm{in}} = dD\). That is a teaching choice, not a claim about every production recipe.

A linear map with

\[ W_{\mathrm{route}} \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times C_{\mathrm{in}}}, \qquad b_{\mathrm{route}} \in \mathbb{R}^{p} \]

then emits

\[ u = h W_{\mathrm{route}}^\top + b_{\mathrm{route}} \in \mathbb{R}^{B \times p}. \]

The rest of the box, with every axis written down:

GO_ROUTER(h [B, C_in], d, s) -> H [B, d, d]
    N <- d*s
    p <- N*(N-1)/2
    u [B, p] <- LINEAR(h [B, C_in])

    A [B, N, N] <- zeros
    scatter u into strict upper triangle of A
    A <- A - transpose_last_two_axes(A)

    I [B, N, N] <- broadcast(identity(N), batch=B)
    # solve (I + A) Q = (I - A); never construct an inverse
    Q [B, N, N] <- SOLVE(I + A, I - A)

    blocks [B, d, d, s, s] <-
        reshape Q as [B, d, s, d, s], then permute to [B, d, d, s, s]
    H [B, d, d] <- SUM(blocks * blocks, axes=(-2,-1)) / s
    return H

Reshaping directly to [B, d, d, s, s] is wrong. The matrix row index splits as (block_row, within_row) and the column index as (block_col, within_col), so the intermediate order is [B, d, s, d, s] and needs a permutation.

How many numbers, how much multiply

The strict upper triangle contains

\[ p(N) = 1 + 2 + \cdots + (N-1) = \frac{N(N-1)}{2} \]

coordinates. A dense factorization and solve costs \(O(N^3) = O(d^3 s^3)\). Forming \(A\), reducing block norms, and writing down \(Q\) cost \(O(N^2)\). With \(R\) independent routing instances those become \(O(R N^3)\) and \(O(R N^2)\).

A bare \(O(N^3)\) tells you the size of one solve. It does not tell you how many solves you asked for. One \(N \times N\) solve per layer is different from one per batch example, and radically different from one per token.

For \(s=2\), the three teaching sizes are \(N = 8\), \(16\), and \(32\):

streams \(d\) \(N=ds\) skew coordinates \(p\) \(N^2\) entries \(N^3\) cubic units paper’s approximate go projection FLOPs
4 8 28 64 512 \(\frac{4864}{3}C\approx1{,}621.3C\)
8 16 120 256 4,096 \(\frac{35840}{3}C\approx11{,}946.7C\)
16 32 496 1,024 32,768 \(\frac{274432}{3}C\approx91{,}477.3C\)

The last column evaluates the paper appendix’s reported expression

\[ \frac{8}{3} C (ds)^3 + 4 C (ds)^2, \]

keeping its channel/count factor \(C\) rather than silently dropping it [R005]. The table is algebra, not a stopwatch. Kernel launch cost, batch occupancy, dtype, and hardware change elapsed time.

The coordinate projection itself has \((C_{\mathrm{in}}+1)p\) parameters and roughly \(2 B C_{\mathrm{in}} p\) multiply-add FLOPs for a batch. In the paper’s full per-layer accounting, the residual projection receives a flattened \(dC\)-wide activation summary, giving

\[ (dC+1)\frac{ds(ds-1)}{2} \]

before the parameters shared with the other Hyper-Connection gates [R005]. So “the skew matrix has \(p\) parameters” is ambiguous. \(p\) is the number of dynamic coordinates emitted per routing instance. The learned linear map that emits them contains many more weights.

The rest of the layer is bigger

A dense Transformer projection over \(B\) sequences, \(L\) tokens, and width \(D\) costs on the order of \(B L D^2\). Attention score and value products add terms on the order of \(B L^2 D\). Leftover-copy mixing

\[ H^{\mathrm{res}} x, \qquad H^{\mathrm{res}} \in \mathbb{R}^{B \times d \times d}, \quad x \in \mathbb{R}^{B \times d \times L \times D}, \]

costs \(O(B L D d^2)\). When \(d^2 \ll D\), that mixing term can be much smaller than a width-\(D\) projection. The dense Cayley solve is also small in absolute dimension at \(N = 8, 16, 32\), but it is less friendly to accelerators than one giant matrix multiply. Small solves may be latency-bound or launch-bound.

Let \(R\) be the number of routers evaluated per layer. Layer-static routing gives \(R=1\). Per-example routing gives \(R=B\). Token-wise routing gives \(R=BL\). The cost is \(O(R N^3)\). Do not infer token-wise routing merely because an equation contains token activations; look at the cited implementation. The code below implements per-example batching on purpose.

A small PyTorch box

The following is pedagogical, executable-in-principle PyTorch. Production code may fuse the summary projection, cache indices more aggressively, pick a solve dtype, or use compiled graph capture. The mathematical path should stay the same.

import torch
from torch import nn


def summarize_streams(x: torch.Tensor, eps: float = 1e-6) -> torch.Tensor:
    """x: [B,d,L,D] -> h: [B,d*D], a pedagogical per-example summary."""
    if x.ndim != 4:
        raise ValueError("x must have shape [B,d,L,D]")
    # RMS-normalize features, then reduce only the sequence axis.
    x_norm = x * torch.rsqrt(x.square().mean(dim=-1, keepdim=True) + eps)
    return x_norm.mean(dim=2).flatten(start_dim=1)


class GoMHCRouter(nn.Module):
    """Per-example go-mHC residual router: [B,C_in] -> [B,d,d]."""

    def __init__(self, in_features: int, d: int, s: int,
                 init_std: float = 1e-3):
        super().__init__()
        if in_features <= 0 or d <= 0 or s <= 0:
            raise ValueError("in_features, d, and s must be positive")
        self.d, self.s = d, s
        self.N = d * s
        self.p = self.N * (self.N - 1) // 2
        self.to_coordinates = nn.Linear(in_features, self.p)

        # Near identity but not exactly at the square-map's stationary point.
        nn.init.normal_(
            self.to_coordinates.weight,
            mean=0.0,
            std=init_std / (in_features ** 0.5),
        )
        nn.init.normal_(self.to_coordinates.bias, mean=0.0, std=init_std)

        row, col = torch.triu_indices(self.N, self.N, offset=1)
        self.register_buffer("upper_row", row, persistent=False)
        self.register_buffer("upper_col", col, persistent=False)
        self.register_buffer("eye", torch.eye(self.N), persistent=False)

    def coordinates_to_router(self, u: torch.Tensor):
        """u: [B,p] -> H: [B,d,d], Q/A: [B,N,N]."""
        if u.ndim != 2 or u.shape[-1] != self.p:
            raise ValueError(f"u must have shape [B,{self.p}]")
        B = u.shape[0]
        A = u.new_zeros(B, self.N, self.N)
        A[:, self.upper_row, self.upper_col] = u
        A = A - A.transpose(-1, -2)

        I = self.eye.to(dtype=u.dtype).expand(B, -1, -1)
        # torch.linalg.solve(L, R) returns X satisfying L @ X = R.
        # Here the two factors commute, so this is the Cayley transform.
        Q = torch.linalg.solve(I + A, I - A)

        blocks = Q.reshape(B, self.d, self.s, self.d, self.s)
        blocks = blocks.permute(0, 1, 3, 2, 4)  # [B,d,d,s,s]
        H = blocks.square().sum(dim=(-1, -2)) / self.s
        return H, Q, A

    def forward(self, h: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
        """h: [B,C_in] -> H: [B,d,d]."""
        return self.coordinates_to_router(self.to_coordinates(h))[0]

There is no explicit inverse. torch.linalg.solve is differentiable with respect to both its coefficient matrix and its right-hand side [R037]. A batched input performs batched solves. It does not need a Python loop.

A skew table has purely imaginary eigenvalues, so \(I+A\) is nonsingular in exact real arithmetic. Large coordinate magnitudes can still worsen numerical conditioning and make Cayley gradients small in some directions. A practical box should watch coordinate scale and constraint residuals, and commonly performs this small solve in at least FP32 rather than trusting an FP16 solve with its life story.

Start near the copy, not on it

At \(A=0\), \(Q=I_N\) and \(H^{\mathrm{res}}=I_d\). That is attractive if you want the leftover copies to pass through. But because \(H\) uses squared block entries, the first derivative of \(H\) with respect to \(A\) is zero exactly at \(A=0\).

The initialization above therefore gives both the projection weights and the bias tiny nonzero noise, scaling the weight standard deviation by \(C_{\mathrm{in}}^{-1/2}\) so unit-scale inputs emit coordinates of roughly init_std scale. It starts near the identity while avoiding the exact stationary point. That is a teaching initialization, not a claim that it reproduces an unpublished production recipe.

Autograd differentiates through coordinate scatter, the solve, the permutation, the squaring, and the reduction. If \(G_H = \partial \mathcal{L} / \partial H \in \mathbb{R}^{B \times d \times d}\), the block-norm stage contributes

\[ (G_Q)_{ij} = \frac{2}{s} (G_H)_{ij} Q_{ij}, \qquad (G_Q)_{ij} \in \mathbb{R}^{B \times s \times s}, \]

which matches the analytical block derivative reported by the paper [R005]. The solve’s backward pass uses linear solves as well. Storing or recomputing its factorization is a framework decision.

Tests that catch a lie

These tests watch observable invariants, not implementation text. Run them in float64 so tight tolerances measure the construction rather than mixed-precision noise.

def test_go_router_shapes_and_invariants():
    torch.manual_seed(7)
    B, d, s, C_in = 3, 4, 2, 11
    router = GoMHCRouter(C_in, d, s, init_std=1e-2).double()
    h = torch.randn(B, C_in, dtype=torch.float64)
    u = router.to_coordinates(h)
    H, Q, A = router.coordinates_to_router(u)

    assert u.shape == (B, 28)
    assert A.shape == Q.shape == (B, 8, 8)
    assert H.shape == (B, 4, 4)
    torch.testing.assert_close(A + A.transpose(-1, -2), torch.zeros_like(A))

    I = torch.eye(8, dtype=torch.float64).expand(B, -1, -1)
    torch.testing.assert_close(
        Q.transpose(-1, -2) @ Q, I, rtol=1e-10, atol=1e-10
    )
    ones = torch.ones(B, d, dtype=torch.float64)
    torch.testing.assert_close(H.sum(dim=-1), ones, rtol=1e-10, atol=1e-10)
    torch.testing.assert_close(H.sum(dim=-2), ones, rtol=1e-10, atol=1e-10)
    assert torch.all(H >= 0)


def test_zero_coordinates_give_identity_router():
    router = GoMHCRouter(in_features=5, d=4, s=2).double()
    u = torch.zeros(2, router.p, dtype=torch.float64)
    H, Q, _ = router.coordinates_to_router(u)
    torch.testing.assert_close(
        Q, torch.eye(8, dtype=torch.float64).expand(2, -1, -1),
        rtol=0.0, atol=0.0
    )
    torch.testing.assert_close(
        H, torch.eye(4, dtype=torch.float64).expand(2, -1, -1),
        rtol=0.0, atol=0.0
    )


def test_gradients_are_finite_and_reach_projection():
    torch.manual_seed(11)
    B, C_in, d, s = 2, 9, 4, 2
    router = GoMHCRouter(C_in, d, s, init_std=1e-2).double()
    h = torch.randn(B, C_in, dtype=torch.float64, requires_grad=True)
    target = torch.randn(B, d, d, dtype=torch.float64)
    loss = (router(h) * target).sum()
    loss.backward()

    for grad in (h.grad, router.to_coordinates.weight.grad,
                 router.to_coordinates.bias.grad):
        assert grad is not None
        assert torch.isfinite(grad).all()
        assert grad.abs().sum() > 0

The zero-coordinate test checks exact identity on purpose. The random-coordinate test checks orthogonality and both stochastic axes. If a block permutation is wrong, at least one stochastic test should fail. If the Cayley solve is transposed incorrectly for a noncommuting replacement, orthogonality should fail. If norms are not squared or not divided by \(s\), the sum tests fail.

Where this box sits

The full Hyper-Connection update is

\[ x_{l+1} = H_l^{\mathrm{res}} x_l + (H_l^{\mathrm{post}})^\top F(H_l^{\mathrm{pre}} x_l, W_l), \]

where \(x_l \in \mathbb{R}^{B \times d \times L \times D}\), \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}} \in \mathbb{R}^{B \times d \times d}\) in our per-example box, and the read/write gates broadcast over sentence and feature axes. The router above constructs only \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\). It does not create leftover copies, evaluate \(F\), or define \(H^{\mathrm{pre}}\) and \(H^{\mathrm{post}}\).

Original mHC applies finite Sinkhorn normalization to residual-routing logits. go-mHC replaces that residual constraint map with the skew–Cayley–block-norm map [R005]; [R002]. The recent go-mHC paper reports batched linear solves and tensor contractions without a custom CUDA kernel [R005]. That is an implementation-advantage claim from that preprint, not a guarantee that every workload or compiler will run faster.

The paper’s approximate comparison gives mHC \(4 C S d^2\) FLOPs for \(S\) Sinkhorn iterations and go-mHC \(\frac{8}{3} C (ds)^3 + 4 C (ds)^2\) [R005]. These formulas expose the regime change. Sinkhorn scales quadratically in \(d\) but repeats \(S\) times. The dense Cayley construction is cubic in \(d\) for fixed \(s\), but it is not iterative. Measured crossover depends on actual batch granularity and kernels.

What you lose if you skip a step

Omit skew symmetrization, and \(Q\) is not guaranteed orthogonal. Omit the solve and substitute a first-order approximation \(I-2A\), and exact orthogonality disappears except at \(A=0\). Omit the square, and \(H\) can contain negative routing weights. Omit division by \(s\), and every row and column sums to \(s\), not one.

Omit batching from the design, and a Python loop may serialize thousands of tiny solves. Conversely, broadcast one \(H\) across a batch that was meant to be dynamic, and the model implements a different router. Performance bugs and semantic bugs occasionally share an apartment.

Omit gradient testing, and an apparently valid identity initialization can freeze the residual router at the square map’s stationary point. Omit column-sum testing, and a merely row-stochastic matrix can pass as doubly stochastic. Finally, omit explicit routing multiplicity from the cost estimate, and a reasonable \(N=8\) solve can become an unreasonable \(BL\) solves without anyone changing the asymptotic notation.

Ways to get this wrong

  1. Calling \(p\) the learned parameter count. It is the emitted coordinate count. A dense projection from \(C_{\mathrm{in}}\) features contains \((C_{\mathrm{in}}+1)p\) learned parameters.
  2. Using an explicit inverse in code. Solve the linear system. It is clearer, more stable, and supported by batched autograd.
  3. Confusing \(N\) with sequence length. \(N=ds\); sequence length is \(L\).
  4. Flattening blocks incorrectly. Reshape to [B,d,s,d,s], then permute.
  5. Testing only nonnegativity. Any squared matrix is nonnegative. Orthogonality and row/column sums carry the contract.
  6. Testing exact real-arithmetic identities with exact floating equality. Use tolerances except for deliberately exact cases such as zero coordinates.
  7. Initializing all coordinates exactly to zero. It returns the identity but gives the squared block map a zero first derivative there.
  8. Claiming cubic automatically means expensive. \(8^3=512\) is small; multiplicity, kernels, and surrounding Transformer work decide runtime.
  9. Claiming cubic automatically means cheap. Token-wise \(BL\) tiny solves can still be expensive. Count instances.
  10. Running the solve in low precision without checking residuals. Validate \(\|Q^\top Q-I\|\), row sums, and column sums under the actual dtype.

Try it

1. Count the coordinates

For \(d=8\), \(s=2\), compute \(N\), \(p\), and the coordinate-projection parameter count when \(C_{\mathrm{in}}=256\).

Answer

\(N=16\), \(p=16\cdot15/2=120\), and the affine projection contains \((256+1)120=30{,}840\) parameters.

2. Find the missing scale

Suppose \(Q\in\mathbb R^{12\times12}\) is split with \(d=4\), \(s=3\), and an implementation sets \(H_{ij}=\|Q_{ij}\|_F^2\). What do rows and columns of \(H\) sum to, and how do you fix it?

Answer

They sum to \(s=3\), because each orthogonal block row/column has total squared Frobenius norm \(3\). Divide every entry by \(s\).

3. Attach multiplicity to complexity

Compare per-layer solve work for per-example and token-wise routing when \(B=8\), \(L=2048\), \(d=4\), \(s=2\). Express both in cubic units \(RN^3\).

Answer

Here \(N=8\) and \(N^3=512\). Per-example routing uses \(R=B=8\): \(4{,}096\) cubic units. Token-wise routing uses \(R=BL=16{,}384\): \(8{,}388{,}608\) units, a factor of \(L=2048\) larger.

4. Diagnose a failed invariant

A router produces nonnegative \(H\) whose rows sum to one, but columns are [0.8, 1.1, 1.0, 1.1]. Name two plausible implementation errors.

Answer

The source \(Q\) may not be orthogonal because \(A\) was not made skew-symmetric or the Cayley system was formed incorrectly. Alternatively, block axes may have been reshaped/permuted incorrectly, preserving accidental row normalization but destroying the block-column argument.

5. Repair initialization

Why can exact zero coordinates stall learning of \(H\), and give one near-identity repair.

Answer

At \(A=0\), \(Q=I\). Off-diagonal blocks start at zero and are squared; diagonal blocks change only at second order under an infinitesimal skew perturbation. Hence \(\partial H/\partial A=0\) there. Initialize the coordinate projection and bias with tiny nonzero noise, then test that gradients reaching both inputs and parameters are finite and nonzero.

Check yourself

  1. What is \(N\), and what is it not?
  2. Why solve \((I+A)Q=I-A\) instead of writing down an inverse?
  3. For \(s=2\) and \(d=4,8,16\), what are the three values of \(N\)?
  4. What does a bare \(O(d^3 s^3)\) forget to count?
  5. Why not initialize every coordinate exactly to zero?
  6. Which two sums must a test check, besides nonnegativity?
Answers
  1. \(N=ds\). It is the side of the Cayley table, not the sentence length \(L\).
  2. \(I-A\) and \(I+A\) commute, the solve is the Cayley transform, and batched autograd already knows how to differentiate a solve [R037].
  3. \(N=8\), \(16\), and \(32\).
  4. Multiplicity \(R\): one solve per layer, per example, or per token. The cost is \(O(R d^3 s^3)\).
  5. At \(A=0\), \(H=I_d\) but \(\partial H/\partial A=0\). Start near the identity, not on it.
  6. Orthogonality of \(Q\), plus both row sums and column sums of \(H\).

The box is small. The surrounding layer is not. Name the tensor, name the axis, name how many solves you asked for, and test the sums both ways.

Sources

  • [R005] T. Dandachi and S. Diggs-Galligan, “go-mHC: Direct Parameterization of Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections via Generalized Orthostochastic Matrices,” arXiv:2604.02309v1, 2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02309
  • [R001] D. Zhu et al., “Hyper-Connections,” arXiv:2409.19606, 2024. https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.19606
  • [R002] Z. Xie et al., “mHC: Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections,” arXiv:2512.24880, 2025. https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.24880
  • [R037] PyTorch documentation, torch.linalg.solve, stable API reference. https://pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.linalg.solve.html
  • [R026] K. Helfrich, D. Willmott, and Q. Ye, “Orthogonal Recurrent Neural Networks with Scaled Cayley Transform,” arXiv:1707.09520, 2018. https://arxiv.org/abs/1707.09520
  • [R038] G. H. Golub and C. F. Van Loan, Matrix Computations, 4th ed., Johns Hopkins University Press, 2013. https://jhupbooks.press.jhu.edu/title/matrix-computations

Chapter 20 — Ten mix-ups

You close the book and try to explain the last layer to a friend. The equation is still in your head. The story you tell about it is not.

You say the extra trays come from a skew matrix. You say each tray is last week’s layer. You say four trays means four Transformer blocks. You say the leftover router is a single frozen matrix. You say any legal leftover matrix can be written exactly, at any block size. You say “rows and columns sum to one” means “the matrix is a rotation.” You say leftover routing is the same gate that picks experts. You say cubic in a small auxiliary size means the method is too expensive to run. You say the routing map is a fat multilayer net. You say the Cayley transform is what learns.

Those ten sentences are the usual wreck. This chapter walks them back, then asks what a paper is still allowed to claim.

The ten sentences, in English

Here they are again, without the algebra. Each one names the wrong object.

  1. The skew matrix is where the extra leftover copies come from.
  2. Each leftover copy is one previous layer, sitting in a rolling cache.
  3. Several leftover copies means several copies of the expensive block.
  4. The leftover router is just one learned matrix, parked once and left alone.
  5. The go-mHC construction can write every legal leftover matrix, at whatever finite block size you picked.
  6. A matrix whose rows and columns sum to one is automatically a rotation.
  7. Leftover routing is the same thing as expert routing.
  8. An auxiliary solve that is cubic in a small size is automatically too expensive.
  9. The map that emits routing numbers is a large multilayer net.
  10. The Cayley transform itself is the learner.

A mathematically exact router can still be a poor model component. A useful measured result does not prove the story told about why it works. Almost every wreck in the list comes from mixing five objects:

  • the leftover copies carried through depth;
  • the small learned map that emits routing numbers;
  • the constraint map that turns those numbers into a legal matrix;
  • the branch that actually transforms token features;
  • the experiment used to score the whole system.

Keep those five on separate hooks. The rest of the chapter is just that habit, once with two numbers, once with the real equation, and once with a paper in your hand.

Two trays, one machine

Take one batch item, one token, one feature, and two leftover copies. Then \(B = m = n = 1\), the stream count is \(d = 2\), and the stored state has shape \(B \times d \times m \times n = 1 \times 2 \times 1 \times 1\). Its only slice is

\[ x_l[0,:,0,0] = \begin{bmatrix} 2 \\ 6 \end{bmatrix} \in \mathbb{R}^{2}. \]

Let the read gate be \(H_l^{\mathrm{pre}} = [0.75,\ 0.25] \in \mathbb{R}^{1 \times 2}\). Contracting only the leftover axis gives

\[ z_l = H_l^{\mathrm{pre}} x_l = 0.75(2) + 0.25(6) = 3 \in \mathbb{R}^{1 \times 1 \times 1}. \]

Let the single branch be \(F(z) = z + 1\), so the note is \(y_l = 4 \in \mathbb{R}^{1 \times 1 \times 1}\). Let the write gate be \(H_l^{\mathrm{post}} = [0.25,\ 0.75] \in \mathbb{R}^{1 \times 2}\). Its transposed write contributes \([1,\ 3]^\top\), stored with shape \(1 \times 2 \times 1 \times 1\).

Choose a leftover router

\[ H_l^{\mathrm{res}} = \begin{bmatrix} 0.75 & 0.25 \\ 0.25 & 0.75 \end{bmatrix} \in \mathbb{R}^{2 \times 2}. \]

Every entry is nonnegative, and every row and column sums to one. Persistent routing gives \([3,\ 5]^\top\). Therefore

\[ x_{l+1} = H_l^{\mathrm{res}} x_l + (H_l^{\mathrm{post}})^\top y_l = \begin{bmatrix} 3 \\ 5 \end{bmatrix} + \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ 3 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 4 \\ 8 \end{bmatrix}. \]

That tiny sum already kills the first three mix-ups, and mix-up 6 as a bonus.

There was one evaluation of \(F\). Neither leftover copy was “the previous layer.” And a matrix whose rows and columns sum to one need not be a rotation. Indeed

\[ H_l^{\mathrm{res}} (H_l^{\mathrm{res}})^\top = \begin{bmatrix} 0.625 & 0.375 \\ 0.375 & 0.625 \end{bmatrix} \neq I_2. \]

The matrix averages leftover copies. It does not preserve every Euclidean length. Row and column mass is not an angle.

Mix-up 1 — the skew matrix is not the trays

The leftover copies are already there. They are axis 1 of the state \(x_l \in \mathbb{R}^{B \times d \times m \times n}\), created when the architecture is built and then carried forward. The skew matrix \(A_l \in \mathbb{R}^{ds \times ds}\) is an auxiliary pad of numbers used later to build the leftover router \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\). It does not invent the trays.

Picture a workshop with several labeled trays that persist from one step to the next. A layer reads a blend of trays, runs one machine on that blend, writes the result into selected trays, and routes the old tray contents forward. The trays are residual streams. The machine is the branch \(F\). The routing coefficients are not the trays, any more than a railway timetable is a train.

A small learned projection emits coordinates. Those coordinates fill a skew-symmetric matrix. A Cayley transform produces an orthogonal auxiliary matrix. Squared Frobenius norms of its blocks produce the leftover router. The auxiliary orthogonal matrix is scaffolding. The network applies the resulting stream router to the persistent state.

The constraint map is a legal-document printer. Learned coordinates supply the contents. The printer enforces the format. The printer does not decide what to write, and it does not secretly become the document.

Mix-up 2 — a tray is not last week’s layer

Streams persist across every layer. They are parallel leftover copies of the whole table, not a rolling cache of the last \(d\) layer outputs.

If you want last week’s layer, you already have it: it is \(x_l\) before this layer writes \(x_{l+1}\). Extra leftover copies do not rename that fact. They add a new axis.

Mix-up 3 — several trays is not several blocks

Not in the central equation. The read gate first combines \(d\) leftover copies into one branch input of shape \(B \times m \times n\), so \(F\) can run once. A deliberately multi-branch extension is a separate design, with a separate bill.

Ordinary residual networks have one tray. Hyper-Connections expose several and learn how to read, write, and propagate them. Unconstrained propagation can amplify or erase state through repeated matrix products. Manifold-constrained Hyper-Connections restrict persistent-state routing to doubly stochastic matrices: nonnegative matrices whose rows and columns each sum to one. mHC-lite, KromHC, and go-mHC use different constructions to obtain such matrices.

Mix-up 4 — the leftover router need not sit still

Hyper-Connection family routers can depend on the current activations. A learned projection reads a normalized or reduced copy of the state and emits routing coordinates. How often that happens — once per layer, once per token, once per channel — is an implementation fact. Do not silently promote a layer-wise router into a token-wise one, or the other way around.

The same warning applies to mix-up 9. The cited formulations use those reduced activations and small learned linear projections. A future implementation may choose a multilayer net. The name of the method does not summon hidden layers.

Mix-up 10 is the same object wearing a fancier hat. The Cayley transform is a deterministic differentiable map. Projection weights learn under backpropagation. Cayley converts their emitted skew coordinates into an orthogonal matrix and lets gradients pass. It does not itself decide what to learn.

Mix-up 5 — legal is not the same as everything

In go-mHC, pick an auxiliary block size \(s \ge 1\) and set \(N = ds\). Learned routing coordinates fill a skew-symmetric matrix

\[ A_l \in \mathbb{R}^{N \times N}, \qquad A_l^\top = -A_l. \]

There are \(N(N-1)/2\) independent coordinates. The Cayley construction produces an auxiliary orthogonal matrix. One paper convention is

\[ Q_l = (I_N - A_l)(I_N + A_l)^{-1} \in \mathbb{R}^{N \times N}. \]

Production code should compute the equivalent result with a linear solve, not an explicit inverse. Partition \(Q_l\) into \(d \times d\) blocks \(Q_{l,ij} \in \mathbb{R}^{s \times s}\) and define

\[ (H_l^{\mathrm{res}})_{ij} = \frac{1}{s} \lVert Q_{l,ij} \rVert_F^2. \]

Orthogonality of \(Q_l\) makes every entry of \(H_l^{\mathrm{res}}\) nonnegative and makes every row and column sum to one [R005]; [R029]. In exact arithmetic the constructed router is exactly doubly stochastic.

That is the whole theorem. For a fixed finite \(s\), the image

\[ \Phi_{d,s}\bigl(O(ds)\bigr) \subseteq \mathsf{B}_d \]

need not equal the entire Birkhoff polytope \(\mathsf{B}_d\). At \(s = 1\), it is the orthostochastic subset. The generalized sets approach full coverage as \(s\) grows in the cited theory [R005]; [R029]. What is established is

\[ \overline{\bigcup_{s \ge 1} \mathcal{S}_{d,s}} = \mathcal{B}_d; \]

adjacent levels need not be nested [R029]. For \(d \ge 3\), the \(s = 1\) image is a proper subset. For an arbitrary named larger finite \(s\), equality or strictness is not established here.

Every output is exactly doubly stochastic. That is not the claim that every doubly stochastic matrix appears. Exact constraint satisfaction and exact set coverage wear annoyingly similar hats.

“Exact” has an object. Ask: exact constraint, exact arithmetic, exact coverage, exact optimizer solution, or exact reproduction? If the noun is missing, so is half the claim.

Exact double stochasticity is a statement about the output of a mathematical construction, up to floating-point error in an implementation. It is not a theorem about final task accuracy, wall-clock speed, stream specialization, or optimization success.

Mix-up 6 — mass is not a rotation

The two-tray matrix above is already a counterexample. In go-mHC an auxiliary \(Q\) is orthogonal; block squared norms of \(Q\) produce a generally non-orthogonal doubly stochastic \(H^{\mathrm{res}}\). Double stochasticity controls row and column mass, not angles.

Mix-up 7 — two gates, two objects

mHC routes persistent leftover copies. Mixture-of-experts routing typically assigns token representations to parameterized experts. The two gates act on different objects. They can sit in the same layer. Their axes, capacity constraints, and compute semantics still differ.

Compute balance, dropped-token behavior, and expert capacity remain mixture-of-experts concerns. Double stochasticity across leftover copies does not solve them.

Mix-up 8 — cubic is a warning, not a bill

In dense go-mHC the auxiliary linear solve scales cubically in \(ds\). Persistent activations scale as \(dBmn\). The skew-coordinate count is \(ds(ds-1)/2\).

Actual overhead depends on those small matrix sizes, how often you emit coordinates, the hardware, memory traffic, kernel quality, and the much larger branch \(F\). Cubic is a warning to measure. It is not a benchmark result. Big-O notation has never paid a GPU invoice.

The same equation, with the axes written down

Let \(d\) be the leftover-copy count, \(B\) the batch size, \(m\) the sequence length, and \(n\) the feature width. These notes use batch-first storage:

\[ x_l \in \mathbb{R}^{B \times d \times m \times n}, \quad H_l^{\mathrm{pre}}, H_l^{\mathrm{post}} \in \mathbb{R}^{1 \times d}, \quad H_l^{\mathrm{res}} \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times d}. \]

The papers often display a stream-first equation. Under batch-first storage, apply it to each batch slice:

\[ x_{l+1}[b] = H_l^{\mathrm{res}} x_l[b] + (H_l^{\mathrm{post}})^\top F\bigl(H_l^{\mathrm{pre}} x_l[b], W_l\bigr). \]

The matrices contract only the leftover axis, axis 1 in storage. They do not mix batch items, sequence positions, or feature coordinates. The branch input and output have shape \(B \times m \times n\). The full persistent state has shape \(B \times d \times m \times n\).

\(H^{\mathrm{pre}}\) reads. \(F\) computes. \(H^{\mathrm{post}}\) writes. \(H^{\mathrm{res}}\) propagates old state. That four-verb split is more useful than memorizing variant names.

The path through one layer is then:

\[ \begin{array}{c} \text{current leftover copies } x_l \in \mathbb{R}^{B \times d \times m \times n} \\ \downarrow \\ \text{small learned routing projection} \\ \downarrow \\ \text{routing coordinates} \\ \downarrow \\ \text{constraint-specific transformation} \\ \downarrow \\ H_l^{\mathrm{pre}},\ H_l^{\mathrm{post}},\ H_l^{\mathrm{res}} \\ \downarrow \\ \text{read, compute, write, and propagate} \end{array} \]

The exact constraint-specific transformation differs. Unconstrained Hyper-Connections use no Birkhoff projection. Original mHC uses finite Sinkhorn normalization. mHC-lite uses convex combinations of permutation matrices. KromHC uses Kronecker-structured factors. go-mHC uses Cayley plus generalized orthostochastic block norms.

What the papers actually measured

[Experiment] The original Hyper-Connections paper reports improvements over residual connections in language-model pretraining and vision experiments [R001].

[Experiment] The mHC paper reports that constraining residual mixing improves stability and scalability in its evaluated regimes [R002].

[Experiment] mHC-lite and KromHC report exact-by-construction alternatives with favorable results in their tested regimes [R003]; [R004].

[Experiment] The go-mHC paper reports synthetic leftover-mixing advantages and similar training behavior among exact mHC variants in a 30M-parameter, six-layer GPT-style experiment. It reports up to \(10\times\) faster convergence on selected synthetic mixing tasks, wider sampled spectral reach than KromHC, and parity — not established superiority — among exact variants in that small language-model study [R005].

These papers are recent. None establishes a universal law of architecture design.

Two further sentences are still guesses:

  • wider spectral reach is useful on large, deep, heterogeneous models;
  • distinct leftover copies spontaneously acquire stable semantic jobs.

A heatmap of routing coefficients shows preference. It does not show a job.

More trays is not more width

The leftover-copy count \(d\) is a genuine architectural degree of freedom. It changes the number of persistent state channels and the space of learnable depth-wise routes. It is therefore reasonable to study it alongside depth, feature width, expert count, and context length.

It is not interchangeable with any of them.

Increasing depth adds sequential branch applications. Increasing width enlarges feature vectors and usually the expensive branch matrices. Increasing expert count adds alternative parameterized computations, often sparsely selected. Increasing context length enlarges the token axis. Increasing \(d\) enlarges persistent state and its routing graph. The standard equation can still evaluate \(F\) once per layer.

Useful scaling in \(d\) would mean that, under controlled compute and parameter budgets, increasing leftover copies improves a measured capability or efficiency frontier. A proper scaling study must sweep \(d\), retune optimization, include activation memory and router time, and compare against spending the same budget on depth, width, context, or experts. The small go-mHC study makes \(d\) plausible as a research axis. It does not establish a scaling law [R005].

Mixing trays is not mixing tokens

When all layers use similar branches and routing stays near identity or gentle averaging, mHC acts primarily as a constrained residual stabilizer. Its main observable role may be preventing extreme propagation while retaining flexible reads and writes.

When different layers or parallel branches perform distinct operations, the same interface begins to look like a learned dataflow substrate: persistent state is routed among computations through depth. That phrase describes topology. It is not evidence of interpretable algorithms.

Possible combinations follow from the interface. They are architectural analyses, not product announcements.

Attention. Let \(F\) be self-attention with input and output \(B \times m \times n\). Read routing selects a leftover mixture for contextual token interaction; write routing distributes the update. This is the closest to the GPT-style validation in [R005]. It does not make leftover routing into attention. Attention mixes token information. \(H^{\mathrm{res}}\) mixes leftover copies.

A state-space model plus attention. Alternate \(F_l^{\mathrm{SSM}}\) and \(F_l^{\mathrm{attn}}\), each preserving \(B \times m \times n\). Hypothesis Leftover copies could preserve different mixtures useful to local recurrence and content-based retrieval. A fair test needs routing probes and interventions, not attractive heatmaps alone.

A WavePDE-like or convolutional operator. Use a sequence or spatial operator as \(F\). Hypothesis Constrained leftover paths may carry information at different effective scales while the operator handles within-layer propagation. No result in the five primary Hyper-Connection papers establishes a general WavePDE advantage.

Mixture of experts. Put the experts inside \(F\): that gate selects parameterized experts for tokens, while Hyper-Connection gates route persistent state. The two gates act on different objects and can coexist. Mix-up 7 again, wearing a wiring diagram.

Memory or Engram-like lookup. Let \(F\) retrieve from an external or learned lookup memory and write the returned \(B \times m \times n\) update into leftover copies. Hypothesis Some leftover copies may become convenient carriers of retrieved state. Proving that requires causal ablation of leftover copies and memory reads.

Several different machines in one layer. A proposed extension can use branches \(F_r\), each with its own read/write pair:

\[ x_{l+1} = H_l^{\mathrm{res}} x_l + \sum_{r=1}^{R} (H_{l,r}^{\mathrm{post}})^\top F_r\bigl(H_{l,r}^{\mathrm{pre}} x_l, W_{l,r}\bigr). \]

Here each branch input and output is \(B \times m \times n\), and the sum restores shape \(B \times d \times m \times n\). Hypothesis Attention, state-space, multilayer, and memory branches might learn compositional routes. This multi-branch equation is an architectural extrapolation, not the evaluated go-mHC block in [R005]. It also evaluates \(R\) branches, so its cost cannot be blamed on the leftover-copy count with a straight face.

What would count as a job

Stream specialization is a reproducible functional asymmetry among leftover copies: removing, swapping, or perturbing copy \(i\) has a systematically different causal effect from doing so to copy \(j\).

Potential roles — local or lexical state, global context, retrieval features, intermediate reasoning state, memory, or modality-specific state — are hypotheses. Evidence should survive leftover-copy permutations, multiple seeds, and matched controls. Router coefficients alone show preference, not semantic function. Because leftover copies may have permutation symmetries, labels such as “stream 2 is memory” can change across training runs while the computation remains equivalent.

If we leave a piece out

Omit persistent leftover copies and the architecture collapses back toward a single residual state. There is no multi-copy route to study.

Omit read/write routing and the branch cannot selectively interface with that state.

Omit leftover routing and each copy mostly carries itself plus writes, reducing learned state-to-state topology.

Omit the constraint in deep unconstrained Hyper-Connections and repeated residual matrices may amplify, decay, or otherwise distort propagation. Learned flexibility alone does not guarantee stability.

Replace the go-mHC block-norm map with raw Cayley output and the router is orthogonal of shape \(ds \times ds\), not a nonnegative \(d \times d\) leftover mixer.

Omit the learned projection and the exact map still produces legal routers, but they are fixed by whatever coordinates remain.

Most importantly, omit the noun after “exact” and mathematical guarantees quietly mutate into empirical promises. “Rows and columns sum to one” becomes “training is stable,” then “reasoning improves,” then a slide deck acquires a rocket. The first implication is proved. The others require experiments.

Try it

1. Name the axes

For \(d = 4\), \(B = 8\), \(m = 512\), and \(n = 1024\), give the shapes of \(x_l\), of \(H^{\mathrm{pre}} x_l\), of \(F\)’s output, and of \((H^{\mathrm{post}})^\top F(\ldots)\).

Answer

\(x_l\) is \(8 \times 4 \times 512 \times 1024\). Contracting axis 1 gives \(8 \times 512 \times 1024\); a shape-preserving \(F\) returns the same; the transposed write restores \(8 \times 4 \times 512 \times 1024\).

2. Legal is not a rotation

Verify that

\[ M = \begin{bmatrix} 0.6 & 0.4 \\ 0.4 & 0.6 \end{bmatrix} \]

is doubly stochastic, then show it is not orthogonal.

Answer

All entries are nonnegative and every row and column sums to one. But

\[ MM^\top = \begin{bmatrix} 0.52 & 0.48 \\ 0.48 & 0.52 \end{bmatrix} \neq I, \]

so it is not orthogonal.

3. Count the coordinates

For go-mHC with \(d = 8\) and \(s = 2\), compute \(N\) and the number of independent skew coordinates. State what this count does not prove.

Answer

\(N = ds = 16\), so there are \(N(N-1)/2 = 120\) coordinates. This does not prove full coverage of \(\mathcal{B}_8\), low wall-clock overhead, or better task performance.

4. Label the claims

Label each statement: (a) the block-norm image of an orthogonal \(Q\) is doubly stochastic; (b) \(s = 2\) is universally optimal; (c) the go-mHC paper found similar small-language-model training loss among exact variants; (d) leftover copies may separate retrieval from lexical state.

Answer
  1. Theorem. (b) Unsupported hypothesis — the paper uses \(s = 2\) as an empirical compromise. (c) Empirical result in a limited regime. (d) Hypothesis until causal specialization evidence exists.

5. Design a test that could fail

Propose a minimal test of “leftover-copy count is a scaling dimension.”

Answer

Sweep several \(d\) values with matched training data and multiple seeds. Compare at matched parameter, FLOP, wall-clock, and activation-memory budgets against width and depth controls; retune learning rates; report task metrics, stability, router time, and uncertainty. A monotone unbudgeted curve is insufficient.

If you remember three versions

Beginner

A residual network carries information around a layer so the layer can modify rather than replace it. Hyper-Connections carry several persistent versions of that residual state.

At each layer, a read gate blends those leftover copies. One computation — perhaps attention or a multilayer net — processes the blend. A write gate distributes the new result back to the copies, while a leftover router moves old contents forward.

The leftover copies are not old layers. They are parallel containers that live through the whole network. Nor does having several copies automatically mean running several Transformer blocks.

Unrestricted routing matrices can repeatedly enlarge or erase information. mHC restricts the leftover router to a well-behaved family of nonnegative matrices whose rows and columns sum to one.

Different mHC variants construct those matrices differently. go-mHC uses learned numbers, a skew-symmetric matrix, an orthogonal intermediate matrix, and block norms. The intermediate machinery creates the router, not the leftover copies.

The exact mathematical guarantee is narrow but valuable: the leftover router satisfies the double-stochastic constraint. Whether a model trains faster or performs better remains an experimental question.

More leftover copies may give a model more ways to preserve and route intermediate state. Whether they become a reliable new scaling dimension or acquire human-readable jobs is not yet settled.

Intermediate

The persistent state is \(x_l \in \mathbb{R}^{B \times d \times m \times n}\). For each batch slice, the layer update is

\[ x_{l+1}[b] = H_l^{\mathrm{res}} x_l[b] + (H_l^{\mathrm{post}})^\top F\bigl(H_l^{\mathrm{pre}} x_l[b], W_l\bigr), \]

with \(H^{\mathrm{pre}}, H^{\mathrm{post}} \in \mathbb{R}^{1 \times d}\) and \(H^{\mathrm{res}} \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times d}\). The operators read, compute, write, and propagate, respectively.

In go-mHC, a learned projection emits \(ds(ds-1)/2\) coordinates, which define skew \(A_l \in \mathbb{R}^{ds \times ds}\). Cayley produces orthogonal \(Q_l\); after partitioning into \(s \times s\) blocks,

\[ (H_l^{\mathrm{res}})_{ij} = \lVert Q_{l,ij} \rVert_F^2 / s. \]

This is doubly stochastic by construction. Fixed finite \(s\) controls a representable subset, not necessarily all of \(\mathcal{B}_d\). Increasing \(d\) expands state and routing but also activation memory, projection output, and solve cost. It is a distinct axis from width, depth, context, and experts.

Expert

go-mHC composes an activation-dependent coordinate map with a Lie-algebra parameterization, a Cayley chart into the special-orthogonal component, and the generalized orthostochastic map \(\Phi_{d,s}\) into \(\mathcal{B}_d\). This turns constrained optimization over residual mixing into unconstrained learning of coordinates followed by an exact feasible map.

The image geometry matters. Double stochasticity fixes a stationary all-ones mode and bounds induced one- and infinity-norm amplification for the residual matrices, but it does not make dynamic products orthogonal or settle nonnormal branch-coupled gradients. At \(s = 1\), generalized orthostochastic reach is a strict subset of \(\mathcal{B}_d\) for \(d \ge 3\). Larger finite \(s\) can add reach, but full finite-\(s\) coverage is not established here and adjacent images need not be nested. Spectral reach is a useful diagnostic, not a complete expressivity metric.

Viewed architecturally, pre/post/res routing creates a learnable graph over persistent state and branch computations. With homogeneous branches it may function chiefly as residual stabilization. With causally verified heterogeneous routes it could become a learned dataflow substrate. Current evidence supports construction exactness and limited-regime performance claims more strongly than scaling laws, semantic specialization, or broad superiority.

How to read the paper

A checklist for arXiv:2604.02309v1. If a paper survives these questions, you understand more than its diagram. If it does not, you have found the next experiment rather than a reason to stop reading.

  1. Version and scope. Is the claim from v1, a revision, code, or a later independent reproduction? The cited experiments are synthetic and small-model-heavy.
  2. Claim label. Is a sentence a definition, theorem, derivation, paper-reported empirical result, interpretation, or hypothesis?
  3. Object and shape. Does “routing” mean \(H^{\mathrm{pre}}\), \(H^{\mathrm{post}}\), or \(H^{\mathrm{res}}\)? Which axes are dynamic, and what are all leading batch and token axes in code?
  4. Exactness versus coverage. Does “exact” mean every output lies in \(\mathcal{B}_d\), or that every point of \(\mathcal{B}_d\) is reachable? At finite \(s\), do not exchange these claims.
  5. Cayley range. Which orthogonal component and matrices are covered by the chosen Cayley chart? Why do block squared norms make some sign and reflection distinctions irrelevant?
  6. Numerics. Is orthogonality obtained with a solve rather than an explicit inverse? What row and column errors appear in the actual dtype and hardware?
  7. Complexity. Are coordinate projection, batching frequency, activation and channel factors, solve constants, memory traffic, and kernel launch costs included — not just \(O(d^3)\)?
  8. Baselines. Are mHC, mHC-lite, and KromHC compared with matched parameters, compute, tuning, and implementation quality?
  9. Expressivity metric. Spectral coverage is a surrogate. Could two router families have similar spectra but different reachable matrices, Jacobians, or task behavior?
  10. Synthetic task. How are targets sampled? Does that distribution favor one parameterization’s geometry? Do alternative distributions preserve the conclusion?
  11. Language-model evidence. A 30M-parameter, six-layer TinyStories-style result cannot by itself establish frontier-scale quality, long-context behavior, or reasoning gains.
  12. Statistics. How many training seeds are used? Are error bars, stopping rules, and hyperparameter searches comparable? LLM-as-judge trials over generated stories are not a substitute for broad downstream evaluation.
  13. Stability. Is stability measured by finite training traces, gradient norms, induced norms, or a theorem? Double stochasticity constrains residual routing but not every component of the nonlinear network.
  14. Scaling \(d\). Is increasing leftover copies compared with matched-budget increases in width, depth, experts, and context? Are memory and wall-clock reported?
  15. Specialization. Are claims supported by causal leftover-copy interventions and permutation-aware analysis, or only by routing visualizations?
  16. Reproducibility and limits. Can code reproduce the exact matrices and reported curves? Does the conclusion preserve the paper’s stated limits: small language models, cubic overhead relative to KromHC, and the dense-solve bottleneck?
  17. Terminology. The Birkhoff polytope has boundaries and corners. Check whether informal “manifold” language is being used where a precise geometric statement is needed.

Check yourself

  1. Where do the leftover copies live, and what is the skew matrix for?
  2. In the two-tray example, how many times did \(F\) run, and what was \(x_{l+1}\)?
  3. Why does “rows and columns sum to one” not make \(H^{\mathrm{res}}\) a rotation?
  4. What does go-mHC prove about \(H^{\mathrm{res}}\), and what does a finite \(s\) not prove?
  5. Why is leftover routing not the same gate as mixture-of-experts routing?
  6. What would you need before calling leftover-copy count a scaling dimension?
Answers
  1. They are axis 1 of \(x_l \in \mathbb{R}^{B \times d \times m \times n}\). The skew matrix is an auxiliary coordinate pad used to build \(H^{\mathrm{res}}\).
  2. Once. \(x_{l+1} = [4,\ 8]^\top\).
  3. The two-tray matrix satisfies the mass constraints but \(H^{\mathrm{res}}(H^{\mathrm{res}})^\top \neq I_2\). Mass is not an angle.
  4. Every constructed \(H^{\mathrm{res}}\) is doubly stochastic in exact arithmetic. Finite \(s\) need not cover all of \(\mathcal{B}_d\).
  5. Leftover routing mixes persistent state. Expert routing assigns tokens to parameterized branches. Different objects, different bills.
  6. A sweep of \(d\) at matched parameter, FLOP, wall-clock, and activation-memory budgets, against width and depth controls, with retuned optimization. An unbudgeted monotone curve is not enough.

The ten mix-ups all name the wrong object. Once the object is right, the remaining questions are ordinary: what was proved, what was measured, and what would count as a job.
