  • [R003] Y. Yang and J. Gao, “mHC-lite: You Don’t Need 20 Sinkhorn-Knopp Iterations,” arXiv:2601.05732, 2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.05732
  • [R004] W. Zhou et al., “KromHC: Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections with Kronecker-Product Residual Matrices,” arXiv:2601.21579, 2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.21579
  • [R005] T. Dandachi and S. Diggs-Galligan, “go-mHC: Direct Parameterization of Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections via Generalized Orthostochastic Matrices,” arXiv:2604.02309v1, 2026. https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02309
  • [R029] I. Nechita, Z. Ouyang, and A. Szczepanek, “Generalized Unistochastic Matrices,” arXiv:2310.03436, 2023. https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.03436

Glossary and notation guide

A memory aid, not a prerequisite. Terms appear in the order you meet them. If Chapter 1 has not happened yet, you do not need “stream.”

Shapes live in the chapters that introduce them. A row of the token table is never a leftover copy of the whole sentence.

Activation (first developed: Chapter 1) — A numerical representation produced while a network processes an input. X∈R^(m×n) is an activation containing m token positions and n features per position. Activations are data-dependent; parameters are learned values retained between examples.

Axis (first developed: Chapter 1) — One independent indexing direction of a tensor. In these notes, batch, stream, token, and feature are different axes even when two happen to have the same numerical length. Canonical batched residual-state order is B×d×m×n.

Batch / batch axis (first developed: Chapter 1) — A collection of B examples evaluated together. A leading batch dimension changes X:m×n to X:B×m×n and x_l:d×m×n to x_l:B×d×m×n. It does not itself determine whether routers are shared across examples.

Broadcasting (first developed: Chapter 1) — Applying a value over axes it does not explicitly store. In (H_l^post)^T y_l, each of the d write coefficients multiplies all m×n token-feature entries of y_l. Broadcasting is convenient but dangerous when an unnamed axis is mistaken for the stream axis.

Computational branch F (first developed: Chapter 1; HC use: Chapter 4) — The learned transformation between read and write routing. With the book’s simplified shape contract, F(·,W_l):R^(m×n)→R^(m×n) per example. It may be attention, an MLP, an SSM, a convolution, WavePDE, or another block. One central HC update contains one evaluation of F; d streams do not imply d evaluations.

Feature / hidden feature (first developed: Chapter 1) — One coordinate in a token representation. The feature axis has length n. It is not the stream axis: several persistent streams can each contain the same n feature coordinates.

Feature width n (first developed: Chapter 1) — Number of features per token in one stream. Thus X∈R^(m×n) and x_l^(i)∈R^(m×n).

Layer (first developed: Chapter 1) — One indexed stage of the network. l names the current layer and L the total layer count. A layer may read and update every persistent stream even though it evaluates one computational branch.

Layer history (first distinguished: Chapter 3) — The ordered record x_0,x_1,… of states at different depths. It is indexed by l and must not be stored conceptually in the stream index i. At a fixed layer l, all x_l^(i) are current persistent states.

Parameter W_l (first developed: Chapter 1) — A learned value associated with a layer. In F(x,W_l), W_l denotes branch parameters and its shape is branch-dependent. Router projection weights are written W_l^route∈R^(r×q) when the distinction matters.

Sequence length m (first developed: Chapter 1) — Number of token or spatial positions represented in the sequence axis. It is independent of feature width n, stream count d, and layer count L.

Shape (first developed: Chapter 1) — The ordered lengths of a tensor’s axes. Shape equality is required before the persistent term and write term can be added: both have unbatched shape d×m×n.

Tensor (first developed: Chapter 1) — A multidimensional array with named semantic axes. A matrix is a rank-two tensor; the unbatched residual state x_l∈R^(d×m×n) is rank three.

Token / token row (first developed: Chapter 1) — One sequence position and its n-dimensional representation. A token row X_t∈R^n is not a residual stream. A stream contains all m token rows.

Ordinary layer (first developed: Chapter 1) — A transformation written y=F(x,W). It replaces its input in the simplest feed-forward picture, before an identity/residual route is added.

Residual connection (first developed: Chapter 2) — An update x_(l+1)=x_l+F(x_l,W_l) that preserves an additive identity path while allowing the branch to modify the representation. Residual connections help optimization and representation preservation, but do not guarantee stability at arbitrary scales.

Identity path / gradient highway (first developed: Chapter 2) — The direct additive path from x_l to x_(l+1) whose local derivative includes an identity contribution. “Highway” is an optimization interpretation, not a guarantee that all gradients remain bounded through any network.

Residual state (first developed: Chapter 2; multi-stream form: Chapter 3) — The persistent activation carried from one layer to the next. In an ordinary residual network it is typically one m×n array; in HC it is d such streams assembled as x_l∈R^(d×m×n).

Persistent residual stream (first developed: Chapter 3) — One state slot x_l^(i)∈R^(m×n) that persists and is routed across depth. It is not a previous layer, token, feature channel, attention head, expert, or separate copy of F.

Stream count d (first developed: Chapter 3) — Number of persistent residual streams. It is the first axis of unbatched x_l∈R^(d×m×n) and the side length of H_l^res∈R^(d×d). Whether increasing d is a useful scaling direction is an empirical and architectural question, not a theorem.

Copied-stream initialization (first developed: Chapter 3) — Initialization x_0^(1)=⋯=x_0^(d)=X. The streams initially contain the same values but are distinct state slots. Unequal writes and subsequent routing can make them diverge.

Hyper-Connections (HC) (first developed: Chapter 3) — The architecture family that carries multiple persistent residual streams and learns read, write, and state-to-state routing around a computational branch. In the family comparison, “HC” refers to the original unconstrained/general Hyper-Connections design; mHC-family variants add particular structure to residual routing.

Static Hyper-Connections (SHC) (first developed: Chapter 3; compared: Chapter 15) — The original HC paper’s input-independent form: learned routing coefficients are parameters shared across examples at a layer rather than functions of the current activation. “Static” describes router dependence, not frozen training; the coefficients themselves are learned.

Dynamic Hyper-Connections (DHC) (first developed: Chapter 3; compared: Chapter 15) — The original HC paper’s input-dependent form, which derives routing values from normalized/reduced activations using learned projections, gains, nonlinearities, and biases specified by that design. DHC is an HC subvariant, not another name for doubly stochastic mHC. DHC×d denotes expansion to d persistent streams, not d branch evaluations.

Expansion rate (first developed: Chapter 3) — The original HC paper’s term for the number of persistent streams. That paper and these notes reuse letters differently: these notes reserve d for expansion/stream count and n for feature width. Always translate the role of an axis, not merely its letter.

Depth-connection / width-connection (first developed: Chapter 3) — Terms used by the original HC paper for learned connectivity across layer depth and across the expanded persistent width/stream structure. In these notes the concrete, less ambiguous operators are read routing H^pre, write routing H^post, and persistent-state routing H^res; the historical terms do not turn the stream axis into layer history.

Read routing H_l^pre (first developed: Chapter 4) — A soft read operator of unbatched shape 1×d. It contracts the stream axis:

\[ (z_l)_{tf}=\sum_j(H_l^{pre})_{1j}(x_l)_{jtf}, \qquad z_l\in\mathbb R^{m\times n}. \]

It selects a mixture of persistent states for the current branch without mixing tokens or features.

Branch input z_l (first developed: Chapter 4) — The m×n activation read from streams, z_l=H_l^pre x_l, before computation by F.

Branch output y_l (first developed: Chapter 4) — The m×n activation y_l=F(z_l,W_l) that is distributed back to streams by write routing.

Write routing H_l^post (first developed: Chapter 4) — A soft write operator of unbatched shape 1×d. Its transpose has shape d×1, and (H_l^post)^T y_l∈R^(d×m×n) gives destination stream i the scaled write (H_l^post)_(1i)y_l.

Persistent-state routing H_l^res (first developed: Chapter 4) — The d×d router that transports old stream states into new stream slots:

\[ (H_l^{res}x_l)_{itf}=\sum_j(H_l^{res})_{ij}(x_l)_{jtf}. \]

Row i describes destination stream i; column j refers to source stream j. H_l^res is also abbreviated R_l in path expansions.

Read–compute–write–persist update (first developed: Chapter 4) — The staged meaning of

\[ x_{l+1}=H_l^{res}x_l+(H_l^{post})^\top F(H_l^{pre}x_l,W_l). \]

Read and compute produce one m×n branch output; write and persist each produce d×m×n; the final addition is therefore shape-valid.

Path through depth (first developed: Chapter 5) — One algebraic route by which an earlier state or branch output reaches a later state through products of R_l=H_l^res and writes p_l=(H_l^post)^T. It is an interpretation of terms in the expansion, not necessarily a discrete path selected by the model.

Stream divergence (first developed: Chapter 5) — The process by which initially equal stream values become unequal, often beginning with unequal write coefficients. Only after divergence can a read mixture distinguish stream contents meaningfully.

Level 2 — Routers, constraints, and HC variants

Router / routing operator (first developed: Chapter 4; generated routers: Chapter 6) — A matrix or vector that moves information among persistent streams or between streams and the branch. It is an activation-dependent numerical operator when dynamically generated, not the projection parameters that generated it.

Learned router projection (first developed: Chapter 6) — A usually small learned linear map from an activation summary hat x_l∈R^r to coordinates u_l∈R^q, schematically u_l=α_l hat x_l W_l^route+b_l^route. Its weights, bias, and gain are trained by backpropagation. It does not alone enforce stochasticity, orthogonality, or another routing constraint.

Activation summary hat x_l (first developed: Chapter 6) — A normalized and/or reduced representation of the current activation used as router input. Its width r and reduction axes are implementation-specific; reduction over tokens must not be assumed unless the cited implementation does it.

Routing coordinates / routing logits u_l (first developed: Chapter 6) — Unconstrained real values emitted by the learned router projection. Shape is q per routed item. A subsequent constraint map interprets them as read/write gates, matrix logits, skew coordinates, or mixture weights.

Router gain α_l and bias b_l^route (first developed: Chapter 6) — Trainable scale and offset applied in the router projection. α_l may be scalar or broadcastable; b_l^route∈R^q. Their exact initialization and granularity belong to the cited implementation.

Constraint map / constraint mechanism (first developed: Chapter 6) — A deterministic differentiable transformation from unconstrained coordinates to a router in a desired set. Examples are finite Sinkhorn normalization, a softmax-weighted permutation mixture, Kronecker composition of constrained factors, and the go-mHC Cayley/block-norm map. The constraint map has no learned parameters unless parameters are explicitly added; learning occurs through coordinates fed into it.

Dynamic routing (first developed: Chapter 6) — Routing whose numerical values depend on current activations. “Dynamic” does not specify whether the router varies per token, sequence, batch item, or layer; that is the routing granularity.

Routing granularity (first developed: Chapter 6) — The axes over which one router is shared or varies: per layer, per batch item/sequence, or per token, for example. Shapes and primary implementations determine granularity. The phrase “input-dependent” alone does not imply token-wise routing.

Gate / soft gate (first developed: Chapter 4; formal distinction: Chapter 6) — An interpretation of coefficients that continuously weight reads or writes. A soft gate need not be a probability vector unless a normalization imposes that condition. HC gating should not be conflated with routing among MoE experts.

Unconstrained residual mixing (first developed: Chapter 7) — Repeated application of H_l^res without a structural constraint designed to control its action. Products can amplify, suppress, alternate, or rotate components. “Unconstrained” describes the operator class, not an assertion that training must fail.

Stochastic matrix (first developed: Chapter 8) — In these notes, a nonnegative square matrix with one specified set of sums equal to one. Always say row-stochastic or column-stochastic when only one condition is intended.

Row-stochastic matrix (first developed: Chapter 8) — A matrix B∈R^(d×d) satisfying B_ij≥0 and B 1_d=1_d; every row sums to one.

Column-stochastic matrix (first developed: Chapter 8) — A matrix satisfying B_ij≥0 and B^T 1_d=1_d; every column sums to one.

Doubly stochastic matrix (first developed: Chapter 8) — A matrix B∈R^(d×d) satisfying nonnegativity plus both unit row sums and unit column sums. It preserves the all-ones mode and total mass under the matching convention. It need not be orthogonal, symmetric, invertible, or immune to information mixing.

All-ones vector 1_d (first developed: Chapter 8) — The length-d column vector of ones. Doubly stochasticity is compactly written B1_d=1_d and B^T1_d=1_d.

Convex combination (first developed: Chapter 8) — A weighted sum Σ_k α_k V_k with α_k≥0 and Σ_k α_k=1. It stays in the convex hull of the objects V_k.

Convex hull (first developed: Chapter 8) — The set of all convex combinations of a collection of points. The Birkhoff polytope is the convex hull of d×d permutation matrices.

Polytope (first developed: Chapter 8) — A bounded geometric set described as the convex hull of finitely many vertices, equivalently by finitely many linear constraints in this setting.

Birkhoff polytope 𝓑_d (first developed: Chapter 8) — The set of all d×d doubly stochastic matrices, a subset of R^(d×d). Exact membership is different from the ability of a parameterization to reach every point in the set.

mHC (original matrix-constrained Hyper-Connections) (first developed: Chapter 9) — The HC variant that constrains residual mixing toward the Birkhoff polytope using the paper’s matrix normalization mechanism, presented here through finite Sinkhorn normalization. “mHC” is also used informally for the broader family; when a comparison could be ambiguous, these notes say original mHC or mHC family.

Matrix logits Z_l (first developed: Chapter 9) — An unconstrained d×d real matrix fed to a matrix constraint procedure. Positivity is obtained before alternating row/column normalizations in the Sinkhorn construction.

Sinkhorn normalization / Sinkhorn iteration (first developed: Chapter 9) — Repeated positive row and column normalization intended to approach a doubly stochastic matrix. In ordinary finite-precision code with finitely many iterations, unit sums are approximate within a measured tolerance, not algebraically exact.

Constraint residual / constraint error (first developed: Chapter 9) — A numerical measure of failed membership, such as max_i|Σ_j H_ij−1|, max_j|Σ_i H_ij−1|, and the magnitude of negative entries. It measures the routing constraint, not task loss or model quality.

Finite-iteration exactness (first distinguished: Chapter 9) — The question of whether the implemented finite computation satisfies a constraint exactly in exact arithmetic. Finite Sinkhorn iterations generally leave residual row/column error; an algebraic construction can satisfy the conditions by construction, while floating-point evaluation still has roundoff.

Permutation matrix P (first developed: Chapter 10) — A d×d binary matrix with one 1 in every row and column. It reorders streams, is doubly stochastic and orthogonal, and is a vertex of 𝓑_d.

Birkhoff–von Neumann theorem (first developed: Chapter 10) — Every doubly stochastic matrix is a convex combination of permutation matrices. The theorem establishes existence; a parameterization that explicitly assigns a weight to all d! permutations may be computationally impractical.

mHC-lite (first developed: Chapter 10) — The HC variant presented through exact convex mixtures of permutation routers. Nonnegative normalized mixture weights make the result exactly doubly stochastic in exact arithmetic. Full enumeration has a factorial burden (d! candidates), so exactness does not imply scalable representation.

Softmax (first developed: Chapter 10) — A map from real logits to strictly positive weights summing to one. Applied over permutation logits, it supplies convex-mixture weights. With finite logits every enumerated weight is positive; an exact zero or a permutation vertex is reached only as a limit unless the implementation adds another mechanism.

Permutation bank (first developed: Chapter 10) — A stored or generated tensor of candidate permutation matrices, typically M×d×d for M candidates. Combining routing logits of shape 𝒢×M with that bank produces routers of shape 𝒢×d×d; the batch/token axes in 𝒢 are not permuted. For full mHC-lite enumeration, M=d!; K remains reserved for the KromHC factor count.

Factorial burden d! (first developed: Chapter 10) — Growth in the number of permutations: 4!=24, 8!=40,320, and 16!≈2.09×10^13. It is a representation/parameterization burden, not a claim that every algorithm for a doubly stochastic matrix must enumerate all permutations.

Kronecker product A⊗B (first developed: Chapter 11) — A block matrix formed by replacing entry A_ij with the scaled block A_ij B. If A is r×c and B is u×v, then A⊗B is (ru)×(cv). Eigenvalues of square factors combine pairwise as products.

KromHC (first developed: Chapter 11) — The HC variant whose residual router is built from a Kronecker product of smaller constrained factors. The structure can reduce construction cost and parameter burden, but restricts which full routers and spectra can be represented. Cost and expressivity must be compared for a named factorization and regime.

KromHC factorization (first developed: Chapter 11) — Choose K factor sizes i_1,…,i_K≥2 with ∏_k i_k=d, construct doubly stochastic factors U_l^k∈R^(i_k×i_k), and set H_l^res=U_l^K⊗⋯⊗U_l^1∈R^(d×d). K counts factors; it is not the number of branches or layers.

Exact constraint satisfaction (first developed: Chapter 10; central distinction: Chapter 14) — Every output of a parameterization lies in the required set in exact arithmetic. It says nothing by itself about full coverage, optimization quality, floating-point tolerance, or predictive performance.

Set coverage / expressivity of a router map (first developed: Chapter 10; central distinction: Chapter 14) — The subset of valid routing matrices reachable by a parameterization. A map can be exact but cover only a strict or otherwise limited subset of 𝓑_d.

Level 3 — The go-mHC construction

go-mHC (first developed: Chapter 12) — Generalized-orthostochastic mHC. A learned projection fills a skew-symmetric N×N matrix (N=ds); a Cayley transform produces an orthogonal matrix; squared Frobenius norms of its s×s blocks produce H_l^res∈𝓑_d. The machinery constructs a router between already-existing streams.

Block size s (first developed: Chapter 12; interpreted: Chapter 14) — Side length of each auxiliary block. With d streams, the auxiliary matrix side is N=ds; Q is partitioned into a d×d grid of s×s blocks. s is not sequence length, stream count, or feature width.

Auxiliary dimension N=ds (first developed: Chapter 12) — Side length of A_l,Q_l∈R^(N×N). For d=4,s=2, N=8.

Skew-symmetric matrix A_l (first developed: Chapter 12) — A square matrix satisfying A_l^T=−A_l. Its diagonal is zero and its lower triangle is fixed by its upper triangle. An N×N skew matrix has p=N(N−1)/2 independent real entries.

Free skew coordinates p=N(N−1)/2 (first developed: Chapter 12) — Number of independent strict-upper-triangle entries in A_l. For N=8, p=28. The router projection emits these coordinates; they are not d stream tensors.

Identity matrix I_N (first developed: Chapter 2; Cayley use: Chapter 12) — The N×N matrix with ones on the diagonal and zeros elsewhere. It leaves an N-vector unchanged and appears in Q=(I_N−A)(I_N+A)^(−1).

Transpose M^T (first developed: Chapter 1) — The matrix obtained by exchanging rows and columns. In the central equation it turns H^post:1×d into d×1; in A^T=−A it defines skew symmetry; in Q^TQ=I it defines orthogonality.

Cayley transform (first developed: Chapter 12) — The rational map

\[ Q=(I_N-A)(I_N+A)^{-1} \]

from a real skew-symmetric A to an orthogonal Q when the solve is defined. Implementations use a linear solve, not a materialized inverse. This transform deterministically maps coordinates; it does not learn without learned coordinates upstream.

Cayley −1 eigenvalue caveat (first developed: Chapter 12) — The standard Cayley chart does not cover orthogonal matrices having −1 as an eigenvalue. With the stated convention it lies in the identity/determinant-+1 component. Therefore “orthogonal output” must not be upgraded to “global parameterization of all O(N).”

Orthogonal matrix Q_l (first developed: Chapter 12) — A real N×N matrix satisfying Q_l^TQ_l=Q_lQ_l^T=I_N. Its rows and columns are orthonormal. Orthogonality is an auxiliary property used to prove sums of block energies; H_l^res itself is generally not orthogonal.

Orthogonal group O(N) (first developed formally: Chapter 18; first used: Chapter 12) — The set of all real N×N orthogonal matrices under multiplication. It has determinant +1 and −1 components.

Special orthogonal group SO(N) (first developed formally: Chapter 18; first used: Chapter 12) — The determinant-+1 component/subgroup of O(N). Cayley transforms of finite real skew coordinates lie in this component, subject to the chart caveat.

Lie algebra so(N) (first developed intuitively: Chapter 12; formalized: Chapter 18) — The vector space of real N×N skew-symmetric matrices, interpreted as tangent directions at the identity of SO(N). It has dimension N(N−1)/2.

Block partition Q_ij (first developed: Chapter 13) — The decomposition of Q∈R^((ds)×(ds)) into a d×d grid of blocks Q_ij∈R^(s×s). i indexes a destination block row and j a source block column.

Frobenius norm ||M||_F (first developed: Chapter 13) — The square root of the sum of squared entries, ||M||_F²=Σ_(a,c) M_ac². For an s×s block, it measures block energy and is always nonnegative after squaring.

Trace tr(M) (first developed: Chapter 13) — Sum of a square matrix’s diagonal entries. The identity tr(Q_ij Q_ij^T)=||Q_ij||_F² turns block orthogonality equations into row/column energy sums.

Block-norm router (first developed: Chapter 13) — The go-mHC residual router defined by

\[ (H_l^{res})_{ij}=\frac{1}{s}\lVert Q_{l,ij}\rVert_F^2. \]

Squared norms give nonnegativity. Orthogonality gives Σ_j||Q_ij||_F²=s and Σ_i||Q_ij||_F²=s, hence unit row and column sums after division by s.

Exact by construction (first developed: Chapter 13) — A property that follows algebraically from how an object is formed rather than from an iterative penalty. For the ideal go-mHC map, nonnegativity and both unit-sum conditions follow from orthogonality. Floating-point code should still test tolerances.

Orthostochastic matrix (first developed: Chapter 14) — A doubly stochastic matrix whose entries are squared entries of an orthogonal matrix: H_ij=Q_ij². This is the s=1 case of the generalized block-norm construction. Orthostochastic matrices generally form only part of the Birkhoff polytope.

Generalized orthostochastic map Φ_(d,s) (first developed: Chapter 14) — The map

\[ \Phi_{d,s}:O(ds)\to\mathcal B_d, \qquad [\Phi_{d,s}(Q)]_{ij}=\frac{\lVert Q_{ij}\rVert_F^2}{s}. \]

Its image consists of routers reachable at the chosen finite block size. Membership of every output in 𝓑_d is proven; full surjectivity onto 𝓑_d is a separate question and must not be assumed.

Image of a map (first developed: Chapter 14) — The set of all outputs the map can produce. im Φ_(d,s)={Φ_(d,s)(Q):Q∈O(ds)} is the actual go-mHC routing family before restrictions introduced by the Cayley chart or learned projection are considered.

Surjective / surjectivity (first developed: Chapter 14) — A map is surjective onto its target when every target point has at least one preimage. Exact doubly stochastic output does not prove that Φ_(d,s) is surjective onto 𝓑_d for a selected finite s.

Finite-s limitation (first developed: Chapter 14) — Any restriction associated with choosing a finite auxiliary block size, including limited image coverage and Cayley-coordinate reach. Increasing s can enlarge the parameterization’s possibilities, but s=2 being useful is a paper-reported empirical design compromise, not a theorem of optimality.

Level 4 — Routing interpretations and spectral dynamics

mHC family (first compared: Chapter 15) — Collective name for HC, original mHC, mHC-lite, KromHC, and go-mHC when discussing persistent-stream routing. Each variant must still be named when exactness, coverage, cost, or evidence differs.

Learned dataflow (first developed: Chapter 16) — The interpretation that dynamic read, write, and persistent-state routers choose how information moves through state slots and depth. It becomes a stronger architectural claim when combined with heterogeneous branches; measured specialization is required before calling a particular latent computation graph an empirical fact.

Mixture of Experts (MoE) (first compared: Chapter 16) — An architecture that routes examples or tokens among parameterized expert computations. mHC primarily routes information among persistent state streams around a branch. Both use gates, but their routed objects and computational consequences differ.

Heterogeneous branch (first developed: Chapter 16) — One of several computational branches with different operators, such as attention, an SSM, WavePDE, or an MLP. Combining heterogeneous branches with HC routing is an architectural extension; it is not implied by the single-branch central equation.

Stream specialization (first discussed: Chapter 16) — A possible tendency for streams to carry different kinds of information. Unless a paper directly measures it with an appropriate protocol, specialization is a Hypothesis or Interpretation, not an architectural guarantee or empirical result.

Eigenvector and eigenvalue (first developed: Chapter 7) — For square matrix M, a nonzero vector v and scalar λ satisfying Mv=λv. Repeating a fixed M scales that mode as λ^L: magnitude above one grows, below one decays, negative values alternate sign, and nonreal values rotate as well as scale.

Spectrum spec(M) (first developed: Chapter 7) — The set or multiset of eigenvalues of M. For A⊗B, eigenvalues are pairwise products λ_i(A)λ_j(B).

Spectral radius ρ(M) (first developed: Chapter 7) — max_{λ∈spec(M)}|λ|. It is useful intuition for powers of one fixed matrix, but does not fully characterize transient growth or products of changing, nonnormal routers.

Nonnormal matrix (first cautioned: Chapter 7; revisited: Chapter 17) — A matrix that does not commute with its transpose/conjugate transpose. Its eigenvalues alone can miss large transient amplification. Dynamic products H_(L−1)^res⋯H_0^res require more than a spectral-radius slogan.

Stationary mode (first introduced: Chapter 8; developed: Chapter 17) — A direction preserved with eigenvalue 1. A doubly stochastic matrix preserves the all-ones vector; additional stationary modes depend on reducibility and structure.

Mixing (first developed: Chapter 17) — Redistribution that reduces differences among stream values under repeated routing. Mixing can be desirable for controlled sharing yet can also erase distinctions; doubly stochasticity does not specify a mixing rate.

Diffusion (first developed: Chapter 17) — The dynamical interpretation of repeated local averaging or redistribution. It describes behavior of a chosen router/product, not every doubly stochastic matrix: permutation routers cycle without averaging.

Cycle / permutation cycle (first developed: Chapter 17) — A routing pattern that sends a sequence of stream indices around a closed loop. A length-k permutation cycle has eigenvalues among the kth roots of unity and can sustain oscillatory state movement.

Complex eigenvalue (first introduced: Chapter 7; developed: Chapter 17) — A nonreal eigenvalue re^(iθ) whose magnitude controls scaling and phase controls rotation/oscillation in an invariant real plane. Real stochastic matrices may have complex-conjugate eigenvalue pairs.

Root of unity (first developed: Chapter 17) — A complex number λ satisfying λ^k=1 for some positive integer k. Eigenvalues of permutation cycles are roots of unity.

Karpelevič region (first developed: Chapter 17) — The classical feasible region in the complex plane for eigenvalues of finite stochastic matrices of a given size. It supplies context for spectral reach; occupying more of that region is not by itself evidence of better task performance.

Spectral reach / spectral richness (first developed: Chapter 17) — The set or diversity of eigenvalue patterns a router parameterization can realize. Claims that KromHC is restricted or go-mHC is richer must retain the paper’s matrix size, parameterization, and evidence scope.

Level 5 — Geometry, optimization, and implementation

Manifold (first developed: Chapter 18) — A space that looks locally like Euclidean space even if it has a curved global structure. O(N) and SO(N) are matrix manifolds defined by orthogonality constraints.

Tangent space (first developed: Chapter 18) — The linear space of infinitesimal directions at a point on a manifold. At the identity of SO(N), tangent directions are skew-symmetric matrices in so(N).

Local chart / chart-like parameterization (first developed: Chapter 18) — Coordinates that describe a neighborhood of a manifold point. The Cayley transform is chart-like around the identity but misses matrices with eigenvalue −1, so it is not a global chart of all O(N).

Dimension (first developed: Chapter 18) — Number of local degrees of freedom. so(N) and SO(N) have dimension N(N−1)/2. Dimension counting can rule out some surjectivity claims, but matching dimensions alone does not prove surjectivity.

Optimization geometry (first developed: Chapter 18) — How a parameterization’s coordinates, curvature, symmetries, singularities, and gradients shape optimization. A plausible geometric explanation for training behavior remains an Interpretation or Hypothesis unless evidence isolates it.

Differentiability (first discussed: Chapter 9; implementation: Chapter 19) — Existence of derivatives needed to propagate loss gradients through router generation. Sinkhorn steps, linear solves away from singularity, block reshapes, and squared norms are differentiable operations in standard autodiff systems; differentiability does not ensure well-conditioned gradients.

Automatic differentiation / autodiff (first developed: Chapter 19) — Library machinery that computes derivatives through recorded tensor operations. Correct gradients require using supported solves and shape operations without unintended detachments or in-place mutations.

Normalize and reduce (first developed: Chapter 6; implementation: Chapter 19) — Produce compact router input hat x_l by normalization and aggregation over explicitly named axes. The operation must state whether it reduces tokens, features, streams, or batch items; otherwise routing granularity is ambiguous.

Batched router construction (first developed: Chapter 19) — Building one or more routers with a leading batch-like axis, for example A,Q:B×N×N and H:B×d×d. It should use batched linear solves and reductions rather than Python loops over examples when production efficiency matters.

Block reshape (first developed: Chapter 19) — A view/rearrangement from Q:B×(ds)×(ds) to a layout exposing d×d blocks of s×s, such as B×d×s×d×s, followed by a deliberate permutation before reducing the two within-block axes. A reshape alone may interleave the wrong axes.

Linear solve (first developed: Chapter 12; implementation: Chapter 19) — Computing X from MX=R without explicitly forming M^(−1). It is the numerically preferred implementation of the Cayley transform. Left-versus-right multiplication and transpose conventions must reproduce the displayed equation.

Explicit matrix inverse (first warned: Chapter 12; implementation: Chapter 19) — Materializing (I+A)^(−1) and multiplying by it. Production code should avoid this in favor of a solve, which is generally more stable and efficient.

Dense solve cost (first derived: Chapter 19) — A conventional cubic arithmetic estimate O(N^3)=O(d^3s^3) for a dense N×N solve, before batching, channels, hardware, and constant factors are accounted for. “Cubic” alone does not determine end-to-end expense relative to the model’s larger matrix multiplications.

Parameter count (first calculated: Chapter 12; compared: Chapter 19) — Number of trainable scalar values. The skew-coordinate output width p=N(N−1)/2 is not by itself the total router parameter count: a projection from width r also carries approximately r p weights plus bias/gain under the chosen design.

FLOP count (first compared: Chapter 15; developed: Chapter 19) — Approximate number of floating-point arithmetic operations. It is not elapsed time; kernel fusion, memory traffic, batching, precision, and accelerator utilization also matter.

Asymptotic complexity (first compared: Chapter 15; developed: Chapter 19) — Growth rate as a size variable increases, suppressing constants and lower-order terms. It must name which variables are fixed: O(d^3) for the dense go-mHC solve assumes fixed s.

Initialization near identity (first discussed: Chapter 6; go-mHC implementation: Chapter 19) — Choosing small skew coordinates makes the Cayley output near I_N, whose block-norm image is near the identity stream router. This can preserve an initial residual path; the actual bias/gain recipe must follow the implementation being reproduced.

Numerical stability (first discussed: Chapter 9; developed: Chapter 19) — Sensitivity of computed results and gradients to rounding, conditioning, scale, and algorithm choice. It is distinct from training stability, activation stability, or the architectural property of being doubly stochastic.

Numerical tolerance (first developed: Chapter 9; tested: Chapter 19) — A finite threshold used when checking equations in floating point. Tests should report dtype-appropriate tolerances for orthogonality, row sums, column sums, and nonnegativity rather than demand bitwise equality.

Orthogonality residual (first developed: Chapter 19) — A measure such as ||Q^TQ−I_N|| used to test the numerical Cayley result. Small orthogonality residual supports the constraint calculation but is not a model-quality metric.

Row/column-sum test (first developed: Chapter 19) — A unit check that H1_d≈1_d and H^T1_d≈1_d within stated tolerance, together with a nonnegativity check. Testing only the average sum can hide a bad row or column; use a maximum or elementwise assertion.

Production kernel (first developed: Chapter 19) — An implementation optimized for actual training constraints such as fusion, memory layout, precision, batching, and backward cost. Pedagogical code may be executable and correct without matching production performance.

Custom kernel (first discussed: Chapter 9; compared: Chapter 19) — A hardware-specific fused implementation used when composition of general tensor operations is too costly. Its existence or absence affects measured speed, so paper comparisons must not turn kernel maturity into a claim about mathematical complexity alone.

Empirical stability (first discussed: Chapter 9; compared: Chapter 15) — Observed behavior such as bounded activations, gradients, losses, or successful training in a named experimental setting. It is a paper-reported empirical result, not a consequence of double stochasticity alone.

Architecture fact (label introduced: front matter; used throughout) — A paper-specified component, shape, or transformation. These notes label it Definition and cite the primary architecture source where needed. An architecture fact states what was built, not whether it works better.

Paper-reported empirical result (label introduced: front matter; first central use: Chapter 9) — A measurement attributed to its source paper and experimental regime. It should not be restated as an unqualified fact or pooled with numbers from unmatched experiments.

Interpretation (label introduced: front matter; used throughout) — An explanatory view supported by the mechanism, such as read/write gating or diffusion over streams. It may guide reasoning without being uniquely established.

Hypothesis (label introduced: front matter; first central use: Chapter 16) — A testable but unestablished proposal, such as semantic stream specialization or a benefit from treating d as a scaling axis. It requires evidence before promotion to an empirical result.

Compact disambiguation checklist

When a symbol or claim feels ambiguous, ask:

  1. Which axis? Batch B, stream d, token m, feature n, layer l, or auxiliary block s?
  2. Which object? Persistent state x_l, branch activation z_l/y_l, router H_l, coordinates u_l, constraint-map matrix A_l/Q_l, or layer history?
  3. Which stage? Learned projection or deterministic constraint map?
  4. Which guarantee? Valid output, exact arithmetic, floating-point tolerance, full set coverage, spectral property, or empirical training behavior?
  5. Which evidence label? Definition/architecture fact, theorem, derivation, paper-reported empirical result, interpretation, or hypothesis?

If those five answers are explicit, residual streams, layer history, feature axes, routers, constraint maps, and empirical claims cannot quietly collapse into one another.

Global references

This bibliography resolves the 109 entries in the chapters’ Essential references sections to 38 unique works. Keys are global and stable: repeated citations of the same DOI, arXiv identifier, or work title share one key. The chapter map at the end preserves each chapter’s source order and provides an explicit key for every occurrence.

The five recent HC-family preprints are annotated as Primary architecture source. Their titles, author records, and current versions were checked against the arXiv records specified in SCHEMA.md; their architectural and empirical claims should be read at the cited version.

HC variants

  • [R001] Defa Zhu, Hongzhi Huang, Zihao Huang, Yutao Zeng, Yunyao Mao, Banggu Wu, Qiyang Min, and Xun Zhou. “Hyper-Connections.” 2024; revised 2025. arXiv:2409.19606v3.
    • Primary architecture source — HC. Defines the static and dynamic Hyper-Connections designs and the expanded persistent-stream architecture. The arXiv record identifies v3 as the current version.
  • [R002] Zhenda Xie et al. “mHC: Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections.” 2025; revised 2026. arXiv:2512.24880v2.
    • Primary architecture source — original mHC. Specifies the manifold-constrained HC design, its finite Sinkhorn-based residual routing, infrastructure work, and reported scaling experiments. The arXiv record identifies v2 as the current version.
  • [R003] Yongyi Yang and Jianyang Gao. “mHC-lite: You Don’t Need 20 Sinkhorn-Knopp Iterations.” 2026. arXiv:2601.05732v1.
    • Primary architecture source — mHC-lite. Specifies exact convex mixtures of permutation matrices and reports the associated implementation and experimental comparisons. The cited record is v1.
  • [R004] Wuyang Zhou, Yuxuan Gu, Giorgos Iacovides, and Danilo Mandic. “KromHC: Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections with Kronecker-Product Residual Matrices.” 2026. arXiv:2601.21579v2.
    • Primary architecture source — KromHC. Specifies Kronecker-factorized residual matrices and reports their parameter, expressivity, and empirical tradeoffs. The arXiv record identifies v2 as the current version.
  • [R005] Torque Dandachi and Sophia Diggs-Galligan. “go-mHC: Direct Parameterization of Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections via Generalized Orthostochastic Matrices.” 2026. arXiv:2604.02309v1.
    • Primary architecture source — go-mHC. Specifies the skew-coordinate, Cayley-transform, and normalized block-Frobenius-norm construction and reports its expressivity, spectra, cost, and experiments. The cited record is v1.
  • [R018] Charles F. Van Loan. “The Ubiquitous Kronecker Product.” Journal of Computational and Applied Mathematics 123, 85–100 (2000). doi:10.1016/S0377-0427(00)00393-9.

Claim ledger

A scoreboard, not a second textbook. Each row says what kind of support a claim has. A paper’s architecture is a Definition of what that paper built. A measured accuracy bump stays an Empirical result. None of these notes promote a preprint number into a law of deep learning.

The six labels are exclusive: Definition, Theorem, Derivation, Empirical result, Interpretation, Hypothesis.

Definition

ID Central claim Exact scope and boundary Source / chapter
DEF-01 A persistent residual stream is one current state slot x_l^(i)∈R^(m×n) in x_l∈R^(d×m×n). It is not a token, feature partition, attention head, expert, branch copy, or output from an earlier layer. Copied initialization makes the slots numerically equal at l=0, not historically indexed. [R001]; Chs. 3–4
DEF-02 The single-branch HC-family update is x_(l+1)=H_l^res x_l+(H_l^post)^T F(H_l^pre x_l,W_l). H^pre:1×d reads one m×n branch input, F is evaluated once in this equation, H^post:1×d writes one result, and H^res:d×d transports old state. Additional branches must be declared separately. [R001], [R002], [R005]; Ch. 4
DEF-03 A learned router projection emits unconstrained coordinates; a constraint map converts them into an operator with specified structure. Schematic form u=α x̂ W^route+b^route. Projection weights learn values; Sinkhorn, permutation mixtures, Kronecker composition, or Cayley/block norms impose structure. “Dynamic” alone does not specify per-token, per-sequence, or shared routing. [R001]–[R005]; Ch. 6
DEF-04 B∈𝓑_d means B_ij≥0, B1=1, and B^T1=1; 𝓑_d is the Birkhoff polytope. Double stochasticity does not imply orthogonality, symmetry, invertibility, absence of mixing, or stability of the complete nonlinear network. [R019], [R020]; Ch. 8
DEF-05 Original mHC constrains the residual router with finite Sinkhorn–Knopp normalization. In the cited implementation, d=4, at most 20 iterations are used, routing is derived from the flattened per-position dc state, and Sinkhorn is applied to H^res; pre/post gates use separate sigmoid maps. Finite iteration is approximate, not algebraically exact. [R002], [R021]; Chs. 6, 9
DEF-06 mHC-lite constructs H^res as a softmax-weighted convex combination of permutation matrices. Every produced mixture is doubly stochastic in exact arithmetic. Full Birkhoff coverage through explicit enumeration uses all d! permutations; a restricted bank stays exact but loses coverage. Finite softmax logits lie in the relative interior of the enumerated hull. [R003], [R019], [R020]; Ch. 10
DEF-07 KromHC constructs H^res=U^K⊗⋯⊗U^1 from smaller doubly stochastic factors, with each paper factor represented by a permutation mixture. The cited KromHC changes residual routing while retaining mHC-style pre/post routing. Exactness applies to the product output; reachable matrices and spectra inherit the chosen factorization. [R004], [R018]; Ch. 11
DEF-08 go-mHC sets N=ds, packs N(N−1)/2 coordinates into A^T=−A, forms a Cayley matrix Q, partitions it into s×s blocks, and defines H_ij=||Q_ij||_F²/s. The construction makes a d×d residual router between already-existing streams. A and Q are auxiliary N×N matrices, not N streams and not branch activations. [R005], [R026], [R029]; Chs. 12–13
DEF-09 Exact constraint satisfaction and full set coverage are different properties. Φ_(d,s)(Q)∈𝓑_d for every admissible Q proves validity of every output; it does not prove im Φ_(d,s)=𝓑_d, reach through one Cayley chart, favorable conditioning, or task performance. [R005], [R029]; Ch. 14

Theorem

ID Central claim Assumptions and boundary Source / chapter
THM-01 Every doubly stochastic matrix is a convex combination of permutation matrices, and the permutation matrices are exactly the vertices of 𝓑_d. Birkhoff–von Neumann theorem for finite square matrices. It is an existence theorem, not a scalable instruction to enumerate d! vertices. [R019], [R020]; Chs. 8, 10
THM-02 Products of doubly stochastic matrices remain doubly stochastic; for B∈𝓑_d, ||B||_1=||B||_∞=1 and ||B||_2≤1. These are properties of the residual mixer or its product. They do not bound the branch injection, derivatives of an input-dependent router, normalization, or the complete HC update. [R033]; Ch. 8
THM-03 Alternating row/column scaling of a strictly positive square matrix converges to a doubly stochastic diagonal scaling. The conclusion is asymptotic as iteration count tends to infinity. Nonnegative matrices with zeros need additional support conditions; a chosen finite iteration count generally leaves residual error. [R021]; Ch. 9
THM-04 If A and B are doubly stochastic, A⊗B is doubly stochastic; spec(A⊗B) consists of pairwise products of factor eigenvalues. Extends to finitely many square factors. Exactness does not imply full Birkhoff coverage; spectral products impose factor-dependent restrictions. [R018]; Ch. 11
THM-05 For real skew-symmetric A, Q=(I−A)(I+A)^(−1) is orthogonal with determinant +1; conversely the ordinary Cayley chart covers the elements of SO(N) without eigenvalue −1. I+A is nonsingular in exact real arithmetic. The map does not cover the determinant-−1 component or SO(N) matrices with eigenvalue −1; implementations should use a solve rather than form an inverse. [R026]; Ch. 12
THM-06 If orthogonal Q∈R^((ds)×(ds)) is partitioned into s×s blocks and H_ij=||Q_ij||_F²/s, then H∈𝓑_d. This is the exact generalized-orthostochastic containment theorem. It proves nonnegativity and both unit-sum conditions only; it does not make H orthogonal or establish coverage. [R005], [R029]; Ch. 13
THM-07 For d≥3, the ordinary orthostochastic image S_(d,1) is a proper subset of 𝓑_d; for d=2, S_(2,1)=𝓑_2. This theorem is only the s=1 statement. Equality or strictness for an arbitrary named larger finite s is not established here; one Cayley chart can reach no more than the corresponding theoretical orthogonal image. [R029], [R030]; Chs. 14, 18
THM-08 For fixed d, the closure of the union of generalized block-norm images over all positive block sizes is 𝓑_d. This is an asymptotic density statement: it does not give full coverage at any named finite s, consecutive nesting in s, a useful error rate, or trainability. [R029]; Ch. 14
THM-09 A fixed irreducible, aperiodic stochastic matrix has a unique stationary distribution and its iterates converge toward it; for a doubly stochastic matrix the stationary distribution is uniform. Fixed-matrix Perron–Frobenius setting. Dynamic products require separate analysis, and permutation routers are periodic counterexamples to automatic mixing. [R033]; Ch. 17
THM-10 A permutation cycle of length q has exactly the qth roots of unity as eigenvalues. The result describes a specified cycle. It does not say a trained router will choose cycles or that cyclic spectra improve a task. [R035], [R036]; Ch. 17

Derivation

ID Central claim Assumptions and boundary Source / chapter
DER-01 Read and compute have shape m×n; write and persist have shape d×m×n, so the final addition is shape-valid. Unbatched canonical convention. With batching or dynamic routing, declared routing-instance axes precede the operator axes; all three routers still act only on the stream axis. [R001]; Ch. 4
DER-02 Unrolling x_(l+1)=R_lx_l+p_ly_l gives the initial state transported by every later R, plus each write transported only by routers after its insertion. Exact algebra for the displayed recurrence. “Paths” are weighted contributions, not necessarily discrete selected wires. [R001], [R002]; Chs. 5, 7
DER-03 Double stochasticity preserves the all-ones mode and stream sum and makes the residual-only transport non-expansive in the stream-vector Euclidean norm. Applies pointwise in token/feature coordinates to H^res and products of valid routers. It does not prove that the full activation or gradient is bounded. [R002]; Ch. 8
DER-04 A finite Sinkhorn computation generally repairs the last-normalized sums exactly in exact arithmetic while perturbing the other sums, leaving a measurable constraint residual. Strictly positive input and the chapter’s alternating convention. Floating-point error adds another source of residual; tolerance is a certificate, not equality. [R002], [R021]; Ch. 9
DER-05 An N×N skew-symmetric matrix has p=N(N−1)/2 independent entries; for d=4,s=2, N=8 and p=28. Counts coordinate outputs, not total projection parameters. An affine projection from width r also has roughly rp weights plus bias/gain under the selected design. [R005]; Chs. 12, 19
DER-06 Orthogonality gives Σ_j||Q_ij||_F²=s and Σ_i||Q_ij||_F²=s; division by s yields row and column sums one. Requires correctly partitioned s×s blocks and exact orthogonality. A wrong reshape, missing /s, or nonorthogonal Q invalidates the conclusion. [R005], [R029]; Ch. 13
DER-07 At s=1, H_ij=Q_ij², so the image is the orthostochastic subset; for d≥3 that subset does not fill 𝓑_d. Entrywise squares erase auxiliary signs but do not make arbitrary doubly stochastic matrices orthostochastic. The d=2 case is exceptional. [R029], [R030]; Ch. 14
DER-08 One dense go-mHC Cayley solve costs O(N³)=O(d³s³), while coordinate width is quadratic in N; persistent activations scale as dBmn. A complete cost must multiply by routing instances (shared, per example, or per token) and include projection width, branch cost, batching, memory traffic, precision, and kernels. O(d³) assumes fixed s. [R005]; Chs. 15, 19–20
DER-09 At Q=I_(ds), the differential of the block-norm map is zero along every orthogonal-group tangent direction; off-diagonal router entries begin quadratically in a small skew perturbation. Concerns losses reaching coordinates only through H. Other coupled paths may contribute gradients. Exact-zero coordinate initialization can therefore stall this map even though near-identity initialization is useful. [R005]; Chs. 18–19
DER-10 All-2×2 KromHC factors have real factor spectra, so their Kronecker product cannot represent a router with a nonreal eigenvalue such as a three-cycle. Scoped to the all-2×2 factorization. A factor of size at least three can itself have complex eigenvalues; the general restriction is factor separability, not “all KromHC spectra are real.” [R004], [R005], [R018]; Chs. 11, 17

Empirical result

Every row in this section is an Empirical result attributed to its source. None is claimed to be independently reproduced by these notes.

ID Paper-reported result Exact paper/platform scope and boundary Source / chapter
EMP-01 Hyper-Connections reported improvements over residual baselines in language-model pretraining and vision experiments. The HC paper includes dense and sparse language-model settings and vision tasks, and studies static HC and input-dependent DHC. The result is not a theorem that multiple streams help every architecture, scale, or dataset. [R001]; Chs. 3, 16, 20
EMP-02 Original mHC reported stable loss/gradient profiles relative to its unconstrained HC comparison and a final training-loss reduction of 0.021 versus its baseline in the 27B run. Paper used d=4 and at most 20 Sinkhorn iterations in reported 3B, 9B, and 27B experiments. In the 27B comparison, HC’s loss surge occurred around step 12,000. This is one training system and comparison, not universal HC failure or mHC stability. [R002]; Ch. 9
EMP-03 On eight reported downstream benchmarks for the 27B models, mHC exceeded the baseline on all eight and HC on seven; MATH was the exception (26.0 mHC, 26.4 HC). Results inherit the paper’s model, data, training budget, prompting/evaluation, and checkpoint choices. They are not controlled evidence against every HC configuration. [R002]; Ch. 9
EMP-04 mHC Figure 7 reported maximum composite backward gain about 1.6, versus nearly 3000 for the compared HC analysis. Same paper-specific d=4 analysis. Twenty Sinkhorn iterations still showed slight single-layer deviation; the plot supports reduced measured gain drift, not exact finite-Sinkhorn conservation or a proof of end-to-end stability. [R002]; Ch. 9
EMP-05 The optimized mHC system reported 6.7% additional training time. In-house large-scale d=4 setting using kernel fusion, custom recomputing backward, activation recomputation, and pipeline scheduling. It is whole-system overhead, not an isolated Sinkhorn cost and not a prediction for an unfused framework loop. [R002]; Ch. 9
EMP-06 mHC-lite reported final losses broadly comparable to mHC and smaller gradient-norm fluctuations than its mHC reimplementation. d=4; 6-, 12-, and 24-layer language models on OpenWebText and FineWeb-Edu. It does not test factorial-scale d or establish universal throughput, stability, or quality superiority. [R003]; Ch. 10
EMP-07 KromHC reported fewer added parameters than mHC and mHC-lite while obtaining similar training loss/validation BPB and higher CORE score in its compared configurations. d=4 language models at six and twelve Transformer blocks: 240K vs 462K/609K added parameters, and 959K vs 1,844K/2,433K. These are two modest model scales and paper-selected comparisons. [R004]; Ch. 11
EMP-08 KromHC reported improving validation BPB as d increased through 4,8,16, plus lower late-training gradient norms in its comparison. Same paper’s language-model setup, factor choices, budgets, and optimizer. This does not establish a general stream-count scaling law or superiority of separable spectra. [R004]; Ch. 11
EMP-09 go-mHC spectral-target experiments reached denser observed regions than the tested KromHC baseline, with s=2 denser than s=1. Toy targets at d=3 for s=1,2, and a d=4 comparison against the all-2×2-factor KromHC baseline. Sampled/optimized reach is not proof of full finite-s coverage or task utility. [R005]; Chs. 14, 17
EMP-10 go-mHC reported convergence up to 10× faster on selected synthetic stream-mixing tasks. Synthetic objectives and paper-selected configurations. “Up to” is not an average, and the result does not isolate a universal optimization mechanism or predict large-model training speed. [R005]; Chs. 18, 20
EMP-11 go-mHC reported similar convergent behavior/parity among constrained variants in a roughly 30M-parameter, six-layer GPT-style TinyStories study. Small-language-model validation in the v1 preprint; the authors explicitly limit broader conclusions because of scale and synthetic emphasis. It does not establish superiority, large-scale stability, or a scaling law in d. [R005]; Chs. 17–18, 20

Interpretation

ID Central interpretation Boundary Source / chapter
INT-01 H^pre, H^post, and H^res are usefully read as soft read, soft write, and persistent-state routing. This is a dataflow lens, not a claim that gates are probabilities, discrete switches, MoE gates, or uniquely causal explanations. [R001], [R002]; Chs. 4, 16
INT-02 HC widens persistent state while the displayed single-branch update can still evaluate F once. State capacity and branch compute are distinct. A different multi-branch architecture may evaluate multiple F_r, but that cost is not implied by stream count alone. [R001]; Chs. 3–4
INT-03 Double stochasticity is conservative redistribution over streams and a controlled residual-only highway. It preserves mass/common mode and rules out gain above one for the mixer’s spectral norm, but may average away distinctions and does not stabilize every component of training. [R002]; Chs. 8, 17
INT-04 HC-family variants share a dataflow skeleton but trade off constraint approximation, coordinate burden, separability, finite-s image, kernel needs, and cost. No unqualified “better” ordering follows. A ranking requires named d,s, routing granularity, hardware, model, budget, metric, and implementation. [R001]–[R005]; Ch. 15
INT-05 Spectral reach describes possible persistent-state dynamics such as mixing, cycles, and rotations. Richer sampled spectra show parameterization reach, not semantic specialization, optimizer access, or higher downstream accuracy. Dynamic nonnormal products also exceed single-router eigenvalue analysis. [R004], [R005], [R032], [R035], [R036]; Chs. 7, 17
INT-06 s=2 is a useful tested go-mHC compromise because real 2×2 blocks can encode complex phases while keeping N=2d. It is not a theorem of optimality, surjectivity, best conditioning, or best hardware efficiency. [R005], [R029]; Ch. 14
INT-07 Stream count d is a genuine architectural degree of freedom alongside, but distinct from, depth, width, context length, and expert count. Calling d a design axis is not a scaling law. Its activation memory and router cost must be charged explicitly. [R001], [R005]; Ch. 20

Hypothesis

ID Testable hypothesis Evidence required / present status Source / chapter
HYP-01 Persistent streams may acquire reproducible semantic or functional specialization. Requires causal interventions, swaps/ablations, probes across inputs, layers, checkpoints, seeds, and permutation-aligned controls. Unequal router weights or a heatmap are insufficient. Not established by the five architecture papers. Chs. 5, 16, 20
HYP-02 Combining constrained persistent-state routing with heterogeneous attention, SSM, WavePDE/convolution, MLP, MoE, or memory branches may create useful learned dataflow graphs. Must compare matched compute/parameters and distinguish state routing from expert/branch routing. The multi-branch equation in the notes is an extrapolation, not the evaluated go-mHC block. Chs. 16, 20
HYP-03 Wider spectral reach and access to complex, nonseparable stream dynamics improve large, deep, or heterogeneous models. Needs matched training where only reachable routing dynamics differ, plus router/spectrum measurements and downstream outcomes. Current support is synthetic and sampled parameterization evidence from [R005]. [R005]; Chs. 17, 20
HYP-04 Increasing d can improve a capability or efficiency frontier and become a model scaling dimension. Requires sweeps over d, multiple seeds, optimizer retuning, and comparisons at matched parameters, FLOPs, wall time, and activation memory against spending the budget on depth, width, context, or experts. No scaling law is established. [R004], [R005]; Ch. 20
HYP-05 Cayley coordinates may optimize more favorably than permutation-simplex or finite-Sinkhorn coordinates. Fibers, chart compression, the zero differential at identity, softmax saturation, target router, initialization, d, s, and optimizer can favor either map. Synthetic go-mHC convergence is evidence only for tested settings, not the mechanism. [R003], [R005], [R026], [R029]; Ch. 18
HYP-06 Large nonnormal or changing-router transient gains make training more sensitive to precision, initialization, and input-dependent routing. Matrix products make the mechanism plausible, but validation requires direct transient/gain measurement and controlled interventions in named HC implementations. It is not a theorem that unconstrained HC always fails. [R002], [R032], [R033]; Ch. 7
